# Think Big To Win. Dare to Transform Your Life

## Think Big To Win

**Dare to Transform Your Life**

Shubha Vilas

*To my guide, who has been a beacon of light for direction,*

*a treasure house of wisdom and an unflagging source*

*of encouragement.*

*To the one who shaped me with his kindness and guidance.*

*I dedicate the six-book Ramayana series to this humble person*

*who inspired, encouraged and pushed me to begin writing.*

*HG Govinda Prabhu, though I know you prefer to be behind the*

*curtains, I wish to end the series by dedicating it to you for your love*

*and faith in me that brought me so far.*

## Acknowledgments

Walking through life, I have always seen myself as a student, surrounded by teachers who have touched and enhanced my life immensely. I would like to express my heartfelt gratitude for all that I have been fortunate enough to learn from them.

To name all of them here would be impossible; however, some of the most prominent teachers in my life have been: H.D.G. A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Srila Prabhupada, the founder Acarya of the Hare Krsna Movement, and H.H. Radhanath Swami, the author of the international bestseller The Journey Home – Autobiography of an American Swami.

I am indebted to Valmiki muni, the author of the Ramayana. And Kamba muni, who further enriched the flavor of the original with his amazing poetry. And to Tulsidasji, whose analogies bring out deeper aspects of this epic. I have learned a lot about the intricacies of the Ramayana from Sri Velukkudi Krishnan Swami and K.S. Narayanacharya, Shri Ramkumar Kinkarji, C. Sita Ramamurti and Sri S. Appalacharyulu. I would also like to appreciate all those who have worked on the website – www.valmikiramayan.net – to make the epic accessible to the world.

My special gratitude to Dr. Shrilekha Hada, for her tireless zeal and invaluable inputs in developing every aspect of the book. She has become an integral part of this series.

I personally owe a million thanks to Meera Menon, whose meticulousness shows through in every sentence of this book, whose thought-provoking enquiries have helped me go deeper into the Ramayana.

A monumental venture like this wouldn’t be possible without constant encouragement from my friends. Particularly, Shadbhuj, Radeshlal, Raas Gopal, Raja Gopal and Nitin Chavan who have supported me in more ways than can be penned. I offer my gratitude hugs to them celebrating the joy of true friendship.

Probably the greatest inspiration has come from the thousands of readers who have enthusiastically written and spoken to me about their appreciation for these books and the life-transformation it has brought in their lives. I consider their wishes to be my primary motivation to keep writing.

And of course, my warm thanks to the entire team of Jaico led by Akash Shah – their intense efforts and sincere dedication are visible on every page of the book. This book has been a memorable journey with so much contemplation going into making the series more accessible to the world. This journey has been about not only working on a book, but also bonding deeply with the Jaico family. I am truly inspired and encouraged to continue this venture in making Ramayana everyone’s life companion with their support.

## Author’s Note

Ramayana—The Game of Life series presents an authentic rendition of the Ramayana in an absolutely modern and riveting manner. This book promises to enlighten loyalists and entertain modernists. Interspersing the story of the Ramayana with thought-provoking contemporary life lessons, the series guarantees satisfaction with a promise to be a lifelong companion. As you flip through the pages, one thought is sure to cross your mind: never has the Ramayana been so applicable to life!

The book series, while holding Valmiki muni’s Ramayana as a lighthouse, sensitively weaves in analogies and poetic ornamentation from the Kamba Ramayana and Tulsi Das’ Ramcharitmanas. In addition, the series also brings in folk tales called Loka Pramana that are in philosophical alignment with Valmiki’s Ramayana. These folk tales bring in background stories that enhance the understanding of the main story as well as its various characters and their moods. Without the knowledge of these tales, the original story would be much less dramatic and comprehensible. Moreover, the series also provides deep insights from great acharyas who have studied the Ramayana thoroughly. Even if it is filled with so many elements, I have made sure that modern readers do not find it inapplicable or irrelevant in their personal life. This series shows readers how these epics have immediate relevance in our life, if presented properly and understood deeply.

Of course, I would like to state here that I have taken some creative liberties to keep the narration gripping. But rest assured, these creative liberties are minimal and do not, in any way, impede or supersede the original author’s work or the divinity of the epic and its characters. If anything, the creative liberties enhance the joy of reading this great work of literature.

Over the last few years, I have presented a series of seminars based on Ramayana—The Game of Life, where I expand on the innumerable life lessons presented in the books with a lot of practical examples and anecdotes. These seminars have made the book even more user-friendly and relevant to the thousands who have heard them. These seminars are freely available on YouTube.

Think Big to Win essentially expounds the Yuddha Kanda of the Ramayana. This is the last section of the magnum opus of Valmiki muni. The Uttara Kanda of the Ramayana, which is the seventh section was written much later by Valmiki and is also written in a very different style. While the first six sections, starting from Bala Kanda up until Yuddha Kanda, are written like a continuous story, the Uttara Kanda is written in the form of disconnected episodes. Thus, in this series, I have chosen to stick to the first six Kandas of the Ramayana. However, I would like to bring to the notice of the readers that I have included some sections of the Uttara Kanda in the relevant places in these six books itself to enhance the details and the narrative. For example, there is a detailed description of the background stories of Ravana and Hanuman in the Uttara Kanda. I have included those in Book 2: Conquer Change and Book 4: Stand Strong of the series, respectively. As far as the rest of the section of Uttara Kanda is concerned, I plan to write it as a separate book sometime in future. But the format of that book will be very different from the series. As it has some controversial topics, it needs to be understood in a different manner and with greater discussions in order to grasp the intricacies. More like a sequel to the Ramayana— The Game of Life series. But more philosophical in approach.

Think Big to Win is an adrenaline rush throughout. Every page of this book keeps you on the edge of the seat. An action-packed adventure filled with nuggets of wisdom that help you take small breaths as you take in all the action. You will experience the anger of Ravana, the calmness of Rama, the boldness of the Vanara army, the heroism of Hanuman, the intelligence of Jambavan, the sinister moves of Indrajit, the pain of Sita, the madness of Kumbhakarna and the righteousness of Vibhishan. Mysticism leaks throughout the book with graphic descriptions that arrest your attention all through the read. Be it the bridge building episode or the fight between Rama and Ravana or the entry of Kumbhakarna during the war or the secret power source of Indrajit or Hanuman bringing back Sanjivani herbs or the fire test of Sita or the Pushpak Vimana’s descent into Ayodhya, all of them churn out a myriad emotions from within your heart. As we approach the end of the series, I am sure of one thing, you will hope and pray that the series never ends. You may even want to slow down the pace of reading, just to ensure that the book doesn’t end. Though the book will sadly end at some point, the subtitle, Dare to Transform Your Life, will remind you of one thing, the responsibility to apply in your life what you have learnt throughout the series. The Ramayana has the power to transform your life by showing you a standard that is lofty yet aspirational. For those who have read the entire series and have reached this point, it is not only your responsibility to apply the teachings in your life but also share them with others. In a world filled with selfishness, the story of the Ramayana is a beacon of hope and love. I pray that every reader who has touched any book in this series experiences great love towards Lord Rama and remains a lover of the Ramayana forever.

## Chapter 1

Realization and Expulsion

Defeat stinks, and stings. But nothing is as excruciating as being beaten for the first time after a million victories. The pain only worsens with unravelling failure. If the opponent is powerful, there is dignity in loss. But when defeat comes at the hands of a mere monkey, can one really reconcile to it? When it is impossible to justify it to oneself, how can one rationalize it to anyone else, especially those in awe?1

Lanka stank of defeat. A putrid smell hung in the air for days after the monkey set had ablaze the city and vanished. So pervasive was the stench that Ravana began to wonder if he was imagining the odors. Such was his paranoia that he smelled smoke even in his wives’ hair. He had ordered the city sparkling clean, but the miasma continued to asphyxiate him and assault his senses.2

The fire had not only obliterated the city’s infrastructure, it had also annihilated the Lankan morale. After a thorough assessment of the Lankan people, Mandodari, Ravana’s wife and the queen of the land, concluded that the incident had both scared and scarred the citizens so much that they even had nightmares of raging monkeys with burning tails. When she shared this with Ravana, he snapped, “Whose side are you on? As my wife, it is your dharma to support me—your husband.” When Ravana said “supporting,” what he really meant was “Do not contradict me.” But the fact is he had a problem with honesty and anyone who uttered the truth, especially when it highlighted his failures or mistakes. He preferred sycophants instead and loved sweet words of praise. But when it was his turn to appreciate, he had nothing good to say about anyone. Instead, he spewed the bitterest of words in condemnation. In fact, Ravana was afraid of the truth, and he refused to admit it.3 Concealing facts rather than confronting them was more his style of leadership.

The first thing he did was to obscure the obvious—the destruction of Lanka. He ordered the celestial architect, Maya, to restore Lanka to its original glory. Maya used his mystical powers to reconstruct the razed city in a matter of days. In fact, he redesigned Ravana’s city to appear even more splendid and opulent than before. As part of his cover-up scheme, Ravana organized daily song-and-dance festivals in his new palace where key members of the Lankan community overindulged in joyful merrymaking and drowned themselves in free-flowing liquor to bury their fears. Ravana laughed a little more than usual. The intelligent could see his insecurities right through those extra guffaws.4

As Lanka was being refashioned using Maya’s illusionary skills, Ravana took a morale-uplifting stroll around the city. One day, during a walkabout, some very disturbing thoughts struck him as he passed by Vibhishan’s house. How and why did the monkey spare only his house of all houses in Lanka during his blazing spree? Did they know each other from before? Was that why Vibhishan stopped me from executing the malicious monkey by asserting that a messenger could not be killed? Ravana shook his heads to shake off his disturbing thoughts and decided to pay his brother a surprise visit. His brother, Vibhishan, was definitely different from the rest of his siblings, but his love was evident. Having decided to give the benefit of doubt to his youngest sibling, Ravana stepped into Vibhishan’s abode. Fragrant smoke from the incense filled the air. The sweet scent inside the house was starkly different from the putrid smell of lust and liquor (now also charred corpses) in most Lankan homes. Ravana couldn’t relate to the fragrance. It almost smelled like a temple.5

The king’s unannounced entry into Vibhishan’s house shocked the soldiers and servants on duty. The clueless personnel merely stepped aside nervously. Ravana walked in freely, bobbing his head to acknowledge those greeting him. Suddenly, he heard what sounded like the repetitive chants of some mantra. A curious Ravana charged toward the divine sound vibrations. When he drew aside a heavy curtain to look inside the room, he was outraged. Seated in a lotus posture with palms folded in prayer and eyes closed in meditation was Vibhishan, his own brother, chanting the names of his enemy, Rama. Vibhishan’s Rama chant reverberated across the room. Ravana’s anger aggravated and he was now fuming. Intolerable! How could my own brother chant the names of my sworn enemy and that too in my own kingdom?

“How dare you?” Ravana hollered, shattering a delicate flower vase kept at the entrance. The tumultuous roar shook Vibhishan out of his trance, and he jumped up immediately. He bowed to his brother and ushered him in with reverence, behaving as if nothing had happened. By this time, however, Ravana was trembling and red with rage. “Explain your unacceptable act within my kingdom.” Vibhishan stayed silent. Ravana repeated his question louder. Denying all allegations, Vibhishan said, “I never chanted the name of your enemy.” Ravana was even more furious. “What a blatant lie!” Vibhishan finally offered his clarification. “I did not chant the name of your enemy. I was instead chanting your name.” Ravana was dumbstruck but this outrageous claim. He opened his mouth, but words betrayed him. “Continue,” he said. He needed to hear more to decode his brother’s assertions. “I was chanting both your names—Ravana-Mandodari. But while repeatedly chanting your divine names, I thought it was a little too long, so I shortened it to the first syllables of both the names and it transformed into Ra-Ma. Ra from Ravana, and Ma from Mandodari. So you see, I wasn’t really chanting the names of your enemy.”

Piety-Sin Pact

The acharyas explain that Vibhishan’s austerities and penances were actually strengthening Ravana. For as long as Vibhishan stayed in Lanka and practiced devotion, his bhakti kept feeding Ravana’s strength, and his dharma continued fueling his brother’s adharma until he severed his ties with his brother and Lanka. Vibhishan’s life in Lanka was one of compromise; he compromised with moha (illusion) and ahankar (ego). An unspoken pact between Vibhishan and Ravana mandated that Ravana not oppose Vibhishan’s worship, and Vibhishan would ignore Ravana’s sins. This mutual understanding between punya (piety) and paap (sin) kept God away from Lanka.

Ravana was beaming after listening to Vibhishan’s justification. I’m glad I gave him time to explain himself. Deeply touched by Vibhishan’s loyalty, Ravana walked out of the palace declaring this as a wonderful way of remembering the king and the queen. Word spread across Lanka and soon many demons were chanting “Rama.” Obviously, barely anyone knew the real meaning of the chant.

Freedom Chant

Hanuman shared his wisdom that helped Vibhishan recognize Ravana’s nature and eventually end his relationship with him. Until then, Vibhishan would appreciate Ravana. “My brother is so generous. He does not believe in god but has made a temple just so I can worship. How tolerant he is.” But was he really tolerant? Actually, he let Vibhishan worship because it was a boon granted by Lord Shiva. And if he interfered in that, his boon also would become void. So he thought it better to let Vibhishan continue with his bhakti so that his boon would also be fulfilled. Vibhishan could not understand this until Hanuman explained the math to him so that him so he could end his compromise with Ravana.

The little association Vibhishan had with Hanuman inspired him to begin the chanting of the name of Rama with great faith and devotion.

Although Ravana tried his best to create a pleasant atmosphere in Lanka, he failed. The burning of the city and the massacre at Ashok vatika had scarred the Rakshasa clan for life. Besides, Ravana had lost his inner peace having realized that his enemies were strategizing. How long could he deny reality and escape from it? One day, he called for an emergency meeting of Lanka’s eminent people to deal with the burning question.6

“Are we ready for an invasion?” Without even exchanging pleasantries, Ravana spat out the question smoldering in his mind and haunting him since the day the monkey left Lanka in cinders. “If a lone monkey could cause such havoc, imagine what would be if a troop of such rogue monkeys were to burst into Lanka. Are we prepared for such an intrusion? We could say that we were unprepared for the surprise attack and were caught unawares. But now that we know of a potential threat, that excuse no longer stands. This is of serious concern. The monkey has not only destroyed my favorite Ashok vatika but also killed my son and many of my most able men.” His head was bent low in disgrace, and he made no eye contact with ministers while discussing the humiliating incineration of Lanka. Lanka’s all-mighty king Ravana wasn’t ever used to speaking the language of defeat.

“All of you assembled here are the most important people in my life. The future of our nation depends on your deep thinking. The wise say that mature advice is at the root of all success and that victory hinges on strategy. Help me deliberate. What should be our course of action now? How do we best meet an attack by Rama’s army? How do we prepare ourselves going forward?” Ravana egged his key men for good counsel, reminding them of the three types of advisors and advices.7

Of Advisors and Advices

Ravana reminds his men of the type of advisors and advice before taking their suggestions with the intent of pushing them to putting on their best thinking hats so that he could extract the best suggestions from them.

Advisors can be of three categories. The first category—uttama purusha—are exemplary men who discuss and seek the counsel of great men concerned about their welfare, ministers efficient in deliberating about decisions, friends with common interests and close relatives before embarking on any venture. They use this advice after weighing the pros and cons of the circumstances and take action relying on the grace of the Supreme Lord.

The second category—madhyama purusha—are mediocre men who are lone rangers with independent thinking and who do not seek advice from anyone. They arrive at their own conclusions. With great faith in their own abilities and in the grace of the Almighty, they launch themselves into action.

The third category—adhama purusha—are the vilest of men who do not heed any advice, do what they like and do not rely on anybody, including the role of god. They take arbitrary decisions without considering the pros and cons and end up procrastinating on the implementation of their decisions and fail. They are the most incompetent of the lot.

Similarly, advice can be categorized as uttama mantaraanga (best or ultimate advice), madhyama mantaraanga (standard or mediocre advice) and adhama mantaraanga (the worst advice).

Uttama mantaraanga is considered the perfect kind of advice. This collective opinion comes from a set of like-minded thinkers who are all on the same page and have no difference of opinion. They arrive at the same advice after elaborate objective examination of the issue involved and careful analysis based on what the scriptures say. Such advice satisfies everyone and is considered the best and most effective.

Madhyama mantaraanga is the advice given after much deliberation and is a patched-up version of several conflicting opinions. It is considered second-class advice because advisors need to set aside their clashing views to focus on their responsibilities to come to an agreement.

Adhama mantaraanga is basically no advice at all. Advisors with divergent views fail to arrive at a consensus even after ample discussion, and this is considered third-class advice. Advisors do not budge from their views from the beginning to the end, and the advice presented is incoherent and inconclusive, which is not at all beneficial.8

After his discourse on advice and advisors, Ravana placed his agenda. “All of you are wise men with great intellect. I have called you for your advice—advice that can save Lanka from destruction. I am sure you are aware that Rama and Lakshmana are on the other side of the ocean—their angry eyes glaring at Lanka as I speak. They are surrounded by millions of vanaras led by Sugriva. The monkeys are as powerful as the one that showed up here and displayed a fraction of what they all are really capable of. Rama alone is so powerful that he could dry up the ocean. He may use any means to reach Lanka. War is now unavoidable. I want all of you to ponder intensely and tell me of a strategy that best suits the welfare of Lanka and its citizens.”

As soon as Ravana stopped speaking, silence descended upon the courtroom and anxious eyes exchanged glances. They had never seen their king in such a pensive mood. Assuming it was their duty to uplift their king and bring back his usual confident mood, the rakshasa advisors all threw in their two bits of guidance into the cacophonous mix of suggestions, with absolutely no regard for anything he spoke and with no discussion whatsoever. When a doctor, minister and teacher indulge in flattery only for reward or out of fear, the collective health, polity and wisdom of such a kingdom quickly degenerate. Reckless advice flowing from the rakshasas came without taking into account the ethics, morality or even the strengths of the enemies, simply because of their desire to please their master with words they knew he loved to hear.9

One sturdy rakshasa stood up and said in his hoarse voice, “O master, do not underestimate our strength. We are not inferior to any army in the world. Remember when we marched with you to the abode of death and conquered Yamaraj the god of death? I still remember your unforgettable conquest of the kingdom of Kubera. Our soldiers still discuss the heroism with which you felled kingdom after kingdom with our forces firmly behind you through it all. Your amazing victory against the Nagas in the netherworlds, your conquest of the mystical Kalakeyas and your learning the magical arts from their worlds are all legendary. O King, we don’t even need your presence in this battle against the puny monkeys. Your valorous son Indrajit alone can defeat Rama and his entire vanara army. No task is difficult for the honorable Indrajit who conquered the heavenly realms and even enslaved Indra. A father to such a brave son need not fear at all. Don’t stress over the mischief of one insignificant monkey. Be rest assured your able men will kill Rama.”

Before Ravana could even react to this, Prahasta, the commander in chief of the Lankan armed forces stood up. “O Lord of Lanka! O Lankeshwar Ravana! When the devas, gandharvas and danavas don’t dare face you in a battle, who are these inconsequential vanaras and petty humans? I take ownership of what happened to Lanka recently. That impudent monkey Hanuman took us for a ride just because we were unsuspecting of such a possibility. We would never have expected a lone creature to penetrate the unconquerable fortress of Lanka and cause such destruction. If I were aware and alert enough, that impudent thing would not have returned alive. We should have followed that retreating monkey, finished him off and removed the obstacle from our path. Had we done that, we wouldn’t have had to see this day. It was my oversight and blunder, and I take complete responsibility for it. Be confident that I am alert and ever watchful. I promise to guard and protect the city with my life. Hereon you can relax as far as the protection of Lanka and its citizens are concerned. If your highness is anxious about the enemy, on your command I will lead our armies to erase the vanaras from the face of this earth. I assure you no trouble will befall you for the abduction of Sita.”

A tumultuous roar followed Prahasta’s enthusiastic declaration. That he had good control over the army was evident from the army chiefs’s enthusiastic response. A demon, Durmukha, did not quite agree with Prahasta’s defensive stance. He was trembling with rage as he stood up spewed fiery words. “This is completely unacceptable. The entire rakshasa clan has been shamed by one vanara. We must avenge the destruction of Lanka and reclaim our honor. I cannot live another day recollecting the insult he had heaped upon us. Just give us the order, and we will slaughter the stupid monkeys and their descendants wherever in the universe they may be. We will render the vanara species extinct.”10

Swinging a massive iron club smeared with blood and gore, Vajradamstra grated, “This club is enough to smash the faces of Rama, Lakshmana, Sugriva and Hanuman. If I may share an idea here in this august assembly that can effectively wipe out the vanara army completely, including their chiefs and the two despicable humans with zero loss or damage to us.” He did not even wait for an answer before continuing. “O king of the demons, I suggest that thousands of our shape-shifting demons assume human forms. Thousands of our men should approach Rama disguised like this claiming to be sent by his younger brother Bharata to help him in his war against Lanka. Once the meek Rama in good faith accepts them, our men should mingle with the forces of vanaras and wait for the right moment. At the right moment, when the enemies have been entrapped in our scheme, our men, armed with the deadliest of weapons, should massacre the entire monkey army when they are least expecting and are least prepared for sabotage.”

When Nikumbha, Vajrahanu, Rabhasa, Suryasatru, Suptaghna, Yajnakopa, Mahaparsva, Mahodara, Agniketu, Rasmiketu and Atikaya heard this, they stood up and unanimously declared that they were willing to cross any expanse of water if it meant being able to eliminate the enemies. They raised their weapons in the air and released a deafening battle-shriek that gave goose bumps to those assembled. The atmosphere was surcharged with passion and great hope now. For a moment, everyone including Ravana felt no need for worry in the presence of Lanka’s powerful and confident warriors. Suddenly, the pensive mood set by Ravana’s agitated speech seemed to change for the better because of the fiery, assertive words of these warriors.11

Truth or Praise?

Good speech should please, be of benefit and be truthful. Of these three, prioritization is very important. Appeasement can be forsaken for truthfulness and for benefit, but it can never be the other way round. Appeasement should not come at the cost of truthfulness and benefit.

A speaker should try to incorporate all three in his speech. The listener should, however, not at all give importance to the sections of the speech that please. Instead the focus should be on that which is truthful and beneficial. Ravana worshipped words of sweet praise. He loved to hear others eulogize him. But when it came to him, he only uttered bitter words that hurt others. Because he was so attached to words that pleased, he was far-removed and detached from words that revealed the truth.

An owl assumes that it has sharp vision simply because it can see clearly at night when no one else can. But in that belief, it ignores the fact that its vision during the day is blurry. If it could see just as well during the day, it would understand others’ reality. The acharyas conclude that Ravana was like the owl, who could not see the light of Rama.

Just then, something happened that altered the fate of Lanka forever. Vibhishan stood up and said something that completely contradicted the current mood in the courtroom. He was calm and spoke with clarity of purpose, with no display of arrogance or any fear of the enemy—something Lanka wasn’t used to. Vibhishan mostly chose to remain quiet. He seldom spoke despite being the king’s brother. He preferred silence during the mindless and heedless discussions steered by extreme passion and wild emotions in the Lankan court. He felt that Lanka’s leaders were driven by emotion rather than logic. Even in the rare instances Ravana asked his ministers for opinions, it was just perfunctory and mostly meant to elicit appreciation and flattery.

Vibhishan’s silence policy made sense. But he did speak on occasion, and because he spoke so rarely, whenever he did, Ravana took him quite seriously. Usually, Vibhishan made sure not to say anything too contradictory to his brother’s wants. But he would often tweak a decision to slightly align it with the principles of dharma that he so strongly believed in. Vibhishan of course was aware that nothing in Lanka was anywhere close to dharma, but he believed that even a slight change in direction meant progress from the current mindset of Lanka. The last time he spoke was to make his brother revoke the consensus that Hanuman be killed on the premise that a messenger should never be killed and could only be maimed or hurt at most. Of course, even that wasn’t anywhere recommended by dharmic traditions, but at least it saved the life of Hanuman. What he was about to suggest now was different.12

Vibhishan had been waiting for the right opportunity to transform Ravana’s mind. His brother hadn’t confided in him before executing the abduction of Sita. Had he done so, Vibhishan would have goaded him to drop that plan. But after the burning of Lanka, Vibhishan felt that Ravana would be in the right mental frame to reconsider his demonic stance after having witnessed a spark of Rama’s splendor. Vibhishan folded his hands and bowed humbly and urged all the excited warriors to take their seats. He pulled out weapons from some hands and pushed some back to their seats gently. Palms folded in front of each charged-up warrior, Vibhishan quietened them all. Once silence returned to the courtroom, he began his logical argument. “In my opinion, dear brother, violence should always be the last resort. Let us first try to resolve the differences peacefully. Wise men suggest that only after trying out saama (mediation), daama (payoff) and bheda (guile) should anyone venture into danda (violence). When we haven’t yet tried the first three methods, why should we jump straight into? Even if you announce war, you must not do so without first critically examining the strengths and weaknesses of your enemy. War could be a good first option when the enemy is not alert or immersed in self-indulgence and hence inattentive or if the enemy is experiencing internal conflicts or is aged or suffering from prolonged illness or is let down by fate. But for Rama, none of the above weaknesses exists. He is always alert, cool and introspective. He is not quick to anger and knows how to channelize his energies in the right direction with great power. Thus He is powerful and very difficult to conquer. Every single one of His vanara soldiers is just as difficult to defeat! Did we ever imagine that someday someone would jump across the ocean, bypass the robust security system of Lanka, destroy the king’s favorite gardens, kill the king’s son, wipe out an entire division of our army, burn our city and still get away? If you cannot rightly estimate the strength of your enemy, at least do not underestimate them.13

Moreover, I feel that a war has to be fought for a just cause. What is our just cause for declaring war on Rama? What wrong has He done to us? If you say we are avenging the killing of Khara and Dhushan, then how is self-defense wrong? Rama simply defended Himself against their attack, and they were killed in the process. What is Rama’s fault? If that was the reason, shouldn’t we have attacked Him ten months ago instead of stealing His wife in His absence? Abducting someone else’s wife is never a way to avenge a war fought fairly. If you say it was due to the brutality done to Surpanakha, then let me tell you that she tried to attack Sita with the intention of eliminating Her. Which husband wouldn’t try to save his wife from being tormented right in front of his eyes?14

Abducting a woman is the most heinous crime. Where there is wisdom, there is prosperity, but where there is a lack of wisdom, there is only destruction. The sin of Sita’s abduction has already engulfed Lanka. If we do not undo it very soon, all of us will be reduced to ashes. Innumerable ill omens are surfacing in Lanka, indicating an upcoming catastrophe. For as long as Sita is in Lanka, grave danger awaits us. As soon as Sita is returned to Rama, all reasons for enmity will vanish. It is not at all wise to harbor enmity with Rama. O dear brother, I may be younger, but I beg you to follow my advice and return Sita. Surrendering Sita is the best course of action right now. Set aside the anger that only destroys righteousness and happiness. Follow the path of dharma that enhances fame and joy. If you want to live and rule for a long duration, the best recourse for you is to follow my advice and not listen to these wicked rakshasas who are not your well-wishers.”

Everyone was left dumbfounded. What just happened? Absolutely no one could wrap their heads around Vibhishan’s speech. How did he even say what he just said? When he finished, all eyes automatically turned toward Ravana, all expecting a volcano to erupt. But Ravana’s eyes were downcast. He was lost in thought. Suddenly, he got up and walked out of the courtroom without saying a single word. The rakshasas were confused. Chatter erupted as everyone began discussing the shocking turn of events. They glared at Vibhishan. Not knowing the next course of action or what was expected of them, they began to walk out of the courtroom in small groups.15

The monkey’s havoc-wreaking run had disturbed him deeply as it had every citizen of Lanka. Besides, it had been a long night for Ravana—a night full of questions he had no answers to. The most nagging ones were: Why did I walk out without giving a fitting reply to Vibhishan? Is he right after all? What ill omens? Could it all be true? Or is possible I read it all wrong? Is Vibhishan really my well-wisher or is he an accomplice of the enemy attempting to demoralize me and my men? The more he thought about the doubts, the more it became complicated. Vibhishan was smart, and his arguments were logical. He needed someone to help him think more laterally. Someone he could trust. Someone who would not say something while meaning something else. Someone whose intentions and actions he never doubted. Someone… he suddenly realized whom he could trust in such trying times. He felt ready to face the world the next morning.16

good brother, bad brother

The next morning, Vibhishan entered the inner chambers of Ravana’s palace. He realized he had overstepped the line the previous day even though he was a part of the royal family. He hadn’t had the chance to complete the energy exchange with Ravana the previous day as he had abruptly walked away from the scene. As soon as he entered the room, Ravana looked away. It was clear he had made up his mind about something and did not want to enter into any conversation at this point. But for his own satisfaction, Vibhishan wanted to say what he had not managed to say the previous day. It was something that could not be ignored and was definitely a piece of information no sane man could ignore. The brahmanas that had lined up in Ravana’s palace chanting hymns and incantations had given some hope to Vibhishan. His brother had two sides—an extremely pious side that believed strongly in rituals and austerities and an extremely arrogant and self-obsessed one that was atheistic and non-believing. While he was walking in, he was hopeful that early morning, he would meet the pious side of Ravana. But Ravana’s disinterest reflected the opposite. But since he was anyway there, he decided to speak his mind.

He began with earnestness, not waiting for any indication from his brother. “O most glorious king of the rakshasa horde, O most powerful brother, I am here to share something you ought to know—something all the citizens have been noticing for the last few days and something you shouldn’t ignore. If you remember, I had mentioned to you yesterday in the court of many inauspicious signs in Lanka heralding the approach of disaster of a magnitude no one in Lanka can comprehend. The wise men claim that they are indicators of total annihilation. I want you to know about them so you could use your prudence to take the right decision. For quite some time now, all the sacrificial fires have stopped burning and are simply emitting sparks and are filled with smoke polluted with soot—this despite the mantras being uttered perfectly and the best quality of ingredients being used in the sacrifices. Serpents have been slithering in the corners of the places where pure substances are stored and near the sacrificial arena. Ants have been invading pure ingredients reserved for the sacrifice and are refusing to go away and returning in large numbers despite all attempts to control the menace. Cows’ milk is curdling across Lanka and does not stay fresh even within seconds of the cows being milked. Our elephants and horses are restless, sometimes trumpeting and neighing all night. They seem to be starving themselves. The camels, donkeys and mules have been shedding tears and coat hair. Treatment doesn’t seem to be working at all. Crows have been flocking in hordes and perching atop houses, creating a racket with their harsh caws. Vultures have been flying in circles all over the city and are inching closer to the ground every day. At dawn and dusk, jackals have been gathering together and howling inauspiciously. Carnivorous animals have been seen lurking near the city gates threatening to break in any day. All these signs are unheard of and unnatural. These point to some great calamity about to descend on Lanka. It would be intelligent to consider these omens as warning and act perspicaciously. Dear brother, please know that your ministers hide bad news from you and prefer only to give you good news and heap praises upon you. I prefer telling you the truth. The best advice that you can get at this point is to return Sita to Rama.”17

“No! No! No!” Ravana got up with a start, violently throwing away his seat. “No one can defeat me. Not even the celestials. No one can take Sita away from my captivity. I am afraid of no one.” Pushing away Vibhishan, Ravana huffed out of the palace, hopped into his chariot and rode off, leaving Vibhishan flustered.

That massive chariot rattled into the assembly hall like rumbling clouds herald rain. When the rakshasas heard that unmistakable rumble of their king’s chariot from their homes, they rushed to the assembly hall. Some carried swords and shields, others bore lances and clubs. Some held axes, and others carried darts. All were unusually dressed—colorful attire with accompanying jewelry. While most arrived in chariots, horses and elephants, some walked in. They rushed in after their master who had just entered the assembly hall. Thousands of trumpets and conches blew simultaneously, heralding the entry of the king into the royal assembly hall of Lanka. These sounds served a dual purpose—a royal welcome to the king and the final warning to those who were late. A gigantic white umbrella was held over Ravana’s head as he walked toward the throne with two servants who held yak-tail fans with shiny crystal handles flanking him. As the king occupied the royal throne, all rakshasa ministers bent their heads, joined their palms in veneration and took their respective seats. The king looked around, but didn’t speak a thing. It was obvious he was waiting for some more people to come in—some important ones. Next to enter the hall was Vibhishan. He walked in hastily and sat down after greeting the king.

Ravana did not even look at Vibhishan, but instead called for Prahasta, the commander in chief of the armed forces. “Prahasta, order the forces! Cover the city from the inside and the outside. Use your abilities to protect Lanka from any sort of attack from any possible direction. Let’s ready our men for the onslaught that is about to begin.”

After he had barked his orders, Ravana looked hard and long at Vibhishan. This was his reply to all the bragging Vibhishan had done the previous evening and this morning. Prahasta immediately jumped up from his seat right into action, leading a few of the king’s confidential army chiefs to carry out the command. In a very short while, he returned with a smile, signaling to the king that his welltrained team would carry out the task and secure the city in and out. That done, Ravana focused on putting to action what he had been planning all night. He rose from his seat and announced. “I have a surprise for everyone.” His thundering clap was the cue for the wall behind his throne to open up. Jaws dropped in the courtroom. That massive door opening could only mean one thing. He had woken up! Kumbhakarna had woken up!

Ravana’s second brother, Kumbhakarna, slept for six months at a stretch and when he woke up, he more than made up for all the fun he had missed the past six months. He ate and drank with gusto; he dove deep into all sorts of pleasures and indulgences until it was time for slumber again. He was no ordinary rakshasa. He was a giant. The door behind the throne extended all the way up to the ceiling to allow him entry into the courtroom. It was created only for his entry. Waking him from his hibernation had been quite a task. It had taken a few dozen men few hours of high-octane activity all night to get him to even stir. Ravana had conceived the plan the previous night and had instructed the waking-up team to get into action. The first phase of wakening involved elephants, horses and donkeys calling out simultaneously. The second phase had a cacophonous chorus of trumpets, bugles and conches, followed by drums, gongs and bells. In the final phase, they had to poke him with tridents, arrows and swords. All this ruckus saw some movement. After a few more hours of provocation, Kumbhakarna woke with a huge yawn demanding food. On cue, hundreds of cartloads of all varieties of food were brought before him and he downed them in a gulp. After a few hours of nonstop eating and drinking, Kumbhakarna finally stood up. He now had the mental frame to hear the reason for being woken days in advance. His regular quota of sleep was still incomplete. When told about his brother’s worry, he readied himself right away to be punctual for the morning’s court meeting.18

Ravana welcomed his brother back from his sleep into action by offering him a royal seat, especially designed to accommodate his size and weight. Kumbhakarna occupied his seat and looked around with great admiration at his friends and colleagues. Often in his absence, so many things would change—so many would disappear having died in some battle; so many new faces would appear. Each time Kumbhakarna woke up, there would be some surprise awaiting him.

After the initial pleasantries, Ravana told Kumbhakarna everything that had happened over the last several months. He began by narrating about his maddening lust for Sita and ended by saying, “Now as we speak, her husband Rama and his vanara army are waiting on the opposite shore of the ocean to launch an attack Lanka.” Ravana lied to him about Sita’s deadline and said that She had asked for a year to agree to his demands. In the same breath that he expressed his anxiety over a single vanara’s crossing the ocean and setting Lanka ablaze, he bragged about how rakshasas had no real danger from humans and monkeys, especially since he had defeated all the celestials put together. Glaring across at Vibhishan, Ravana confided in Kumbhakarna that he trusted him with his life and wanted his valuable opinion on the issue—specifically how to retain Sita in Lanka and eliminate Rama and Lakshmana.19

“What needs to be done first, you do last, and what should be done last, you do first!” Kumbhakarna spoke in his wisdom-loaded penetrating voice. “Shouldn’t you have consulted with us before abducting someone’s wife? You acted out of impulse while abducting her, then why are you overthinking this obvious battle with her husband? Consultation before an act with severe consequences is worthy, but consultation post action is foolish. When your mind is guided by principles of justice, there will be no occasion to repent. When a king seeks guidance, he never strays into the path of wrongdoing. Actions carried out without a clear strategy can only lead to a mess. You are fortunate Rama did not kill you while you were stealing His wife.

Though you were totally wrong, as a brother I will stand by you. I will destroy your enemies and undo your mistake. Whoever Rama and Lakshmana may be and however powerful they may be, I will vanquish them. I will kill Rama even before his second arrow reaches me. Once the brothers are destroyed, I will terminate the entire monkey army. O brother, you can now relax and enjoy the finest wines. Just assume that Rama is dead and Sita is yours.”

Wisdom Sides with Wrongdoing

Kumbhakarna was aware of dharma as much as Vibhishan was, with the difference being Vibhishan’s focus on practicing what he knew and Kumbhakarna’s intention of knowing but never applying. Kumbhakarna was too attached to his adharma-following brother Ravana to even bother practicing dharma. He told Ravana that it was a miracle Rama hadn’t killed him yet, which meant he knew the power of Rama. Being steeped in tama guna or the mode of ignorance, Kumbhakarna could not apply knowledge, and being steeped in sattva guna or the mode of goodness, Vibhishan lived by what he spoke and believed in.

Just as Kumbhakarna completed his speech, a very powerful rakshasa named Mahaparsva stood up and barked out his thoughts. “Only a fool risks going into a dark, dense and dangerous forest and not consuming the nectarine honey he has found by luck. O king, you are the master of the world. Who can prevent you from fulfilling your wishes? Step on the heads of your enemies and ravish Sita. Why don’t you just enjoy her forcefully? Why have you been patiently waiting for this long for your union with that divinely beautiful nymph? After enjoying her beauty, what do you have to fear? While you enjoy yourself, we will stand and protect you from any attack. Have faith in Kumbhakarna and Indrajit and the powers they possess. Led by them, your army is capable of withstanding any attack by all the Indra-led celestials. I do not believe in accomplishing things through saama, daama and bheda. That is not the rakshasa way of doing things. A true rakshasa wrests what he wants by using the most hostile means. That’s what creates terror, and terror is what keeps us in charge of this world. The power of weapons is the greatest power.”20

“I haven’t shared some secrets of my life with anyone yet.” Ravana admitted replying to Mahaparsva’s shameless suggestion. “I had once defiled a celestial angel named Punjjikadevi who unfortunately happened to be the daughter of Lord Brahma. When she complained to her father about my misdeeds, Brahma cursed me that if I were to ever try to be intimate with a woman without her consent, my head would split into hundreds of pieces. It is that curse that always stops me from enjoying Sita without her consent. Although I am so tempted to risk the curse for the sake of ravishing her beauty, I haven’t done it yet and have somehow so far managed to restrain myself.”21

He turned to Kumbhakarna. “It is time to let Rama know our power. I am unconquerable like the ocean and swift like the wind. He is unwittingly waking up a sleeping lion. He hasn’t experienced my blazing arrows that sting like venomous snakes. He has not experienced the pain of death my arrows cause. When powerful gods such as Indra, Varuna and Kubera couldn’t match my power, who is this trivial human? Kumbhakarna, I have full faith in your loyalty and your prowess. Let us put an end to these frivolous jokers.”

Seeing the progression of the discussion, Vibhishan stood up. His brother had cleverly brought in Kumbhakarna to pivot the conversation. Even then nothing would really change. Vibhishan felt it was important to let everyone know that, lest they remain in their illusory worlds of superiority and high-mindedness. To think calmly, one had to be on the ground and not floating in the clouds. He spoke with clarity and with no intent of massaging anyone’s ego. In a land filled with ego massagers, someone had to speak the truth openly. “O king, Sita is not the beautiful angelic nymph you consider Her to be. Please know that She is a deadly serpent wrapped around your neck. Her pearly teeth are Her venomous fangs and Her fingers are Her hoods. Only foolish people are careless around a poisonous snake. Before She bites you, please return Her to Rama. Sita is kala ratri or the dark night of Lanka. From the moment She has entered Lanka, it has been going through doom. Do not underestimate the power of Lord Rama’s arrows that resemble thunderbolts. If you do not give Sita back, you will find the heads of demons flying off in all directions soon. Hordes of vanaras will fight tooth and nail to cause enough and more havoc in Lanka. None of us is capable of handling that brute force. Neither Kumbhakarna nor Indrajit nor these boasting rakshasas will survive. No one in the three worlds can protect you from the arrows of Rama. Even if you escape to the abode of death itself, His arrows will chase you and fell you.”

“Why is fear so much a part of your vocabulary?” Prahasta cut him short. “When the whole rakshasa clan does not know the meaning of the word ‘fear,’ how are you so fearful? When we don’t fear even the celestials, how do you fear mere humans?”

His words made Vibhishan furious. “You fool, you brag because your body hasn’t been penetrated by the sharp arrows of Rama. Once a single arrow of Rama penetrates your body, no words will spill out of your foul mouth. Let me assure you that none of you is capable of facing Rama in a battle. Not Ravana, not Kumbhakarna, not even his sons Nikumbha or Indrajit, not even you, Prahasta, are capable of withstanding the fury of Rama’s arrows. Our most powerful warriors Devantaka, Narantaka, Atikaya, the gigantic Atiratha and Akampana are also not qualified to face the mighty Rama.

Our king is filled with the seven evil habits that every leader should abstain from—harshness of speech, relentlessness in offering punishment, superlative degree of extravagance, addiction to intoxicants, lust for women, addiction to hunting and great attachment to gambling. When our king is already weak in all these departments, he is made weaker by being surrounded by foolish companions such as all of you, who are actually his enemies in disguise. Your advice will ensure the ultimate destruction of our king and the kingdom. If you claim to be his real well-wishers, you must collaborate and help him find release from this mad obsession with Sita. Be like the friends and relatives who help exorcise the ghost from a possessed man sometimes by hitting him hard. If you don’t rescue him soon, the ocean named Rama will engulf him just as the ocean sometimes submerges small islands. Please give good counsel and help your master make a realistic assessment of the strength of his enemy and his own powers.”22

“Uncle! Stop this nonsense!” A furious Indrajit stood up, completely fed up of Vibhishan’s rant. He was shocked Vibhishan was even allowed to speak so much. “You may be intelligent, but your words have no meaning in the present context. Here might speaks, not wisdom. Uncle, your words are meaningless and only reveal your fear. You are actually a blot on our family. Even the rakshasas not born into our family wouldn’t speak like this. In the entire rakshasa race, you are the only one that lacks strength, heroism, power, courage and valor. You will go down in history as the worst rakshasa ever. Why are you trying to frighten us by speaking highly about ordinary humans? Don’t you remember that iconic day when I slammed the mighty Indra to the floor? Not only did I defeat him, but I even pulverized his gigantic carrier, the celebrated Airavata. I still can hear the screams of the frightened celestials from the time I pulled out the teeth of that stupid elephant. When I have crushed the celestials, do you think I will be afraid to face two puny humans?”23

“Ha! Ha! Ha!” Vibhishan laughed at his nephew’s bravado. “You are still a baby in your mother’s womb. You are a child with unstable thoughts. Your budding mind is too immature to comprehend the truths of life. All that you just rattled out are simply words of selfdestruction. The reality is that you are in fact your father’s greatest enemy. I would say that you should be eliminated right away to secure the future of Lanka. Whoever has recommended you to be in the assembly of counselors is the biggest fool and should be killed. Your advice is stupid, irresolute, rude, unwise, evil and inexperienced. The arrows of Rama possess a form like the god of death himself and to ensure our safety from those arrows, let us simply return Sita to Rama, respectfully, along with wonderful precious gifts.”

“That’s enough! I will hear no more!” Ravana was trembling in great anger. “One can stay with an enemy or a snake, but should never be in the company of anyone who claims to be a friend but is an enemy. There is great envy among relatives. This is true especially when a person is very successful, effective, highly educated and courageous. Relatives always try to humiliate such an achiever and rejoice when calamity strikes him. Such cynical relatives with treacherous minds and twisted thoughts can never be trusted. Even forest creatures will vouch for this. I know of Padmavana, an elephant that saw some men approach it with nooses in their hands. He said that he did not fear fire or weapons or even nooses as much as he feared his selfish relatives who he knew would let him down to save themselves.

Vibhishan, I have been observing you for some time now. I am convinced that you are unable to handle the praises everyone across the world heaps on me. My super success is like salt on your sore wounds. Two water droplets cannot stay together on the lotus leaf for too long and they slide off, similarly, wicked people cannot hold on to friendship for too long and they part ways. Just as clouds may sometimes thunder in autumn but it will never rain, selfish friends cannot have a tender heart. Disloyal friends are like the honeybee that extracts nectar from a flower but does not stick to it. Just as the honeybee that tries to suck nectar from grass never finds it there, a selfish person cannot have friendship in his heart. Just as an elephant bathes in a river and then abuses its own body by throwing mud on it, a selfish person’s presence abuses the purity and cleanliness of the relationship of friendship.

Vibhishan, if you were not my brother, you would be dead by now. I would have eliminated in the most violent manner any person daring to speak in my presence in the tone you used. I order you to get out of my sight and my kingdom.”

Ravana got off his throne and walked all the way down to where Vibhishan was and kicked him with his left foot pushing him off his seat. A series of gasps went up and down from those in the courtroom before everything fell silent again. This was a family fight, and no one had the right to comment. Ravana was shaking with anger as Vibhishan recovered from the powerful kick.24

Vibhishan now knew that everything was over and that there was nothing left to speak or hear. Even then he felt compelled to give his brother parting advice. Even though his brother was the personification of adharma, he was attached to him. He could not disregard his filial connection to his brother. He strived for his brother’s well-being despite not getting any result. This kick, which proved to be the last straw, broke Vibhishan’s attachment to his brother.

Bond Broken

It is not easy to break away from any attachment on your own. Only the bad behavior of the person we are attached to forces us to break that bond. As soon as Ravana kicked him, Vibhishan thought this was God’s plan to show him the reality of this relationship. He told his elder brother, “You are like my father, therefore, even in your kick there is something good for me.”

The acharyas explain that when Ravana became angry and kicked Vibhishan out of his kingdom, he also kicked out Rajya Lakshmi. Hence he lost his kingdom.

Ravana had closed all options for Vibhishan except one— surrendering to Lord Rama. Vibhishan decided to take that path with patience. He was convinced Rama would end the demonic community.

Vibhishan even had a smile when he spoke to his brother. He did not hate his brother, in fact he loved him dearly. All that he had spoken in the past was not at all out of envy but out of concern. His parting message to a brother he loved so much was one of justification for his actions. “O king, you are my elder brother. The elder brother is like one’s own father. Even if he deviates from the path of dharma, he should be respected. The only reason I said whatever I did was because I do not wish to see you being killed by Rama. It’s easy to have people flatter you, but it’s very rare to find someone who will speak the truth. Please forgive me for speaking the truth even though it seems unpalatable to the ears. Please protect yourself and this city. I wish and pray that all ends well with you. I am leaving now. I am sure you will be happier without me.”25

Vibhishan picked up his mace and started to walk out. Just before stepping out of the courtroom, he turned around and announced that anyone willing to leave with him could do so right away. Four of his most trusted accomplices got up and joined him.

Ravana breathed heavily as he watched his brother step out of the courtroom and his life. Vibhishan did not turn back even once for one last look at the kingdom and the king he had served his life so far. Lanka was history. A new future awaited him across the ocean….

Lessons from Vibhishan’s Departure

Vibhishan’s life underwent a sudden change. He had to make some tough choices. He had to choose between:

A life of opulence and a life of satisfaction

A life of compromise and a life of focus

A life of pretence and a life of substance

A life of exploitation and a life of sacrifice

A life of vices and a life of values

A life of security and a life of unpredictability

If only he had tolerated Ravana, he would have had everything. But because tolerating Ravana meant embracing a life of adharma, he gave up everything and chose the path of the truth.

When Vibhishan left Lanka, Valmiki muni called him Sriman. The word Sriman means the one who is blessed by Sri, the goddess of fortune, which means the one living a life of opulence. Why did Valmiki call him Sriman when he had lost everything? Although he had lost everything material, he now had access to the greatest treasure—the lotus feet of Lord Rama—and that made him Sriman in the truest sense.

Some additional thoughts on Vibhishan–Ravana interactions:

Ravana strangely allows Vibhishan to leave and go to Rama. Ideally, he should have imprisoned or killed him. Instead, he allowed him to join the enemy. Ravana had a secret thought behind it. He believed that just because he had followed Vibhishan’s advice on not killing Hanuman, his Lanka was set ablaze and razed to the ground. He felt that Rama would be destroyed. He felt Vibhishan’s advice led only to destruction.

When Vibhishan went to Rama, he went with great joy. It’s usually very tough to be happy just after a very public insult. He could not even meet his family before leaving. How was he happy then? This is an indicator of his exalted spiritual status. Anyone in such a situation would feel insulted, angry and vengeful, but Vibhishan felt joyous because he strongly believed that god has a hand in whatever happens in life. He felt that Ravana’s kick would lead him to find shelter at Rama’s feet. So far, he had felt the hard feet of Ravana, now he would feel the soft feet of Rama.

A happy state of mind when everything is going well (or anukul), it reflects a material mindset. A happy state of mind even when things aren’t going so well (pratikul), reflects a spiritual mindset. A spiritualist is happy in every situation of life, good or bad. When Rama left His kingdom and was exiled, He felt great joy as He saw it as an opportunity to associate with saints. When Vibhishan was exiled from his kingdom, he saw it as an opportunity to associate with Rama and the saintly vanaras.

When one takes shelter of God, all fear disappears. Fear is mostly imaginary, much like Vibhishan’s imaginary fear of the consequences of opposing Ravana. He always felt that Ravana had controlled the elements and all the gods, so he was powerless against such a powerful person. But from the moment Hanuman entered Lanka, Vibhishan realized that Ravana had actually no control over anything. If he really had, he could have prevented the burning down of his city. That realization dissipated all his fears. He became so fearless that he had the confidence to announce his intent of seeking shelter under Rama in an open courtroom. If he had any trace of fear left in him, he would have disappeared from Lanka clandestinely in the middle of the night. If one takes sincere shelter of Lord Rama, there is no need to be afraid. All fear will disappear.

## Chapter 2

Consultation and Inclusion

It took a dozen days of relentless progress to move the whole army down to the southern tip of the landmass. Directing an army of millions of vanaras was no ordinary task. But Angad was no ordinary leader and no stranger to his team making the impossible possible. He had helmed the Find Sita Mission and succeeded.

As the troops moved ahead with Rama and Lakshmana at the center riding on the broad shoulders of a now-resized gigantic Hanuman, every vanara soldier was aware that time was of the essence. Everyone knew of the fast-approaching deadline. Seventeen days had already passed since Sita’s 30-day ultimatum. After burning down Lanka and meeting Sita, it had taken Hanuman almost two days to return to the vanara army waiting for him. It had then taken the army three days to travel from the southern ocean to Kishkinda because the division moving upward was relatively smaller. But the retreat to the southern ocean from Kishkinda took much longer because of the colossal size of the army.

Every moment of these twelve days had been hard on Rama even though He was now moving toward His beloved. It felt much less painful when He was clueless about Her exact location because He had no clear indication of where to look for Her. Now the pain of anticipation was far worse. He spent each day of the journey in constant gratitude toward Hanuman for accomplishing something that was seemingly unachievable and in fond remembrance of Sita’s words that Hanuman had so deftly recounted.26

As Rama rode on Hanuman’s shoulders, He couldn’t help but admire the gentle giant’s sincere dedication. Hanuman was the ideal assistant. Not just Him, but the entire Raghu clan was forever obliged to Hanuman for his contribution to their dynasty by this single act of finding Sita.27

An Assistant Above Par

Rama identified three types of assistants. The first is the enthusiastic type. The master entrusts a very difficult task to the assistant who not only performs it with great eagerness but also accomplishes a whole lot more. Rama considered such an assistant to be first-class or superior. The second is the stick-to-the-brief type. Even though very capable, such assistants do only what is told—no more and no less. They put no extra effort and do not think or act beyond the call of duty. They execute the master’s instructions but without thinking on behalf of the master. Such an assistant, according to Rama, was second-class or mediocre. The third is the incompetent type. Such assistants, even though capable, do not carry out the master’s instructions. They find some excuse to delay or postpone action and eventually blame it on another person or reason. Rama considered such an assistant to be third-class or inferior. In Rama’s eyes, Hanuman far exceeded the level of the first-class assistant and was in a league of his own.

At the end of the sojourn, they stood at the top of the Mahendra mountain. Rama trekked up to the highest peak for a better and wider view. From where He stood, all He could see was this endless expanse of water. He looked behind at the vast vanara army. He could not help but compare it with the deep blue carpet in front of Him. Right ahead was the surging ocean and right behind, for as far back as He could see, was an infinite wave of vanara soldiers. He wondered which of the two would hold sway. Could the vanara army tame the ocean? Or would the ocean submerge the vanara army? Just then a surge leaped dreadfully high and dashed against the rocks at the foot of Mahendra mountain, as if to proclaim the ocean’s victory as inevitable. Rama suddenly felt dejected by the thought of the possibility of the entire army’s failure to cross the ocean. Noticing a downcast Rama, Sugriva stepped in with a few words of comfort. “So what if all vanaras cannot leap across the ocean like Hanuman? Just command us, and we will build a formidable bridge across the ocean in no time. The might of the vanara army lies in accomplishing impossible tasks. We will move mountains and cross oceans to bring Your back Sita from Lanka. Rest assured Ravana will be killed very soon.”28

On hearing Sugriva, Rama felt a surge of confidence. He quickly directed the entire vanara army to set up camp on the shore at the bottom of Mahendra mountain. It was now time to strategize their move toward Lanka. As the army went about the task at hand, Rama walked closer to the shore and looked longingly toward where His beloved was held captive. They were now only an ocean apart. Of course, it wasn’t just a puddle He could hop over. This abode of Varuna was boundless and inhabited by countless fierce alligators and dangerous fishes. The foamy waves grew higher, bigger and fiercer with the setting sun. By nightfall, the rough surf settled into a devilish dance, almost as if the fathomless and bottomless ocean was an even bigger enemy than Ravana. It was vast and melded with the skyline—the ocean and the firmament all becoming one dark blur.

With Rama stood the whole vanara army, sprawled along the entire shore. With gaping mouths, they stared at the vast formidable ocean they would have to cross to reach Lanka. The army, too, was so large that it almost seemed as if one ocean was looking at another ocean. Surely, the ocean was equally amazed at countless vanaras standing on its shore.

The beachfront was now a massive, bustling vanara army encampment. Mainda and Dvivida, supervisors of the camp project, busied themselves with setting everything up in record time. The vanaras worked at a feverish pace to meet the deadline. Meanwhile, Rama glanced at the moon and wondered, “My beloved Sita must also be looking at the moon from the other side of the ocean.” The moon then became the lovers’ meeting point, connecting Them through Their eyes.

Rama shared his agony with Lakshmana who had been his only confidant for the past so many months since Sita went missing. “Lakshmana, I hope that this breeze that just brushed past His skin flows on reaches Sita and caresses Her, thus connecting Us by touch.” Lakshmana held Rama close to his heart and consoled him. “Come back to the present, O Rama, do not lament about the past and the loss.” Lakshmana helped his brother stabilize His emotions and focus. Rama gathered His strength and began to offer His evening prayers.

Long-distance Love

Sita and Rama were at this point technically in time in a longdistance relationship.

Long-distance relationships thrive on four things:

Remembering time spent together

Finding reasons to remember each other

Finding subtle connectors and connections

Hoping to reunite again

seeking shelter from a foe

“Sorry, Rama is too busy.” Sugriva tried to stave off the unwelcome guest who had come by air. Although it seemed offensive, Sugriva felt it was important to be rude at the outset. He was convinced the arrival of an enemy only meant trouble even if he claimed otherwise. Sugriva was quick to make wild assumptions about Vibhishan’s intentions of arriving at their camp before even wanting to hear or know his side of the story.

Loyal and Devoted Foe

Valmiki does not mention how long it took Vibhishan to cross the ocean. In a journey that was extremely meaningful to him, Vibhishan was not too bothered about how long it took. He could not care less about the ocean, the distance, or his speed. He crossed the ocean physically, but his mind was somewhere else, on a much higher plane. This truth is applicable to everyone. If we calculate the distance and monitor our speed, it’s possible we will become exhausted, but if we travel like Vibhishan, even the biggest obstacle, the biggest ocean, becomes easy to traverse. So how was Vibhishan traveling? He was moving, absorbed in loving thoughts of God. While his body performed the act of crossing the ocean, his heart and intelligence were absorbed in God.

Six thoughts on Vibhishan’s mind when crossing the ocean: “I will be blessed by the darshan of

the feet that delivered Ahalya, wife of rishi Gautam;

the feet that purified dandakvan, the forest of Dandakaranya;

the feet that Janki keeps in Her heart;

the feet that chased the fake golden deer;

the feet that Lord Shiva carefully preserves like a lotus in his heart; and

the feet that wore the padukas now with Bharat.”

Most people consider Vibhishan a traitor who was disloyal to his brother and kingdom. But acharyas consider him an ideal purusha. He did not buckle under greed or fear, nor did he con Ravana. He was a man of principles. He strived to give dharmic advice to Ravana and steer him on to the right path. In return, Ravana kicked him in a full assembly and threw him out of Lanka. Even then, he did not adopt any underhand means to seek revenge. Instead, he declared his decision to seek Rama’s shelter to the public. If loyalty meant endorsing a brother’s evil plans, then Ramayana has no place for such loyalty.

Vibhishan had been dealing with his own pains, and he had high hopes when he arrived on this side of the ocean. He had learned quite a lot about Rama’s greatness, but had heard about His kindness a lot more. Humiliation at the hands of his own brother Ravana had wounded him deeply. He was only trying to help his brother. In fact, he was doing everything possible to save him from certain ruin. Rather than thank him, Ravana disgraced him and kicked him out of Lanka. When his own had rejected him, what was the possibility of a stranger accepting him? Even though this question had clouded his mind in the beginning, he had decided to brush it aside and focus on the goodness of Rama. His faith in dharma and the existence of goodness hadn’t waned. But on hearing Sugriva’s curt words, for a moment Vibhishan felt this side wasn’t any better than the other. But he hung on to hope because he had met the sensitive Hanuman before and had heard a lot about the most compassionate Rama.29

Not wanting to engage in any negative conversation, Vibhishan took on a humble approach and explained his dire situation to Sugriva. He said, “My dear vanara chief, let me explain to you once again that I am desperately looking to surrender my life to the service of Lord Rama. I have left behind my family, wealth, prestige, security, position and residence to wholeheartedly be under Lord Rama’s shelter. Please give me a chance to meet Him just once. Please remember that I am a demon and the mind of a demon is very fickle. Right now, I feel like surrendering myself to Rama. If I do not do that right away when my mind desperately wants to, it might just refuse to surrender later. Please do not create any obstacle in the process of my surrender.”30

With folded hands, Vibhishan appealed to Sugriva. That posture made Sugriva thoughtful. Vibhishan hadn’t even landed on the shore and was still suspended in the sky along with four of his men, two on either side. Vibhishan stood there, his golden mace dangling between his palms. Sugriva whispered into the ears of the vanara standing closest to him. “This rakshasa is very dangerous because he is holding weapons so powerful that even Rama’s power will be redundant. You see those folded hands? Those are his most powerful weapons. When Rama sees his folded hands, His heart will melt. This person knows how to win hearts of even his enemies. He seems to be very aware of Rama’s weakness—compassion. Although the rakshasa claims he has abandoned Ravana to join Rama in His venture to reclaim Sita, I strongly feel He is a spy who has come to gather information about our strategies, weaknesses and strengths.”31

Finding no way out other than involving Lord Rama in this matter, Sugriva instructed his most trusted vanara men to stand alert and keep a watchful eye on the rakshasa gang while he was away consulting Rama on the matter. Surgriva started off on a negative note straight away when going to deliver the news to Lord Rama, who was seated on a rock in a discussion with Lakshmana and Hanuman.

On seeing a tense Sugriva approach them, Rama shifted his attention toward the vanara king. “An enemy has come claiming to be a friend. Vibhishan, the younger brother of our arch enemy, Ravana, has come seeking shelter. He claims he has nothing to do with the rakshasa clan now. But to me he seems like an owl infiltrating a community of crows to destroy it when the crows are least alert at night. Having a rakshasa in our midst and that too the brother of Ravana is the same as inviting trouble. These rakshasas are deceitful and very cunning. They can change their form at will and disappear in no time. They may possibly trick us into having differences among ourselves. He may say that he is in trouble and needs shelter, but how does he expect us to believe him? In my opinion, Vibhishan should be arrested along with his allies immediately. Let us not trust his crooked intention and repose our faith in him. In fact, I would go on to say that we should just terminate them immediately and diffuse any impending danger to our army.”32

Many-headed Kin

Sugriva refers to Vibhishan as the younger brother of Dashanan, another name for the ten-headed Ravana. He could have called him the son of sage Vishrava. But he did this to emphasize his evil connection with Ravana. Rama, however, looked at Lakshmana and laughed. He was the elder brother of Sahasranan, the thousandheaded Sheshanag. So he had nothing to fear about the younger brother of Dashanan.

Sugriva was an orator, and when he spoke, people listened. By this time, a sizeable group gathered around assessing that the conversation was of a serious nature. After Sugriva stopped speaking, every vanara gathered there couldn’t agree less.33

But when they saw Rama’s eyes, they froze. Even their thoughts froze in their minds. His eyes were blazing with anger. They had never seen Him so angry. They understood that He was totally disgusted with the idea of killing a guest who had come seeking shelter.

Hiding His anger within moments, Rama spoke to the vanaras gathered around. “My dear friends, every one of us needs good advice to help us make wise decisions. One should especially seek advice from those who are his well-wishers. Now I ask all your advice on this matter. Please express your opinions freely. Can I give shelter to Vibhishan?”34

The vanaras were quite taken aback that Rama was actually consulting with them and genuinely wanted to know their opinion. They were not used to this idea of leaders asking them for inputs. It made them feel empowered. “At least our opinions matter to someone, that too someone as great as Rama no less.” Taking the call for feedback quite seriously, the vanaras put on their thinking caps. One vanara who otherwise stayed quiet, suddenly began to speak eloquently. “O Rama, nothing in the three worlds is unknown to You. Yet You seek our counsel simply to honor us. You are the personification of whatever is truthful in this world. Whatever You choose to do is always after sufficient due diligence. Your great memory recalls with immense gratitude the good deeds of anyone who has done anything remotely significant to You. Your heart is ever committed to Your friends. I hope our suggestions help You arrive at the right conclusion.”35

Angad took on from there. “I feel we need to assess him before taking a final call. After all, he is technically still from the enemy camp. No one should be above suspicion unless proven innocent. I have observed that deceitful people impersonating well-wishers often strike when least expected and where it hurts the most. Only after sufficiently examining the pros and cons of any decision must we take action. On studying him carefully, if we find considerable faults in him, we must reject him instantly. On the other hand, even after thorough examination, if we find lot of good in him, we must accept him no matter his background.”

Seconding Angad’s suggestion, Sharaba offered an implementation idea. “Let us assign a spy to monitor Vibhishan. Under sustained observation, one cannot hide one’s true nature, especially during private moments. The spy should be intelligent and be his shadow. Only after this investigation is completed assiduously can we arrive at a conclusion.”

Assimilating all his wisdom and scriptural knowledge, Jambavan said, “Close proximity to the sinful Ravana who commits sins without any conscience is Vibhishan’s greatest fault. That alone is reason enough to suspect his intentions. Someone who has been in close contact with sin personified cannot be assumed to be sinless unless proven otherwise.”

Mainda did not quite agree. He felt this matter needed to be handled more sensitively than thus far suggested. “Let us question him gently. It is possible to ascertain what is on a person’s mind by simply posing the right questions. After you sufficiently ascertain his mindset, listen to your heart and act.”36

After hearing out some prominent vanara leaders, Lord Rama turned toward Hanuman, eager to know what he felt about this matter. His keen intelligence and ability to assess people quickly mattered a lot. Offering his respects to Lord Rama, Hanuman shared his thoughts. “Dear Lord, Your intelligence is superior to all of us put together. Even Brihaspati, the guru of the gods would feel intellectually ashamed in Your presence. Your eloquence is superlative. In spite of Your superiority in every way possible, You still chose to allow us to give our opinions. That is a sign of Your greatness. What I am going to offer now is neither my attempt at proving my superiority over my friends who have already spoken nor my desire to take on an argumentative tone.
