I am neither here to compete with them nor am I here to win any debates. I am simply voicing my opinion because this crucial matter needs to be decided very carefully.37

I strongly feel there is an error in the whole analysis so far. It is not possible to analyze anyone’s intentions just by observing for some time. No matter how intelligent the spy, deciphering another’s intention is next to impossible.38

Humans expertly hide their character within a thick shell of good behavior. Any judicious investigation into human character for a short term is bound to be error-ridden. Intentions are best understood when a person is entrusted with some responsibility. Only while executing a task does a person’s true nature surface. But understanding the crucial and delicate juncture that we are at, I am not sure if it would even be advisable to risk a test with Vibhishan. About the view that Vibhishan has arrived at an inopportune time to take shelter, I believe there is no right or wrong time to arrive at a conclusion about the right thing to do. The moment one comes to that realization is the right time, and one should immediately act on it. Vibhishan has always been trying to correct Ravana, but probably today he realized that his brother is incorrigible. Perhaps today, he decided that Ravana is pure evil and that there is no hope of ever transforming him. Perhaps today, he has fully understood that You, Lord Rama, has so many good qualities that are totally missing in his brother. Perhaps today, he has recognized that his nature matches that of Yours, O Lord, and that he is totally opposite to his brother. Alignment is impossible between two people with opposing personalities.

About him being questioned cleverly, I consider that a wrong approach, too, especially when dealing with a wise person. When a wise person is questioned, no matter how discreetly, he is apprehensive. Such questioning can convert a potential friend into a lifelong foe. One needs to be astute in assessing intonation to understand a person’s intention simply by the tone. Even that is very difficult in a rapid conversation.39

I can say one thing for sure based on my limited experience in facereading. There is no reason to doubt him as his face is very bright. A person with deceit in his heart cannot approach the enemy camp so fearlessly. I do not find any reason to doubt him. In addition, my encounter in Lanka also aids my conclusion. When all the rakshasas in Ravana’s court were making plans to kill me, Vibhishan was the only one who stood up and spoke against it. Eloquently quoting the dharma sastras, he declared that an envoy should never be killed. His words triggered a transformation in Ravana’s heart, and he decided to burn my tail instead. From that day on, I have faith in his righteousness. Only a righteous person can stand up for what is right, even in an assembly of people who support the wrong. He was already righteous, even before I met him, but after he heard of the greatness of Lord Rama, the killing of Vali and the appointment of Sugriva as the new king, surely, he must have had similar desires about ruling Lanka after overthrowing his treacherous brother with the help of Lord Rama. This being the case, it makes perfect sense for us to accept him as he is in need of our help. Just as Lord Rama helped the needy Sugriva, and now Sugriva is going all out to help Him, similarly if Lord Rama helps the needy Vibhishan, he will go all out to help Him.

Shelter for the Bent and the Straight

Hanuman went on to tell Rama, “If You want, You can ask Your bow and arrow.”

What does he mean?

Both bow and arrows have Rama’s shelter. This affirms that God gives shelter to both the straight and the bent. He sometimes sends the straight arrow far away from Him, but the bent bow is always near him. He knows the arrow will go to its mark and then return to Him, but He has doubts if the bent crooked bow will come back, so He keeps it near. Hanuman implied that if bow and arrows could get Lord Rama’s shelter why not Vibhishan.

Being straight indicates being honest and simple. Being bent indicates being crooked and possibly diplomatic.

This does not mean that we should be bent because God will accept anyway. It just means that even bent people have hope of getting his shelter.

“My dear Lord, I have spoken according to my limited understanding and ability to decipher complicated subjects. I now request you to decide what is correct based on your unlimited wisdom and ability to easily understand most complex subjects.”

After hearing everyone patiently, Lord Rama finally asked, “Do you all want to hear My opinion?” Everyone was amazed and nodded in agreement. His humility touched them. He didn’t even need to ask them their opinions. They had offered their lives to Him, and He could take decisions on His own. Yet, not only did He seek their counsel, He was even asking for their permission to express His opinion. They were awestruck by His exalted leadership style.

“I think you did not hear Me well. I did not ask if I should accept Vibhishan. I only asked you, if I could give him shelter? Your answers simply had to be a yes or a no. A yes would mean I am capable of giving shelter to him and no would mean I am incapable of giving shelter to him. My dear friends, please know that I am from the dynasty of Shibi Maharaj. My ancestor King Shibi was ready to offer his life to save a pigeon that had taken his shelter. When this desperately scared pigeon sought the king’s refuge while trying to escape from a hawk, the king assured it of full protection. That’s when the hawk came and demanded its right over its meal. Both the hawk and the pigeon were his subjects, and he had to cater to the needs of both. King Shibi placed the pigeon on a weighing scale and matched its weight by cutting off chunks of flesh from his own thighs. He thus saved the pigeon’s life and at the same time addressed the hawk’s need, too. When I belong to such a dynasty, how can I not give shelter to someone desperately seeking it? When I go back to Ayodhya and meet my spiritual master Vasistha, what reason will I give as to why I did not give shelter to Vibhishan. Wouldn’t he be very disappointed in me if he hears that I rejected Vibhishan’s request?40

Let me tell you a story. A hunter who had gone to the forest to hunt, spent the whole day in vain. By the end of the day, he was famished. It began to rain heavily, making it impossible for him to find his way back home and causing him great inconvenience. He created a fire to keep himself warm, but his stomach began growling in hunger. Right above him was a pigeon’s nest. The pigeon recognized the hunter as someone who had killed its wife a few days back. But when the pigeon saw him suffering from cold and hunger, he chose to forget his enmity and focus on being compassionate to the guest who had sought shelter in his house. He flung himself into the fire and roasted himself as an offering to his guest. When an ordinary pigeon cannot mistreat an enemy seeking, then why should we do that? If a pigeon can be so noble in its thoughts, why can’t we emulate its compassion toward a guest seeking shelter?”

At this point, Sugriva interrupted Rama. “But if someone has rejected his brother when he needs him most, how can such a person be trusted? Why will he not betray us similarly?”

Rama smiled looking at Lakshmana. Turning toward Sugriva, He began to speak with passion. “What irony! If I had thought similarly when I met you, how could I have trusted you? If Vibhishan has betrayed Ravana, you betrayed Vali, too. What is the difference between the two of you? Just as Vali was wrong to kidnap your wife and throw you out of the kingdom, Ravana was wrong in excommunicating Vibhishan from Lanka. If I have accepted you, then why can’t I accept him? We cannot expect all brothers to be like Bharata or all sons of a father to be like Me nor all friends to be like you.

As far as I am concerned, it doesn’t really matter if the demon is good or bad. He is not capable of causing any harm to me or our army. No matter of how many of them come, I can crush them with my thumb. They are no match for my valor.41

Let me now tell you what Kandu maharishi’s opinion is on this matter. Anyone who spreads his hands in front of you or cups his hands begging you or any one in great misery or anyone who seeks refuge should never be killed or harmed. An enemy who seeks protection, no matter how arrogant or bad, should always be protected by the strong-minded. Even if this means sacrificing your own life, you should not hesitate to offer protection to those seeking shelter. The one who is capable but does not offer protection is considered highly sinful. Such negligence brings infamy, destroys reputation, diminishes one’s strength and results in loss of higher realms after life. I declare this today for all of you to hear loud and clear.”

*sakrdeva prapannaya*

*tavaasmiti ca yaacate \|*

*abhayam sarva bhutebhyo*

*dadaamyetat vratam mama \|\|*

\[Anyone who just once tells Me “I am Yours”, to such a person I assure protection against all types of calamities. This is My solemn vow.\]

*anayainam harishreshtha:*

*dattamasya abhayam mayaa \|*

*vibhIshano vaa sugriva!*

*yadi vaa raavana svayam \|\|*

—Valmiki Ramayana yuddha. 18.34.

\[O Sugriva, be it Vibhishan or even Ravana himself, anyone who comes to seek My shelter, I will assure them protection. Get them to Me immediately.\]

Do Not Fear

Sugriva wanted to at least interrogate him first and decide later.

He was firm in his opinion, refusing to bend. Lord Rama understood the love behind this stand. He respected his opinion. He did not agree with him, but he replied by first appreciating him.

He said He had taken a vow to remove fear from whoever sought His shelter.

Politics is centered on doubts. It believes one should neither trust someone who does not deserve it nor trust someone totally even if they deserve it. The fear from trust destroys the original trust.

In this world, friends can easily become enemies. Those who trust easily get duped by the very people they trust. Everyone wants power, opulence and self-enjoyment. Friendship is merely a compromise based on give and take. We never find two friends on an equal footing. So if one is more honored, the other becomes jealous, wanting it all that for self. A politician is always alert with friends and enemies and protected by a doubting nature and alertness. But all this is applicable only for those who want power and position for themselves.

Rama is different. He is only concerned with others’ happiness. He does not act on the politics of doubt and fear. His vow makes that clear. Anything based on fear cannot be permanent. Fear can force something to be done, but it is like a sword dangling overhead. A powerful person can inspire others to be good through fear and can also inspire to be bad out of fear. One fear cannot be dealt with another fear. Parshuram uses fear to improve society while Ravana uses fear to downgrade society. Thus Parshuram and Ravana can be competitors but not options. The real answer to fear is fearlessness. That is Rama’s message as an avatara—do not fear.

giving shelter to a foe

“Do not delay the process of shelter by coming and asking Me. Just bring them to Me directly,” Rama said.

Witnessing the magnanimity of Rama, Hanuman immediately fell on his knees with tears in his eyes. Fortunate is that person who has a master who is so kind and welcoming. All the vanara heroes present there offered followed Hanuman and prostrated in front of Lord Rama. This was a totally new experience for them. They were learning the process of sharanagati or taking shelter. Till now they had learned how eager the master was to offer shelter to those who eagerly seek His shelter. Now they were about to learn the mood of taking shelter from Vibhishan who just walked into the assembly of Lord Rama. This time, the treatment meted to Vibhishan was much more loving and welcoming. He immediately understood that this transformation was surely the effect of the words of Rama. His respect for Rama soared up immediately. He instantly knew that he had made the right decision.

At the first glimpse of Lord Rama, Vibhishan fell on the ground and prostrated in front of his new master.42

The vanaras noticed the peculiar obeisance. His hands were held over his head and folded in great veneration. His crossed legs stretched behind him. While everyone wondered about the importance of such an homage, Rama smiled understanding the mood of Vibhishan. By folding his hands in front of him, Vibhishan was trying to tell Rama that he had no possession with him and was completely depended on the Him for sustenance. By crossing his legs behind, Vibhishan was trying to tell Rama that he had nowhere else to go and his legs were tied up now and he could not wander away to anyone or anywhere.43

Rama picked up Vibhishan and welcomed him. Vibhishan shared with Rama the pain of his humiliation in Lanka for speaking the truth. He revealed all the secrets, weaknesses and strengths of the Lankan forces. He then promised complete support in ensuring eliminating Ravana and freeing Lanka from his tyrannical rule. Although he loved his brother, he realized that longer his brother lived, the more pain and trauma this world would suffer. Seeing Vibhishan anguish, Lord Rama vowed, “I shall not return to Ayodhya without eliminating Ravana.” Having said that, Rama embraced Vibhishan with great love. Introducing him to everyone in the army, Rama declared him as his seventh brother. Vibhishan’s real brother had kicked him out and Rama, a stranger, declared him as his brother at the very first meeting.44

Then Rama did something no one was prepared for. He asked Lakshmana to get water from the ocean for the coronation of Vibhishan. The vanaras were flabbergasted. They couldn’t fathom how Rama could coronate him king even before the war was fought and even before the previous king was overthrown. Vibhishan was equally bewildered. But everyone knew that Rama knew what he was doing. With waters from the ocean and chanting of special mantras, Rama crowned Vibhishan the king of Lanka.

Supreme Sacrifice

A person can never give up three desires. These are desire for wealth, association of a spouse and children, and fame. But Vibhishan had left all three and come. The day he left the golden Lanka, he renounced his desire for wealth. The day he left his family, he left desire for loving association. And he also left Ravana, knowing well that it could earn him a bad name. So from Rama’s perspective, if Vibhishan had these desires, he would not have made this supreme sacrifice.

When Vibhshan questioned Rama as to why he was crowned if he didn’t have these desires, Rama replied that it was His own desire. Vibhishan did not need fame, but He did. He had to live up to the reputation of being generous. Rama was so humble that he said that by accepting the kingdom, Vibhishan had done him a favor—he had saved His reputation.

As the whole vanara army floated in an ocean of joy, they saw tears roll down Lord Rama’s cheeks. Their smiles gave way to worry. Why would Rama be sad on a joyful occasion like this? Even before they could think further, Rama spoke His heart out. “I have given everyone who has sought My refuge a kingdom and crowned them kings. Bharat took shelter, and I offered him Ayodhya. Sugriva took shelter, and I offered him Kishkinda. Vibhishan took shelter, and I have given him Lanka. But unfortunately, I was unable to offer anything to Jatayu who gave up his life while serving Me.”45

The vanaras empathized with Rama. With each passing day, they got to witness more depths in His personality. They felt as if they were evolving into better versions of themselves just by observing Him interact with people and hearing His thoughts. Suddenly one tiny vanara pushed ahead and asked. “Lord, what would You do if Ravana himself came to surrender? Now that Vibhishan is crowned the king of Lanka, what would happen to Ravana?

Rama smiled and said, “I would make Ravana the king of Ayodhya.” That answer shocked the vanaras. “If Ravana were appointed king of Ayodhya what would happen to Bharata?” was the question on everyone’s lips. Rama said with great confidence, “I would make Bharata the king of the spiritual world, the Vaikuntha planets.” Then finally the little vanara asked his last question. “Then what about You? What will You do if You offer all these kingdoms to others?”

Although the question seemed audacious for a little vanara to ask Rama, but it was a pertinent one on everyone’s mind. Rama’s answer won their hearts completely. Lord Rama said, “I will travel to all these kingdoms and serve all these wonderful souls.”

The vanaras were so happy to witness the selfless non-attached nature of their master. Vibhishan was grateful that he had made the right choice of choosing to be on the side of such an exalted personality.

Sugriva was thinking a step ahead. “How do I prepare Vibhishan for surrender. Should I suggest that he bathe in the Ganga itself? Rama replied, “Ideally I should have gone to Lanka to accept his surrender. I am thinking why I gave him so much of trouble to come all the way?”

Exposing Godhood

The process of surrendering itself is like bathing in the Ganga, there was no need of another external bath to purify oneself.

By crowning Vibhishan as king of Lanka, Rama had secured his future, thus proving His godhood here. Only God could give such an assurance even before the war had begun. The acharyas explain that though Rama expertly hides His godhood throughout the Ramayana, His godhood is exposed here.

message from the enemy

The strange creature was seen for the third time in a short duration. It seemed to be flying around, almost spying the army. Although it seemed to be have wings, something about it made Angad skeptical. Angad gestured with his eyes, commanding four of his trusted vanara chiefs to capture it for a closer inspection. The vanaras waited for the right moment and exactly when that flying creature returned once again and was hovering about the vanara army arrangement, they trapped it in the air. The lone flying creature was no match for four super powerful vanaras and was subdued.

The four vanaras dragged it to the ocean shore and chained it with iron shackles. Angad wondered what or who it was. The captured being seemed like a winged bird from far but on closer look it seemed like some kind of a bird-human hybrid. Seeing the commotion, all the leaders gathered together. Vibhishan stepped in and introduced the intruder as Shukha, one of Ravana’s best spies. As soon as the vanaras heard this, they began to beat the spy. Shukha began to beg. “Please stop. I have an important message from Ravana for King Sugriva.”

Finally, when the beating stopped, Shukha caught his breath and began speaking to the vanara King Sugriva. “O vanara chief, here is my master Lankeshwar Ravana’s message to you. You belong to the noble vanara clan and are extremely powerful being the son of Rikshraja and the younger brother of the invincible Vali. Being such a close friend of Vali, Ravana considers you a friend, too. In fact, he considers you to be like his younger brother. What difference does it make to you that Ravana has kidnapped someone else’s wife? As long as you do not have any personal enmity, you should not interfere in this matter. Return to Kishkinda with your subjects and rule happily. Even if you want to try, Lanka is inaccessible and impenetrable to even the gods, leave alone insignificant monkeys.”

Sugriva laughed loudly. “You fool! Your king is a bigger fool. Tell your king that he is not my friend. Anyone who is an enemy of Lord Rama is my enemy. Anyone who is a friend of Lord Rama is my friend. Tell your king that he is worthy of being killed. We will not only kill him but also all his relatives, descendants and friends. Once the vanara army arrives in Lanka, we will reduce the country to a pile of debris. For the sins that Ravana has committed, no one can protect him from being vanquished now. Without being aware of the strength of Rama, he has dared to kidnap his wife. Tell Ravana that his days are numbered now.”46

Sugriva’s aggressive tone excited the vanaras, and they began to pound him once again. Shukha realized there was no way he could get away alive. Out of great desperation, he began to appeal to Lord Rama who was at a distance away from the place of the commotion. Hearing the loud pleas of Shukha, Rama walked up to the place where the mob was assaulting the spy. “Please spare my life,” he begged Rama. When Angad saw that Rama had ventured in, he immediately said, “Shukha is not just a messenger but a spy who will reveal all details of our army to the enemy. While a messenger has to be treated with respect, a spy should always be punished and definitely not allowed to return.” Hearing the prince, the vanaras became even more enthusiastic to break every bone in Shukha’s body.

But Lord Rama held out His hand and signaled them to let go. A bruised and battered Shukha staggered up. He folded his hands in front of Rama with gratitude. Words don’t always have to be spoken. Shukha’s eyes spoke a million words of gratitude to Rama. As soon as he stepped out of the gang of vanaras, he leaped into the air and flew away toward Lanka with great speed flapping his wings, before the unpredictable vanaras changed their minds again.47

## Chapter 3

Revelation and Restoration

They had been staring at the ocean for the past several hours. The waves lapping against the feet of Rama ever so gently seemed to be trying to cajole Him into a smile by tickling His feet. Their father, the ocean god, wasn’t pleasing Him in any way. In fact, Rama considered the massive ocean to be a hurdle between Him and Sita.48

Rama turned toward Vibhishan for his opinion on dealing with their biggest obstacle. Vibhishan answered immediately, “O Lord Rama, do You remember Your ancestor Sagara Maharaj had done the ocean a favor by helping expand its dominion. Now is the right time to ask the ocean god to return that favor done by the Ikshvaku dynasty. I suggest Your offer prayers to invoke the ocean god and when he appears, You could ask him to lay a path to Lanka for the vanara army.”49

Rama then turned toward Lakshmana. As usual, Lakshmana was not in the mood for prayers and requests. He believed in the power of his arms and the potency of his bow. “It would be a foolish waste of time praying especially when time is ticking away so rapidly for Sita. By the might of Our arrows, we could dry up the ocean instead. O Brother, allow me to do the needful and very soon a pathway will emerge across the ocean over which the vanaras could just walk down.”

Lakshmana’s impatience always made Rama smile. Lakshmana was enthusiasm personified, and it seemed as if he had left his patience back in Ayodhya. Here were two important people giving Him two diametrically opposite views—one was from His own brother and the other from the enemy’s brother; one was loyal and the other’s loyalty was yet to be tested; one had been with Him from the day He was born and the other had just joined him. It seemed tricky and sensitive, but He had to take a call. When the vanaras saw this potentially conflicting situation and realized that Rama was caught between the two views, they became curious to find out Rama’s decision. They knew Rama. He was an expert at bridging gaps, but there was a gap so wide that it could create disunity and differences between the leaders.

Standing between the differing parties, Rama spoke up. He said, “I love you both, and your opinions matter a lot to me. I want to follow both your suggestions, but to be fair, I will take into consideration the opinions in the order they were given. I will first follow Vibhishan’s suggestion and then Lakshmana’s. I will offer prayers to the ocean god for three consecutive days. If the ocean god does not oblige by then, I will resort to Lakshmana’s opinion. That way, we give the ocean a fair chance to reciprocate and save himself from destruction.”50

The idea resonated with both and it seemed a win–win. The vanaras were impressed by Rama’s adeptness at dissolving a brewing conflict.

Choosing Peace over Violence

Rama gave preference to Vibhishan’s suggestion because He believed in peace over violence when it came to solving problems.

The ocean was Ravana’s neighbor, and Rama felt that His gentle dealing with the ocean would send an appropriate message to Ravana, and he, too, may choose to emulate the good behavior. With this in mind, Rama chose to pray to the ocean god first. But He knew well that if this did not yield any result, He would have to adopt stricter means. Thus, He did not discount Lakshmana’s idea, but rather put it on hold and asked him to wait patiently for the right time to come.

Spreading the sacred kusha grass on the sea shore, Rama sat facing eastward. He offered His salutations to the sun and the sea and began His intensive prayer sessions to invoke the ocean god. He prayed all day long and at nightfall, He lay down at the same place on the kusha grass, His arms doubling as His pillow. Not even for a moment did he abandon that spot. He was determined to use every ounce of His energy to please the ocean god and seek a pathway to Lanka. Rama spent three days and three nights on the seashore. The days and nights merged to become a long continuous intense prayer session. The ocean mostly remained silent, occasionally throwing up waves in Rama’s direction, as if provoking Him to act. By the fourth morning, Rama was very upset. This was totally audacious. He had spent three days and nights on the shore of the ocean ardently appealing to the ocean god for an audience but to no avail. The ocean continued to stare coldly at Rama with absolute disdain.

Wet and Vile

In three days Rama realized the wickedness of the ocean god. The association with Ravana, because of the close proximity between their residences, had negatively affected the ocean god. Yet Rama felt it was important to observe the person’s character before devising a strategy to deal with the person. Rama observed the ocean’s character for three days. Once He understood the mindset of the ocean god, He was able to formulate a strategy that would work.

Rama stood up feeling very angry on the fourth morning. His eyes were red with rage when He called out to Lakshmana. “Brother, you were absolutely right. Good qualities like calmness, tolerance, kind speech and straight-forwardness are considered signs of weakness by arrogant fools. In spite of My patience, the arrogant ocean god has shown no interest in empathizing with Me. Perhaps, he assumes that I must be weak and incapable because another man abducted My wife. Although the size of a spark of fire may be small, no one should underestimate it. In such a trying circumstance, I have spent three whole days and nights beseeching him. It seems as if this world only honors boastful, corrupt, shameless and debauch men. If that is the language they understand, so be it. Let Me convey My opinion in a language harsh enough to shake the very core of the ocean bed. Fetch My bow!”51

Lakshmana promptly handed over the bow to Rama. Knocking an arrow that resembled the thunderbolt weapon of Indra, Rama shot it into the ocean. The arrow sped toward the ocean, split its surface and penetrated into the deep. For a few seconds, complete silence followed. Then suddenly the dance of dissolution saw the waters begin to bubble and froth. The agitated fishes and sea animals began to jump out to the surface in great agitation. Huge tidal waves began to rise and flood the shores with deep-sea organisms and seaweeds. Sea snakes, crocodiles and sea monsters began to creep out onto the shores. The roar of waves lashing against the mountains and rocks became deafening. The splashing ocean water was hot—boiling hot. The terrible turmoil terrified the vanaras. It almost seemed as if the world was about to end.

Lakshmana grabbed his brother’s bow and tried to prevent Him from shooting any more arrows into the sea. The sages in the surrounding areas ran out and began to beg Lord Rama to set aside His destructive mood and become calm once again. But despite all the destruction, the ocean god was nowhere to be seen. Lord Rama was even more furious. Thus far, He had only demonstrated a small fraction of His prowess to reveal His potential. Often that should have been enough to goad the ocean god to act quickly. But the inert ocean god forced Lord Rama to think of a sterner step. With the supreme Brahmastra in His bow, Lord Rama began to chant mantras to invoke the power of the secondary creator into that arrow. As Rama continued to chant and pull the bow string toward His ears, the whole universe appeared to quake. Vengeful thunder and lightning struck the earth, and dark clouds began to cover the skies, and it was total darkness all of a sudden. Meteors began to shoot across the sky in all directions. Winds blew with so much rage that the trees, unable to generate enough strength, began to split through the middle to counter the force of the gusts. Unable to understand what was happening around them, various sea, land and sky creatures began to bellow in fear.52

Just then, right from the middle of the ocean, a massive surge gushed out like a fountain. It was mesmerizing to see the sudden and forceful ejection. Atop that mystical fountain was Varuna, the god of the ocean, glistening like a glossy emerald encased in ornate gold. His exquisite white shimmery pearl garland had a huge pendant that resembled the Kaustubha jewel on the chest of Lord Vishnu. A set of little waves constantly pirouetted around him. Soft clouds encircled his exceptionally handsome face and gentle winds blew his hair backwards. Escorting him were the personifications of river Ganga and the river Sindhu. Varuna appeared with folded hands joined together in veneration. He gazed at Rama with great awe, and the first word that escaped from his mouth and sounded like the rumbling of waves was “Rama!” He composed himself and continued, “My dear Rama, the elements are fixed in their nature and never change according to anyone’s desires. Earth, water, fire, air and ether have their own specific characteristics. The nature of the ocean is to be fathomless. It’s impossible to swim across the ocean. No living entity, however powerful, can cross it this way. To expect me to become shallow is aspiring for the impossible. That can never be. However, I empathize with your cause and am ever grateful to your ancestor, the great Sagara Maharaj who has given me so much of abundance and contributed to my growth. Thus, I have a suggestion to help you accomplish your cause. I will allot a section of the sea from this shore all the way to Lanka across which the vanara army can construct a bridge that I will fully support. I will ensure that the bridge sustains despite the constantly moving water on which it will be constructed. I will also ensure that all the dangerous sea animals do not disturb them in any way during both the construction and subsequent journey toward Lanka.”

Rama smiled. His anger having abated, He was happy that a clear plan to help them reach Sita soonest had emerged. Once the ocean would become cooperative, the obstacles to reach Lanka would disappear. But He still had one problem to resolve. He had already mounted the most powerful arrow on His bow and, once mounted, it had to be discharged. Obviously, there was no reason now to shoot at the ocean. But instead Rama asked the ocean god, “Do you have any particular use of this arrow that could annihilate any destructive force no matter how powerful?” Varuna immediately had a suggestion of putting that missile to very good use. He spoke up again, “Toward the north is a very holy place, Drumatulya. A gang of robbers now unfortunately inhabits this supremely holy place. Abhiras is their leader, and their deeds are scary because there is no sin they haven’t committed and there is no limit to their cruelty. They regularly bathe in the sea. Such is the amount of negative energy that emanates from their bodies that I am unable to even bear their touch. O most powerful Rama, fire this arrow toward them and eliminate these sinful rogues.”53

The next moment, an ear-piercing twang reverberated along the shore and a bright fiery arrow zipped off to land at the exact spot it was directed at, and it did exactly what it was supposed to do. The robber gang became extinct, and the place where the gang sat was now a huge crater. No one understood what exactly happened. Some saw a fiery missile fall from the sky onto the robber gang enjoying a round of drinks. Everything was up in smoke after that.54

The ocean god was so grateful that he went on to explain the bridge-building idea to Lord Rama. Pointing out to Nala, the ocean god said, “This vanara, Nala, is the son of Vishwakarma, the celestial architect. He has been blessed by his father to be equal to him in design and architectural astuteness. Immediately begin the construction of the bridge using his expertise. Rest assured, you have my complete support. I will hold that bridge together.”

Float-Not-Sink Curse

Here is another version of the bridge-building story. Nala and Nila were cursed in their childhood by the sages they would harass. As naughty and restless monkeys, Nala and Nila would trouble the sages by throwing their paraphernalia into the river. The sages were harassed but at the same time they hesitated inflicting a harsh curse on the children. They finally found a midway to solve their problem of losing stuff. They cursed Nala and Nila that whatever they threw into the river would float and not sink. That way they could retrieve them easily. So, essentially, whatever they would throw into water would float but not sink. That curse not only solved the problem of the sages, but also came handy when building the stone bridge over the ocean to Lanka.

The ocean god disappeared soon after. A small wave rolled onto the shore and lapped against Lord Rama’s feet. The entire vanara army moved back a step to allow a moment of bonding between Varuna and Lord Rama. Nala walked ahead to take up the challenge of building the bridge over the ocean. Rallying the vanara army, Nala began to deliver instructions on exact the type of stones and wood needed for the structure. The vanaras went out in many directions to gather the materials necessary. The vanaras worked tirelessly in small groups, and the collection process took all day. They brought thousands of stones and tree barks from all over, and by the end of the day, considerable material had been accumulated. While the vanaras were busy with collecting things, Vibhishan was busy with something he considered more important. He had left without informing anyone about his plan.

a bird’s eye view

A pigeon silently flew around the city of Lanka. Not that pigeons didn’t fly on other days, but this one seemed very sneaky. It had come with a purpose to Lanka and was quietly observing everything. Its target zone was Ravana’s palace. It managed to maneuver its way into the innermost section of the private discussion chamber of Ravana. The sight there was beyond what it could imagine. There is no time to waste now, nothing further to observe now. The exhausted pigeon flew 100 yojana across the ocean and landed at Lord Rama’s feet.

POOF!

There stood Vibhishan. Clearly he had changed his form to that of a pigeon. His face displayed a sense of urgency. All the leaders had gathered realizing the gravity of the situation. ‘O Rama! There is a grave danger to your life. Ravana has planned something that I was always apprehensive about. This, probably, will be our greatest test—much more complicated than anything we can fathom. We have to combine all our wits and be really united.” Those present wondered what his warnings really meant.

Lord Rama appeared very casual about the whole thing. He said in a very relaxed manner, “I have full confidence in your wisdom, Jambavan’s experience and Hanuman’s abilities. I am happy to cooperate with anything you feel should be done.” Lord Rama then walked away along with Lakshmana.55

The entire vanara army got into action. It initially seemed to be chaotic, but once everyone had settled down into their designated positions and their rhythms were synchronous, it was an evident strategy. The vanara army had been arranged in the form of a fort wall, with the center kept vacant and clear. That way, each person in the army was stationed as one part of the wall and all eyes were fixed at the center where Sugriva and Angad remained on guard. Once this was done, the execution of the second and more complex part of the plan began. Hanuman and Vibhishan were the only people stationed outside the wall. Hanuman began to expand his tail. His tail began coiling around the army wall. After one complete circle, the tail extended even further and began to make another layer on top of the previous one. Soon, the entire army was trapped within Hanuman’s tail wall. A two-layered fort was created. Only a small opening was left to enable entry and exit into and out of the improvised fort. Hanuman has personally guarded this entry.

When Lord Rama and Lakshmana returned from their stroll, they were surprised by the intelligence and dedication of the army. Vibhishan guided them into the fort wall. As soon as Lord Rama and Lakshmana entered, the entire inner wall of vanaras bowed down in respect and explained that this system ensured protection from the sides, but the skies were still unguarded. He requested Lord Rama to invoke the sudarshan chakra for protection from the skies. With one gaze of Lord Rama, the sudarshan chakra of Lord Vishnu came gliding in. The effulgent disc began making its rounds, guarding the fort from the skies. With the most obvious areas covered, Vibhishan explained to Rama that all this was just to make sure that He and Lakshmana got a good night’s sleep. Rama and Lakshmana laughed. This seemed to be the most complicated way to get good sleep.56

Their humor lightened up the situation. Vibhishan had strategically placed Sugriva and Angad at the center of the fort wall, so that even if someone managed to sabotage every protective layer to reach Rama, they would have two formidable superpowers to fight. Sugriva and Angad sat on the floor. Rama and Lakshmana slept on the ground, resting their heads on the laps of the two Vanara leaders.

Meanwhile, a dark shadow loomed, well hidden from the views of thousands of watchful eyes. It was the most important part of Ravana’s plan. In fact, it was Ravana’s last plan to avoid the war and deal with his enemy in a non-confrontational fashion. This was the king of illusion, Mahiravana, in action. He was Ravana’s friend from patalaloka, the subterranean world. Vibhishan had stumbled upon the confidential meeting between Mahiravana with Ravana in Lanka. Every demon in the world knew of Mahiravana’s illusory powers. He had, in fact, captured and imprisoned Mayadevi, an illusory energy herself.57

As soon as he saw the fort made out of the tail of Hanuman and the sudarshan chakra blazing on top, he knew that Vibhishan was at work here. But for him, Vibhishan had always been a child—a child with little foresight. Fort or no fort, Rama and Lakshmana would disappear in no time. Thinking thus, Mahiravana began to move toward the fort.58

As he walked, his form changed to that of King Dasaratha, the father of Lord Rama. He had noticed that Hanuman was stationed at the entry post, vigilantly guarding the access, while Vibhishan patrolled around. The morphed King Dasaratha made his entry as soon as Vibhishan had passed by Hanuman and turned around the corner.

“O vanara hero, I am King Dasaratha. I am here to meet my sons, Rama and Lakshmana. It’s been so many years now. I was unable to wait for their return to Ayodhya. I heard they are inside this tail fort. Would you be kind enough to permit me entry to be with my sons?” King Dasaratha’s words moved Hanuman, yet Vibhishan’s words “a master of illusion” began echoing in his head.

Not wanting to take any risk and at the same time not willing to be disrespectful to King Dasaratha, Hanuman chose a safe approach. “Let Vibhishan come. He is in charge of this fort. Once he permits, I will escort you in.”

Hearing these words, King Dasaratha excused himself, saying that he would be back by the time Vibhishan returned. When Vibhishan came back, Hanuman narrated the visit of King Dasaratha. Vibhishan immediately became alert and said, “Even if your father comes, don’t let him in unless I sanction it.” With this stern warning, he walked away, patrolling all directions. As soon as Vibhishan left, Bharata, Lord Rama’s brother came. He made the same plea, and Hanuman repeated the request. Bharata left in a hurry. When Vibhishan returned and was informed of the arrival of Bharata, he repeated the same instruction. After a while, Kaushalya, Lord Rama’s mother came and went, followed by Janaka, Sita’s father. On Hanuman’s refusal to admit entry, he began an argument with him. Just then, a loud roar was heard and Janaka suddenly scurried away.

Decoding Illusions

Mahiravana represents the cleverly packaged illusions and temptations of this world. Hanuman represents the confused mind that accepts everything as good. Vibhishan represents the keen intelligence that can see through the illusion. The vanara fortress represents the body. Rama and Lakshmana represent the soul within the body.

While illusion tries its best to gain entry through the confused mind, intelligence has to be vigilant to warn the mind time and again. One small lapse, and the mind could unwittingly allow an illusory thought or temptation to enter and ruin the peace of the resting soul.

Vibhishan emerged from the other end of the fort, smiling at his accomplishment and warned Hanuman again. He then left for another patrolling session. Again. After this experience, Hanuman decided to lock anyone who came until Vibhishan….

Before Hanuman could finish his thought, Vibhishan returned. He had only just gone, but how was he back so soon? He had barely turned the corner. On seeing Hanuman’s bewilderment, Vibhishan said, “O Hanuman, I have realized this night is going to be more complicated than I had estimated. Lord Rama and Lakshmana will need more protection than our watchful eyes can provide. I have a special holy thread I want to tie around their arms to protect them from evil. You had better be alert while I tie this on the brothers and return. Mahiravana is in full action now.”

Vibhishan then patted Hanuman on his back and entered the fort with a sly smile that left the gentle vanara confused. Hanuman couldn’t decode a thing. Everything was an illusion.

Once inside the fort, Vibhishan walked to the center, where the princes were resting. He opened his palms and placed them horizontally in front of his face and blew air from his mouth as he spun around. The fine pink dust shot out at great speed. Before anyone could even react, every vanara that stood guard dropped unconscious. The seated Sugriva and Angad didn’t even have the time to rise up before the dust hit their nostrils. The clamor of the vanara army collapsing with their weapons was clearly audible outside, and Hanuman panicked. He wanted to rush in, but he couldn’t abandon his post. Just then he saw Vibhishan return from his rounds!

What was happening here? Hadn’t he just entered the fort! If this was Vibhishan, then who was that? If that was Vibhishan, who is this? Hanuman grabbed Vibhishan by his shoulders and began to shake him violently. “If you are here, who was that who went in to tie the holy thread on Lord Rama’s arms? Who are you? Was all this your plan to capture Lord Rama and make Ravana victorious? You have acted as a well-wisher. You are after all a demon. Why have you done this?”59

Vibhishan’s protective instincts were now in overdrive and the only thing on his mind was Lord Rama’ welfare. As he tried to enter the fort, Hanuman pinned him down so forcefully that he couldn’t even move an inch. He tried to use all his convincing powers. “Hanuman, try to understand, I am the real Vibhishan. The one in there is Mahiravana. Lord Rama is in deep trouble. If I have deceived Lord Rama in any way, let me be held guilty of the sin of killing a brahmana or a cow.” Hanuman wasn’t impressed by the declaration. Why would a demon be bothered about the death of a brahmana or a cow? Realizing that Hanuman wasn’t buying his innocence, he spoke further trying to clarify. “Hanuman, think clearly. How can I be Ravana’s spy? This is not the time to doubt, but serve Lord Rama in cooperation. Lord Rama needs us now!”

Finding merit in Vibhishan’s words, Hanuman loosened his grip and remorse filled his heart. He had committed a major blunder. Not only had he allowed the wrong person in, he had also doubted the right person. Grasping the seriousness of the situation, Vibhishan entered the fort, and Hanuman followed shortly after recoiling his tail, and thus, dismantling the fort. It was agonizing to see every vanara comatose. Right at the center, Sugriva and Angad lay asleep with their legs crossed, indicating they didn’t even find the chance to stand up. The most painful and horrifying thing of all was the absence of Rama and Lakshmana. They were missing! Their resting place was now an endless tunnel. Vibhishan broke down. Through his loud wails all that could be heard was, “All hope is lost... all hope is lost.”

Although sad and dejected, Hanuman helped all the vanara soldiers to consciousness by sprinkling water on their faces. Soon, all the monkeys were crying at the loss of the very life they guarded. None could think clearly. Jambavan spoke first. “Let us send Hanuman in search of Lord Rama. There is no realm inaccessible to him.” Every vanara agreed that only Hanuman was the friend of the distressed. After receiving the blessings of everyone present, Hanuman dove headlong into the tunnel. For the initial few yojanas,60 it was a free fall, but beyond that a force began to suck him in. The speed of travel escalated beyond comprehension. It was too fast, even for the son of Vayu.61

Suddenly, he was thrown off, and he landed on the ground. The force was such that he rolled on for a while before he could regain balance. As soon as Hanuman realized what was about to happen to him, he took the shape of a regular monkey to withstand the fall and balance faster.

Looking around, he found himself in the magical city of patalaloka. He leapt from tree to tree and reached a beautiful lake. Many women come to fetch water. A group was gossiping animatedly. He began to eavesdrop.

“What’s going on in the palace today? Why do we hear music emanating from the inner sections of the palace? I have never seen so many brahmanas go in gleefully as if they were being rewarded. Could you please share the inside news?” an inquisitive girl asked an elderly lady working in the inner chambers of the palace.

“Shhhh…. This is highly confidential. Don’t dare tell anyone. There is going to be a special sacrifice in the evening to goddess Mahamaya. A human sacrifice! The king has kidnapped two extremely handsome human beings. I have never seen anyone with that kind of beauty in this life. Sad, they will be dead soon. Anyway, I must go now. Lots of special duties have been assigned today. Please make sure that you keep this information confidential until evening. Please, I earnestly request you. It’s a matter of my life.” Saying this, the elderly lady scampered away into the palace, hurriedly looking here and there, ensuring no one had eavesdropped on this conversation.

Hanuman realized he had to act fast. He was racing against time. He had no clue about the magical city. How would he know where Lord Rama and Lakshmana had been held hostage? Suddenly an idea walked out of the palace door! A team of armed guards trooped out with a middle-aged woman bound in iron chains. From her attire, she appeared to be part of royalty, but from the shackles, it was obvious she was a prisoner. The guards looked around to ensure that everything was safe. From the nervous look on the lady’s face, it was clear she didn’t like doing what she was being pushed to do. The entourage was walking toward the lakeside. That’s when Hanuman noticed something peculiar in one of the soldier’s hand. An earthen pot!

As they reached the lake, that soldier handed over the earthen pot to the lady. One of them came forward and unchained her. With her hands free, she turned toward the lake and squatted. She gently placed the pot on the banks of the lake and sat down with her hands folded. Hanuman took on a miniscule form and sat on the rim of the pot in front of her and observed her carefully. She had closed her eyes and was offering a prayer. Once done, she opened her eyes and muttered something under her breath. Of course, Hanuman’s sharp ears caught it. She said, “What a sad situation I am in? My brother is a completely useless demon. I can’t believe that he is going to sacrifice such handsome princes today. O goddess, please protect Lord Rama and Lakshmana. They don’t deserve to die such a miserable death.”

Instantly, Hanuman spoke, “Do you know where Lord Rama and Lakshmana are? Will you please help me find them?”

Initially, she couldn’t find the source of the voice. She looked around anxiously. She was shocked to see the tiny monkey on the rim of her pot. She looked at him with suspicion and said, “Are you Ravana in disguise; is this how you keep a watch on me?”

Hanuman spoke up mustering all his sincerity and innocence, “O Mother, please don’t doubt me. I am not any demon but a humble servant of Lord Rama. I am here to rescue him.”62

Hanuman’s reply gave her great joy—something that had gone out of her life. “Oh, I am so happy to hear this. If you can manage to rescue Lord Rama, I am almost sure that the life of my son and my own will also be saved. Although I am Duratandi, the sister of Mahiravana, I am a prisoner in my own home.”

“Mother, if you help me, I will help you.” Hanuman was happy to serve and realized that this unfortuante woman was in genuine need of help. He was curious to know what calamity could have landed on Mahiravana’s sister in this perplexing situation.

She revealed a stunning story. “Many years ago when my son, Nilamega, was born, an unembodied voice from the higher realms had declared that this boy would be the future king of patalaloka when Mahiravana dies. This news antagonized my brother so much that he imprisoned us that very day. My son has grown up and spent all his life in prison and hasn’t even experienced the outside world yet.”

Although this tale was definitely interesting, Hanuman wanted to know how the magical demon allowed her to venture out to fetch water today. She said, “I have a unique boon that if any ritual is performed using the water that I fetch, then, it would surely be a grand success. This water that I am carrying is to be used during the sacrificial ceremony of Lord Rama and Lakshmana.” As soon as Hanuman heard this, he grinned. He told her that he would hide beneath a mango leaf that she should set afloat on the water in the pot. She picked up the pot, filled it with water and put in the mango leaf. Hanuman clung on it as planned. With the pot on her head, she began to walk slowly toward the unsuspecting soldiers.

The first wall opened, and they entered the palace. The guards inside wanted to inspect the pot. They asked her to place it down. With trembling hands, Duratandi lowered the pot and the curious soldiers glanced inside. Carefully shifting the mango leaf aside, they inspected the contents of the pot. “Nothing! It’s just water.”

She was escorted straight up to the place where the sacrifice was to be made in a few hours. As soon as she placed the pot on the ground, a wasp flew out of it and began to buzz around her. The wasp flew all the way up to where the deity Mahamaya was and circled around for some time. Then it returned to Duratandi. She silently got up and walked toward her cell with the wasp following her closely. Soon, when she was locked up, Hanuman, the wasp, asked her about Mahiravana in detail and learned every secret she knew about him. Having gathered all valuable information, the wasp flew away. Hanuman flew around the prison, looking for the cell where Lord Rama and Lakshmana had been held captive. The moment he spotted it, tears welling up in his eyes. Without wasting another moment, he flew straight into the cell and fell at the feet of Lord Rama. When Lord Rama realized that it was His dear associate Hanuman, He was overjoyed. They quickly exchanged notes and Hanuman left. It was time for action now!

Hanuman then proceeded toward Mahiravana’s private chamber to have a good look at him before confronting him. But, surprise! He saw a part-monkey, part-crocodile guard patrolling the palace all alone. Since Hanuman was now very tiny wasp, there was no chance of being spotted. He was very confident of his disguise, so he tried to squeeze in through the slight gap at the bottom of the door to Mahiravana’s chambers. But the guard put his foot right in front of Hanuman, sealing the gap neatly. Oh no, I’m caught! Hanuman now saw no point in retaining his minuscule form so he returned to his usual, original self. The guard took a war-ready stance. Hanuman felt there was something familiar about his posture. “Who are you?” Hanuman asked the guard. The reply felt like a ear-numbing thunderclap to Hanuman. “I am Makharadwaja, the son of Hanuman.”

“What? How can you be my son? I never looked at a woman with lust, let alone marry and beget a child.” Hanuman was flabbergasted with this revelation.

“I was told that when Hanuman burned the city of Lanka, a drop of his sweat fell into the ocean and a crocodile consumed that. I was born from the belly of that very crocodile. So, you are my father. But, no matter how we are related, I am now Mahiravana’s guard and I cannot allow anyone to enter his abode without his sanction. So, please do not embarrass me by forcing me to defeat my own father in combat.” Makharadwaja was direct.

Hanuman didn’t wait another moment. Over the next few seconds, Makharadwaja was blindsided by the rapid onslaught of fists and legs. It did not take long for him to go from battle stance to extreme agony to face down on the ground, all tied up. In a flash, Hanuman had overwhelmed him, overpowered him and shackled him by tying up arms and legs and gagging him. After leaving his son crushed, Hanuman reassumed his minuscule form and flew right into Mahiravana’s unguarded chamber.63

A contemplative and clueless Mahiravana had no time to react to Hanuman’s sword assault, which left the netherworld king slashed right through the middle from head to toe. Mahiravana dropped dead. He was a bloody mess!

It’s done! Hanuman couldn’t believe that he had finished this powerful demon with just one stroke. He was happy that this ordeal was finally over. He stepped back to return to Lord Rama when he heard a weird sound. He turned just in time to see Mahiravana rushing toward him with a raised sword. Hanuman somersaulted in the nick of time and successfully evaded the brutal strike. Mahiravana lost his balance, tripped and fell headlong. The sword slipped from his hand. Hanuman grabbed the sword instantly, and began to chop Mahiravana mercilessly into pieces that splattered everywhere.

Satisfied this was the final episode in the book of this demon’s life, Hanuman began to find his way back. No sooner had Hanuman taken a few steps away from the bloody mess than he heard a sound again. He was back! How did this monster return from the dead every single time?

How was it possible that despite being cut into pieces, he was alive again? What I had heard about him is true after all! No matter how many times he is killed, he will return to life again. No point continuing this fight him in the regular style. Hanuman then assumed his tiny form again and escaped from the palace.64

As soon as Mahiravana understood that Hanuman had arrived to rescue Lord Rama and Lakshmana, he decided to re-schedule the sacrificial ritual. He ran out toward the sacrificial arena at the temple of Mahamaya and began to bark out instructions to his soldiers and servants. Everyone scampered to get things moving. They tied Rama and Lakshmana to poles. As soon as the two princes noticed such hectic activity much ahead of the stipulated time, they realized Hanuman had succeeded in the first phase of the plan and began hoping that he could make it back on time. Everything depended on his speed. While preparations were on in full swing, Hanuman reached a cave hundreds of yojanas away from the sacrificial arena. This was the cave Duratandi had told him about and he had reconfirmed from Mahamaya. He had an important mission in the cave, the consequence of which would determine the next course of events in Mahiravana’s palace.

This unique cave had five entrances. Hanuman had no time to think too much. He had a job to do. It doesn’t matter how many entrances there are, all I need do is blow out five flames. He ran into one of the entrances, reached the far end and blew out the blazing flame. He then rushed to the second entrance and did the same. One by one, he blew out all the five flames. It’s done! Hanuman was relieved. However, when he returned to the first entrance, the rear wall all lit up! The second, third, fourth and fifth entrances all lit up again. Not one to give up, he went about blowing out the flames all over again. But again, they all reignited. After several attempts, Hanuman was fed up.

He closed his eyes and invoked his father Vayu and sought his advice. The wind god revealed that all the five flames had to be extinguished simultaneously. Hanuman returned to his mission. He stood right in the middle of the five caves and closed his eyes. Instantly, four more heads grew out Hanuman’s shoulders— a ferocious lion, Garuda the eagle, a black boar and a horse. With these five heads, Hanuman roared fiercely. The roar was thunderous and its vibrations shook up the mountains. He not only blew out all the five flames at once but also the entire back wall of the cave. Hanuman rushed back. It was crunch time now!65

Meanwhile, Mahiravana untied Rama and Lakshmana, brought them down to the deity Mahamaya. After many incantations and mantras, he asked Rama to bend down and be ready to die. Rama feigned innocence. He told Mahiravana, “Since I am a prince and have been raised in a royal palace, I have no idea how to bend. Please show Me exactly how to bend and then it would be easy for Me to follow suit.” Mahiravana was disgusted at Lord Rama’s ignorance. Nonetheless, he agreed to demonstrate personally, since this was an important sacrifice for him.

He fell on his knees and placed his head on the wooden altar at the exact slot for the neck. Doing that, he closed his eyes to show Lord Rama how he should position himself. As soon as Mahiravana closed his eyes, Lord Rama picked up the sacrificial sword placed close by and beheaded Mahiravana. That very instant, Lakshmana ran across the temple room, climbed up a wall, broke open a door and smashed a diamond kept within the closed doors.

At exactly that time, Hanuman flew into the temple room. With all the three tasks accomplished simultaneously, Mahiravana’s head did not rejoin his body this time. He was finally dead!

The three heroes embraced each other. Suddenly, the whole building they were in began to vibrate, the floor started quaking. Something was wrong here…

Hanuman expanded himself. Lord Rama and Lakshmana sat on his muscular shoulders. He sped as fast as the wind, dodging falling pillars and walls. Somehow, he managed to exit before the palace caved in. Hanuman kept running as the catastrophe wasn’t over yet. The entire city was collapsing now. He began to run toward the hole through which he had entered patalaloka.

As soon as he found it, he jumped in, carrying the two brothers. Suddenly, they were sucked upward and expelled. Once they were out and safe, the tunnel had closed once and for all. When they opened their eyes, they saw thousands of happy faces. Home with friends and well-wishers, at last! The vanara army had a lot to ask and they had a lot to tell….66

## Chapter 4

Assumptions and Expectations

The night was eventful. The next day, the army plunged into serious action. The excitement about building a bridge was dampened by the lack of clarity. After the several hours-long attempt at laying the foundation of the bridge failed, the army’s morale plummeted.

The vanara army was morose. Despite all the brainstorming, all the leaders could not figure out a solution to this serious problem. Even Nala and Nila—the best minds in the entire vanara kingdom— couldn’t fathom a working solution. On His part, Lord Rama seemed least interested in helping them because He trusted their intelligence and capabilities. This added to the army’s responsibility. After a whole day of thinking, they were exhausted and utterly frustrated. Hanuman was feeling particularly helpless because this problem didn’t even concern him. Why would he worry about how to cross the ocean when he had only just flown to Lanka and returned? Yet, the question remained: How would the huge vanara army cross the ocean? Hanuman often wondered about Lord Rama’s need for such a huge army when He could destroy Lanka all by Himself and bring back Mother Sita. But he mustered the courage to ask Lord Rama this question because he knew that his Lord strongly believed n encouraging everyone to work together to achieve a goal.67

Everyone realized that this wasn’t the time to argue and overthink, but to find a tangible solution. Thousands of monkeys stood ashore, staring blankly at the vast expanse of water in front of them. Many began shedding helpless tears. All of them had done so much, including scanning the entire globe in a little over two months, to find Sita’s whereabouts. Now when they knew where She was, they couldn’t reach there. Hanuman could clearly read all their minds, and knew he had to solve their problem. But he couldn’t really do anything in this situation. During such despondent times, he always loudly chanted Lord Rama’s name, seeking His guidance to bestow intelligence on him. Today, along with his chant, he flung a stone along with his frustration into the water, as if to admonish the ocean for not making way.68

Unbelievable! How could this happen?

Hanuman was overjoyed and began jumping in the air. On seeing Hanuman’s excitement, the monkey army gathered around him. “See that? The stone I just threw into the water is floating!” Hanuman shared his amazement. So he took another stone and flung it into the water.

Sunk!

He kept throwing stones and invariably every stone sank. But the first stone that he’d thrown was still afloat. This gave the monkey leaders hope of a new possibility. All of them joined Hanuman in trying to decode the secret of the floating stone. They began throwing stones of all dimensions to ascertain what made that one stone float.

Hanuman realized that merely throwing stones wouldn’t help. He tried to recollect exactly what he was doing when he flung that stone into the water. Ah! That must be it! That must be the reason behind this miracle—Lord Rama’s name! To test his theory, Hanuman began to chant Lord Rama’s name as he threw a stone into the water. Lo and behold, this one floated, too! The vanara camp was euphoric, and all of them broke into a celebratory dance at this novel discovery. Soon each vanara began to chant Lord Rama’s name and tossing stones into the water, and, invariably, every stone floated. They had found the solution at last! All they had to do now was pile up enough stones to build a stone bridge across the ocean.

Construction began with gusto. While most monkeys were busy procuring large stones from far and wide, some supervised the construction and organized the placement of the stones. Lord Rama was impressed with the monkeys’ efficiency and was eager to assist. What intrigued Him the most was the mystery behind the floating stones. Although no one really wanted to embarrass Him by telling Him that it was the power of His name that made the stones float, Lord Rama had observed the monkeys chanting His name while throwing the stones. However, His humility stopped Him from attributing the chanting of His name to the floating. All Lord Rama wanted to do now was to help make the bridge across to Sita. Not wanting to hog the limelight, Lord Rama chose a quiet corner to do His bit for the project.

He decided to take a massive boulder and throw it into the ocean. To His utter disappointment, the stone sank. So He picked up another stone, but He followed the trend and threw it into the ocean while chanting His own name. The stone sank yet again, much to His disappointment. He was absolutely confused now, and, in fact, He was even a mildly embarrassed by His non-performance. Just then, a gentle voice startled Him. It was Hanuman. The loyal disciple was kneeling on one leg, his head bent low and his eyes closed. With folded hands he said, “My Lord, how do You expect the stone to float? Anyone or anything that You have rejected and thrown away is meant to sink. The rejected have no hope. When You decide to give up on someone, the possibility of liberation from the ocean of material existence ceases for that person. However, when we throw the stones taking Your holy name, the stones float in the hope that they can be of service to You and receive the grace of Your lotus feet. Kindly allow us to utilize these stones for You. Please don’t reject them and deny them an opportunity to serve You.” Lord Rama saw logic in Hanuman’s proposition and walked away from the construction site, realizing that some tasks were best left to His name.69

obstructer becomes enabler

Work was progressing at a very rapid rate, but something kept disrupting the bridge’s cementing process. The stones kept drifting apart from one another despite all efforts to keep them glued together. The vanaras’ enthusiasm waned when the stones refused to stick together. To investigate and reach the root of the problem, Hanuman dove into the ocean. A shoal of fishes was responsible for the stones becoming scattered. Hanuman shook his tail vigorously and thrashed the waters, paralyzing the fishes.70

Soon the leader of the shoal rushed to rescue the fishes. Hanuman was surprised to see a beautiful, golden mermaid. She introduced herself as Swarnamatsya—the golden mermaid—queen of the fishes. Hanuman demanded an explanation from her. She said, “Ravana had ordered me to disperse the stones with the help of my fishes.” By this time, she had had a good look at Hanuman and she fell for him. She spoke in a very seductive voice. “Who are you? You seem to be strong, handsome and intelligent. What have you got to do with this war between Rama and Ravana? Marry me and enjoy life to the fullest. Together we will rule the ocean, unaffected by the worries of the world above!”

Hanuman’s reply was stern. “Of what use is my strength, beauty and wisdom if they are useless to others? The one who uses one’s abilities only for one’s growth is a fool. I live for my master Rama. I have no life apart from Him.”71

Hanuman declined her offer and rose to the surface. The fish queen was so impressed with Hanuman’s selflessness that she ordered all her creatures to help him build the bridge. The fishes, serpents, seals and sea monsters held the stones together and the bridge to Lanka started to take shape.

monkeys, squirrels, rocks and pebbles

Over the last two days, the floating stone path had finally begun to look like a bridge across the ocean. Considerable distance had been covered, and the vanaras were working nonstop, fetching rocks from all nearby mountainous areas. Each did the most that his capacity and strength allowed. The vanaras were now transporting rocks of various kinds. While some carried huge boulders, several big rocks at once or even mountains filled with rocks, others carried many small rocks at a time. Some leaped a few miles into the mountains not so far away to get more rocks, whereas some leaped hundreds of miles to mountains far away.72

As many were engaged in fetching rocks and stones, an entire division was focusing on trees. This division procured trees of species—sala, asvakarna, karnika, dhava, bamboo, kutaja, arjuna, tinisa, bilva, saptaparna, mango, and ashoka. Some uprooted the trees and brought them to the construction site. The roots were still visible! Other vanaras brought in other trees, such as palmyra, pomegranate, coconut, vibhitaka, karira, bakula and neem trees. The vanaras who could not carry these huge trees collaborated to make mechanical devices to transport these trees. The entire shore was now like the massive warehouse for the vanaras to offload building material. When the vanaras tossed these on the shore or into the ocean, loud sounds reverberated across the distance.

The last phase required acquiring and making ropes using sea reeds and weeds to tie up all the rocks and trees in a way that they stayed in place and together despite the constant movement of the ocean waters. It took quite a while to find these ropes because these had to be of a particular size: long enough to cover a distance of 800 miles and wide enough for 80 miles. Although the bridge was being constructed over such a large distance and the communication between the builder monkeys was likely to be intense and complicated, they managed perfectly because of their enthusiasm and spirit of cooperation. The vanara sena submerged itself in the massive bridge construction project completely.73

Nala helmed the project, looking after both construction and design. He knew exactly what fit where. Although the stones floated because of their magic chants, Nala used his expertise to ensure that the stones stayed tied together to form a sturdy, continuous bridge rather than unconnected random floating stones. In fact Nala arranged for various layers of sand, tree barks, larger stones and gap-sealing smaller stones to be placed below the floating stones.

When all of this was done, Hanuman realized that he had a major problem. Well, perhaps it wasn’t really so major and was a very minor but irritating one for him. A group of eager squirrels had seemed to really connect with Lord Rama. These squirrels wanted to contribute to this project in some way, too. Obviously, they couldn’t carry big or small stones, but they could lug a pebble or two. So some of them found a unique way to do more than transporting the one or two pebbles they could. They jumped into the water, wet their fur and rolled on the sand vigorously. When a good amount of sand stuck to their fur, they ran toward the bridge, reached the right spot and shook off all the sand, depositing safely in the gaps between smaller stones and becoming the final filler in Nala’s bridge project.

Their presence was both comical and encouraging to the vanaras, but simply a nuisance to Hanuman. Every time he returned with massive mountains, many squirrels would almost get crushed under his huge feet. Often he would bend over and brush them off with his hands to keep them away from his path. But on this particular occasion, he was so irritated with them that as he was about to brush them off with his foot as his hands were occupied, Lord Rama saw him. The moment Hanuman realized being seen, he knew a lesson would follow. As Hanuman expected, Lord Rama called him aside. Hanuman sheepishly followed Him.

Rama asked Hanuman, “Do you know why we are building the bridge?” Hanuman knew the purpose: for the vanara army to cross the ocean. Rama spoke to Hanuman. “I could dry up the ocean if I wanted to, you know. If you could cross the ocean by taking My name, then surely I could empower the other vanaras to cross the ocean similarly.” Hanuman then said, “The real reason for the building of the bridge is to reach Lanka and kill Ravana.” But Rama countered this by saying, “If I chose, I could kill Ravana this very moment by stopping his heartbeat. He would die instantly.” Hanuman had run out of ideas at this point. “Lord, could You explain the reason behind such a herculean effort?” Rama smiled. He was waiting for this opportunity to place His point across. “The bridge is being built to engage everyone in a service that purifies the soul. Just as all the vanaras need engagement and service, squirrels too aspire to contribute in some small way. To everyone, the service of the vanaras may appear more valuable and the service of the squirrels might seem trivial. But in the eyes of the Supreme Lord, the service of both is exactly the same. No one’s service is really small or big. Everyone should contribute according to one’s capacity and never undermine anyone else’s service. One should focus on the purity of intention to serve rather than the capacity to serve.”74

Rama then walked up to the squirrels who were bundled up together in one corner, quite aware of the bother they had been to the great Hanuman. Lord Rama sat on His hunches and extended His hand and rubbed the back of the leader of squirrels. Suddenly, three lines appeared on the back of the squirrel where Lord Rama had run His fingers. From then on, every squirrel has been born with these three lines on their backs to commemorate their service on that day.75

stones, boulders and a special mountain

The southern part of the land was covered. Every major mountain, hill and rocky terrain had been explored. The construction of a 100-yojana long and 10-yojana wide stone bridge wasn’t that easy. They had now run out of stones. Since only Hanuman could venture far north to bring the huge stones available in abundance there, he began making north–south trips several times a day.

After innumerable trips, he was tired of flying. He decided enough was enough. This trip, he wasn’t going to bring boulders but pluck out an entire hill instead. The project was already in its final phase, and if he could just bring a big-enough mountain, the other monkeys wouldn’t need to take more trouble. With this in mind, he flew far north toward the Himalayas. After scanning the landscape for a while, he found just what he was looking for—the most magnificent hill he had ever seen. All his life, he had spent on different hills and mountains. Most had similar terrains, but this was different, almost out of this world. Its natural beauty was exquisite. It had beautiful lakes and even the trees seemed to be from the spiritual realms. As soon as Hanuman set his eyes on the hill, it started throbbing and bouncing, as if, expressing its eagerness to travel with Hanuman.

Hanuman pulled out the entire mountain, placed it on his palm and took off. As he was flying southwards, a monkey sent by Nala intercepted him. “The bridge is complete. We do not need more rocks. You are required to report back.” On hearing the instruction, Hanuman immediately began his descent to place the hill on the land above which he was hovering. Suddenly he felt wet from drops of water raining on him. However, unlike rain, these drops were hot.

Hanuman landed to place down the mighty hill below and observe it. That’s when he realized that the hill had been shedding tears and seemed very disturbed. Hanuman was concerned. “You seem to be a special hill with emotions and love. May I know who you are? Why you are weeping?’

The hill bellowed. “I am Govardhan, the King of Hills. I was eagerly waiting for an opportunity to serve Lord Rama. I have been sent to this world only to assist in the Lord’s pastimes. I was looking forward to fulfilling my destiny when it occurred to me that the Lord doesn’t need my service anymore. I am totally disgusted with myself. How can I live after knowing that the Lord doesn’t need me anymore?’

Hanuman was so touched by Govardhan’s devotion that he decided to personally appeal to the Lord. Hanuman pacified the mountain. “I understand your predicament. I assure you the Lord loves all His devotees.” Hanuman then flew back to the construction site. He noticed that construction was over and the bridge was now operational. The vanara sena was busy packing up to begin their siege of Lanka. Hanuman noticed Lord Rama seated alone in a quiet part of the shore, longingly looking toward Lanka where his beloved was captive. Hanuman approached him in all humility.

Hanuman narrated his unique experience with this hill named Govardhan. “My Lord. Govardhan’s devotion is deep. I request You to consider this hill as a special case and bestow Your mercy.” On hearing of the hill’s eagerness to serve him, Lord Rama said to Hanuman, “In My next incarnation as Krishna, I would bestow My blessings to this hill for its devotion. In fact, I would spend most of My eleven years of stay in Vrindavan on the Govardhan Hill itself. Not just that, since Govardhan has such sincerity and a personal touch with Me, as Krishna, I will carry the hill on My finger for seven consecutive days and nights.”

A happy Hanuman conveyed Lord Rama’s message to a happier Govardhan.. 76

stepping in the war zone

The historical 800-mile-long bridge over the ocean was named Nala Setu by the vanaras to laud Nala’s architectural astuteness. The entire bridge took only five days to complete. On the first day itself, the vanaras managed to cover 112 miles, while on the second day they beat their first-day record and achieved 160 miles. On the third day, they pushed themselves further to achieve another 168 miles cover, whereas on the fourth day, they were even more productive at 176 miles. Finally, on the fifth day, they completed 184 miles and laid the final stone that touched the shore of Lanka. When the 800-mile long and 80-mile wide bridge was completed, the construction team, which laid the final stone, began to celebrate on the bridge itself. Some of the ecstatic vanaras jumped into the ocean and enjoyed a swim. Some jumped into the air and began kissing their tails in bliss. They couldn’t contain their emotions on being able to pull off such a mammoth task in such a short time. One vanara ran across the fully completed bridge with a victory flag in his hand, making full-throated cries of joy to inform the others on the other side. A group of really enthusiastic and happy dolphins were frolicking on either side of the bridge. When the flag bearer reached the other side, the entire vanara army broke into a jig. Their untiring efforts had finally paid off, and they were now fully ready to embark on this monumental journey across the ocean. For many who lived in land-locked homes, this would be the first time they would cross an ocean as big as this one. The excitement was palpable.77

Rama was in tears seeing the crazily delighted flag bearer. His Sita was now just a bridge away. When He placed His first step on the bridge, He felt closer to Her already. The ocean swelled in pride as soon as Rama set foot on the bridge. The vanaras could feel the ocean’s happy vibes as they watched it change mood from sober to euphoric. Colorful fishes that would never be seen on the surface began to flip and jump. All sea creatures big and small rose to the surface. Crocodiles, tortoises, mahamatsya timi, timingal, conches and oysters rose to the surface.
