There were so many of these aquatic creatures packed so closely and so immobile that they seemed like a part of the bridge. They thus increased the size of the bridge manifold.

Large conches emerged from the depths and plopped onto the stone bridge. The vanaras picked up these conches and began to blow them. The reverberations sent joy rippling through the vanara ranks.

The entire vanara sena followed Rama and Lakshmana on to the stone bridge. Millions of vanara heroes followed their leaders Sugriva, Angad, Jambavan and Hanuman on the inaugural walk over the bridge. The devatas, gandharvas, kinnaras, apsaras, siddhas and caranas thronged the skies. They wanted to catch a glimpse of the bridge and participate in the joy of Rama’s historic march toward Lanka. They had all been patiently waiting for this for decades since Lord Vishnu had promised to eliminate Ravana and his brute rakshasa hordes that had wreaked havoc across the universe with their tyranny and recklessness. Adding in drumbeats, the Gandharvas augmented the tempo of the excited vanara forces. The vanara heroes were itching for a good fight now. The hours of walk over the 800-mile-long bridge was filled with excitement and adventure. The vanaras were alternatively taking long and short leaps even as they shouted out in glee and excitement.78

Lakshmana was on Angad’s shoulder and Rama on Hanuman’s. Rama had specifically instructed Hanuman to be the last one to step off the bridge. The vanaras ran ahead knowing that Rama was behind so that they could reach earlier and prepare for His grand arrival. Vibhishan and his four men took the airways and reached the other shore sooner to prepare for Rama’s welcome into their land for the first time. The vanara army rushed across the bridge at great speed. The bridge was choc-a-bloc with so many vanara soldiers, but it didn’t give away. Varuna had kept his promise to Rama. The bridge held on and held steady. Of course, not all vanaras walked over the bridge. Some swam, some flew and some leaped. As soon as Hanuman stepped off the bridge with Lord Rama on his shoulders and reached the other side, all aquatic animals on the surface went underwater again. The aquatic extension of the bridge disappeared just as quickly as it had appeared. The vanaras were surprised by the perfection of the arrangement.

When the army crossed over partly using the stone bridge and partly the aquatically bridge, Jambavan could not stop himself. He asked Lord Rama, “Would it be possible for You to build such a wonderful aquatically formulated bridge so easily? Why did You get the vanaras to build the stone bridge and put in such herculean effort?” Lord Rama replied, “Had you all not made this bridge of stone, where would I have stood and built this new bridge?”

As the Sita-rescue army prepared to step off the bridge, the welcome party comprising Nila, Nala, a small group of wonder-monkeys and Vibhishan’s men waited on the other shore. For quite some time, all they could see was an empty stone bridge amid the ocean. Then all of a sudden, they heard conches, roars and hooting, followed by the cacophony caused by vanaras of all sizes, shapes, colors and appearances moving at great pace in the water, sky and the floating mass of a bridge. Right at the helm were the heroes Rama and Lakshmana mounted on Hanuman and Angad. As soon as they stepped on Lanka’s shore, Vibhishan bowed to them and welcomed the brothers with a beautiful garland of fragrant flowers into his kingdom. He had also arranged truckloads of fruits for the famished vanaras who had been working tirelessly and without a break for the last five days. The wonder-monkeys were all too happy to devour the fruits.

enemy’s knock on the door

He was petrified of going back again. Every limb ached even now. The soreness of the wounds afflicted on him by the merciless vanaras still hurt his soul. Shukha was scared to his bone. But Ravana couldn’t care less about another’s feelings. All he cared for was information. Shukha was his best spy, and there was no one else he could trust as much. Just to make it easier for the perturbed Shukha, Ravana sent along Sharana. Sharana was a superlative spy, second only to Shukha in his discerning abilities. Together the invincible duo had amassed critical information over the several battles and wars that were crucial to Ravana’s victory.79

Shukha was anxious, but the presence of his friend and adventure partner gave him enough courage to disguise himself as a vanara and sneak into the hectic camp. The two disguised friends began to move through the vanara ranks and gather key information their master had demanded. They began by counting. They had great expertise in this, but this time it was a pain. Usually rakshasa and human soldiers were predictable and orderly, which made the counting easy. But the vanaras were all over the place—some on treetops, some over rocks and mountain peaks, some in the ocean, some still arriving. The bridge was still full of countless vanaras. How can we even count these many vanaras? They pushed aside the disturbing thought and tried all they could, but eventually gave up and decided to focus on the strengths and weaknesses of the vanaras. They began to single out the most powerful among them, the ones they thought capable of putting up a tough fight. Even that count was mind-boggling. They then began to list out the names of vanara chiefs who commanded large phalanxes. They even gathered subtle pieces of information such as the names of vanara chiefs Rama and Sugriva favored more. As they walked down the bridge, they marveled at the architectural brilliance of a bridge over the ocean, something they considered a technically impossibility. The more they studied, the lesser they understood it. The buoyancy of the rocks just didn’t make sense. They even walked a few miles into the ocean to test the rocks right in the middle of the ocean. It was all the same. The bridge defied every law of nature. Shukha and Sharana intuitively realized that Ravana had met his match. These vanaras were unique. To be able to rally such toughened and unruly beasts into a mission spoke much about Rama’s ability as a leader.80

Just as they were returning from their bridge analysis, they were surrounded by a group of powerful vanaras in combat posture. The spies immediately realized that they had been exposed and cornered. A stern Vibhishan walked into the cordoned area with a frown. Shukha shrunk with embarrassment. This was his second bust-up within a week. Nothing really escaped Vibhishan’s trained eyes. He caught hold of the two spies by their necks and dragged them to Rama. He didn’t want to let them be left alone with the vanaras lest they beat them to pulp at the first given opportunity. Shukha whispered to Vibhishan, “Thank you for doing what you did.” He could see the disappointment of a missed opportunity in the eyes of the growling and glaring vanaras. By this time, the two spies shed their disguise and assumed their rakshasa forms. There was no benefit in retaining their disguises now.81

Dropping them in front of Rama, Vibhishan said, “Here, my Lord, are the best of Ravana’s spies.” As soon as Vibhishan said that, Sugriva’s stance changed from peaceful to aggressive. Now surrounded by the most formidable vanara chiefs, Shukha and Sharana spoke in unison. “O great Rama, please spare us. We are harmless spies sent by King Ravana to check on the strengths of Your army.”

Rama smiled. That smile was like a breath of fresh air for the two spies. They had been suffocating in the panic and anxiety following their arrest. In a voice that allayed their fears, Rama spoke with great compassion and kindness. “My dear friends, have you completed your task yet? Do you have all the information you need? Or is there something left to survey? If there’s still some inspection and analysis left, please continue with what you have do. In fact, if you need any specific information you haven’t been able to procure and need help finding, Vibhishan will gladly assist you. He can show you around the whole camp and introduce you to all facets of the army. He will even share with you the smallest of details that you are perhaps unaware of but would need to submit in your final report to your king. You may have been caught spying, but you need not fear. Since you hold no weapons, you pose no threat to the army. We do not believe in harming the unarmed.”82

Rama’s balanced and confident approach really impressed the two spies. They had seen many enemy kings in the past, but never one as cool and calm as Rama. He brought dignity even to enmity. As they stood admiring their enemy king, Rama continued to speak. “When you go back and meet your king, please deliver this message faithfully and as is. ‘O Ravana, now will begin testing times for your brute forces. The same power on which you had so much faith and because of which you dared to kidnap My wife is going to be tested. Tomorrow, at daybreak, My arrows will pierce through every security measure of Lanka and cause havoc. Tomorrow, you will witness My anger.’”

Shukha and Sharana could not help but admire Rama’s courage. In fact, they were so inspired by Lord Rama’s charisma that they spontaneously shouted out a victory cry heralding the triumph of the mighty hero they had just witnessed in action. Happy to have been saved from any vanara battering, the two spies rushed toward the palace of their master. A lot needed to be reported. A lot needed to be done.

They found Ravana standing on the terrace of his palace, scanning the vanara army sprawled across the entire shore of Lanka. Trikuta Mountain, on which Ravana’s palace was situated, offered a panoramic view of the whole island. As Ravana tried to assess the just-arrived and currently settling vanara army, the two spies made their entry.

The two spies updated Ravana on every detail about their visit and their observations about the vanara army’s strengths. The fact that they couldn’t spell out a single weakness in the enemy thoroughly disturbed Ravana. Worse still, his spies eulogized his enemy in ways he could not fathom or accept. They had only spent a few hours in the enemy camp, but they were glorifying the enemy as if they belonged there. Their description of Rama’s greatness unsettled Ravana beyond his imagination. The spies ended their exaltation with this advice: “We recommend that you return Sita to not only avoid the brewing conflict but also, more importantly, save yourself from the embarrassment following your certain defeat. We strongly feel that it would be the wisest decision under the circumstances.”

A Formidable Foe

Ravana had assumed that someone who took a whole ten months to find where His wife was incapable of waging a war. When Hanuman arrived and burned Lanka, it rattled Ravana a bit but he calmed his nerves by finding fault in Hanuman. He considered the burning of Lanka a freak accident. This way, he hid behind his own interpretation and justification of every event that had taken place. When he heard from his spies that Rama was sitting on the other side of the ocean on a three-day long fast trying to please the ocean god, he laughed out loud. Such an insecure person could never wage a war, leave alone win one.

No one ever really understands how Rama functions. Ravana tried to evaluate Rama with his foolish parameters, and that was his biggest folly. He could never imagine anyone could construct a bridge over the ocean. So when he heard about the floating bridge, he was shocked and distraught. Fear finally began to take root in his mind: Could I really take the one who built a bridge across the ocean with the help of restless monkeys lightly?

The spies were anticipating an onslaught, but, for a change, Ravana smiled. From his terrace, he had seen vanaras catching and dragging them. This outburst is just a result of that traumatic experience. He thought to himself and decided to give them the benefit of doubt. “I have no intention of returning Sita even if all the gods came down to fight with me.” Ravana told the spies. He was emphatic and unwavering. He then turned around and walked toward the outer most section of the terrace, facing the vanara camp.

Anticipating what their master wanted, Shukha and Sharana tagged along. Standing on either side of Ravana, the two spies began to identify the heroes of the vanara army. Ravana trained his monocular on the army and at the vanara chiefs Shukha and Sharana pointed as they described their features, strengths and the army size they commanded. They explained every tiny detail of each phalanx’s specialization and expertise.

Ravana was fascinated that mere monkeys could be so well organized and well trained in the military arts. His knew his men had the advantage of highly sophisticated weapons and combat vehicles at their disposal, but these monkeys had raw energy and strength and an inspirational cause. Shukha and Sharana not only outlined the vanara leaders’ strengths, but also narrated many stories of their heroism. The spies’ uninterrupted and rapid supply of numbers and volumes of data overwhelmed Ravana.83

As they were scrutinizing the camp, Ravana noticed a very distinct activity in the vanara army. He saw a huge section of the army move in the direction of Mount Suvela. He realized that an entire division was racing up the mountain. He turned his device toward Mount Suvela peak. All he could see was an empty mountain peak, but all of a sudden his eyes caught a movement. He zoomed in and saw something dazzling. For the first time, he saw a handsome human face rising from behind the peak just like the sun rising over a mountain. Slowly, he could see more of the face, and he was forced to admire the beauty of that persona. Within the next few seconds, he could see the silhouette of the entire body. That human was seated on the shoulder of the very vanara that had ravaged Lanka just a few days back. One word inadvertently escaped from his mouth: “RAMA!”84

Rama had finally ascended the Suvela mountain that served as the entry point into Lanka. Suvela was the vantage point from where the vanara army could observe the whole of Lanka and strategize their attack on the city sprawled in front of them beyond the thick forest cover. Just behind Hanuman was Angad, carrying Lakshmana on his shoulder. Once the vanara heroes reached the summit, they helped Rama and Lakshmana down to their feet on the mountain. Ravana was carefully observing the two impressive brothers. He studied their persona, their sturdy bodies, their bows, their confidence and their stoic dealings with the restless monkeys. Ravana then moved his monocular to take a closer look at the other vanara heroes speeding up the mountain. Each appeared herculean, with chiseled, rippling muscles. Ravana studied each vanara hero intently. Suddenly, he felt something strange trickling down his head. He noticed his hair was wet.

Ravana touched his hair to feel the wetness. It wasn’t water for sure. The liquid was stickier. Wondering what it could be, Ravana looked up to the sky to search for the source of the dropping. Way up, he spotted a speck hovering above him. Ravana was aghast and furious at his discovery. How on earth? A vanara who had taken a tiny form was flying over his head and dropping saliva on him. That wetness on his hair was the spit of that puny monkey. How dare a stupid monkey spit at the king of Lanka? Ravana was beyond enraged. He was angrier than anger could handle. He lunged and caught that tiny monkey in his hand. The vanara began to wriggle to escape from the choking grip. Ravana brought the insignificant creature closer to his face and glowered at the impudent monkey. It was Sugriva! The brother of Vali and the leader of the vanara tribe. In all his fury, Ravana could just gobble him up right then and there. But the complete lack of fear on the captured monkey leader’s face infuriated Ravana even more.85

Sugriva was angry, too. He shouted out. “You foolish Ravana! I am a friend and servant of Lord Rama. No war is really needed to finish you off. I am enough to put an end to your tyranny. By the grace of Rama, I am good enough to eliminate you.”

Ravana laughed at the trapped and ignorant monkey’s audacious blabber. He suddenly thought of a little joke, that too at such a serious juncture. He had to tell it. “Your name, Sugriva, means one with a beautiful neck. But from now on, everyone will call you Hinagriva, which means one with no neck.”

Ravana found his own joke hilarious. Suddenly, he stiffened and said, “Right now I am going to snap your neck and rip it apart from your body.”

With great force, Ravana hurled Sugirva at the terrace floor in an attempt to smash him and kill him instantly. But he was clueless about the agility and speed of the wonder-monkeys. So he was taken aback when Sugriva bounced off the floor and jumped up into the sky. Returning to his regular size, Sugriva rebounded and slapped Ravana on his face throwing him off balance and sending him tumbling on the floor. Shukha and Sharana stepped aside knowing well that this was going to be an intense duel. Extremely insulted at having been humiliated in front of his own men and in his own palace by a mere monkey, Ravana was blistering with rage. As Ravana stood up with his hand smacked on the floor, a crack ran through the length of the terrace. He lunged at Sugriva. Two huge bodies collided. Sugriva slipped from the force of Ravana’s dive. His back hit the terrace railing. The weight and force of the two men grappling tore down the railing and sent the two hulks hurtling down to a floor below. That fall caused a crack on the floor. Ravana began to pound at Sugriva with his closed fists. After being at the receiving end of ten or so hard blows, Sugriva tossed Ravana aside and stood up. Acting quickly, he clenched both his hands together and struck Ravana on his back. The blow was so hard that Ravana collapsed face down on the floor. Although bleeding profusely, Sugriva’s satisfied grin flaunted the damage he had caused.

But the very next moment, Ravana stood up and pounced on him at such great speed that Sugriva didn’t even have a chance to react. Ravana kicked him hard on his chest and flung him to a great distance. Sugriva crashed against a metal gate that bent and snapped instantly. Not one to give up that easily, Sugriva ran toward Ravana with all his might and both the giants collided midair. Wrestling, hitting, tearing into each other’s flesh, the enemies began to rip each other apart. To the onlookers, at times it seemed like giant tuskers ramming against each other and at other times it appeared as if two wild cats were wrestling for a piece of meat.

Realizing that the fight wasn’t going to end so soon if he continued in the normal course, Ravana invoked the mystical powers that gave him great advantage over his enemies. The sun was setting rapidly and with the setting of the sun, Ravana knew his competency would enhance million-fold. When Sugriva saw Ravana closing his eyes in the middle of a fight and chanting mantras, he immediately knew this was no longer a fair fight. So even before Ravana could open his eyes, Sugriva leaped out of the terrace and disappeared into the darkness.86

## Chapter 5

Desperation and Misdirection

“Why would you do this?” Sugriva’s spontaneity upset Rama. That one freak moment could have been fatal. For the first time in this mission, Rama repented having restless monkeys as his aides. Such irresponsibility from the king of the entire vanara race was not only extremely disconcerting but also of grave concern. Rama embraced Sugriva, gently touching his wounds. For the few moments that Sugriva was fighting Ravana so furiously, Rama had almost forgotten to breathe. That was the last thing anyone would have expected a king to do.

The vanaras were, however, applauding their king’s daring. Amazing! Their king had not only spat at Ravana—at his own palace and in the presence of his own guards—but also beat him black and blue. While the fight had been going on in the distance on the palace atop the Tri-kuta mountain, the vanaras had been hooting and cheering for their leader, but Rama had been anxious. When the wounded yet beaming Sugriva landed back at Suvela, his friends and fans ran to greet him, but Rama pulled him aside to give him a piece of his mind. What the monkeys called heroism, Rama saw as sheer foolishness.87

“Without consulting Me, why did you carry out this reckless act? Courage does not mean foolhardiness. To win wars, courage alone is not enough. You need presence of mind, too. As a warrior, if you want to be gallant, then do it when the war begins, not like this. Do you understand how much anxiety you have caused all of us? Do not act like this in the future. If something had gone wrong during your misadventure, do you even understand how much pain I would have gone through? Even if I have Lakshmana, Sita, Bharata or Shatrughna by My side, losing you would have been unbearably painful. Had you not come back, I would surely have killed Ravana and the entire rakshasa force out of great anger but then I would not have returned to Ayodhya. Instead, I would have gone away into the wilderness never to return.” Rama gently patted Sugriva’s head.

When the vanara heroes saw the spontaneous affection bereft of selfishness and insecurities between their leaders, they felt fortunate to witness a very rare form of leadership, where leaders genuinely cared for each other. The most affected was Sugriva. He couldn’t fathom the love Rama had for him as Rama did not demonstrate that love so openly. But on rare occasions such as this, everyone got to see the depth of His emotions.

Sugriva was grateful to his own monkey mind for having goaded him to jump at Ravana. Had he not done that, he wouldn’t have had the chance to see the depth of Rama’s love for him. Of course, he didn’t dare share this thought with Rama. Instead, Sugriva justified his action. “The first sight of Ravana, the cruel-hearted demon abductor of Mother Sita, made me feel hurt and angry at the same. I had to douse his anger. So I did what I did.”88

Rama realized that every vanara in the army was equally incensed and motivated. This wasn’t the time to tell them to hold themselves but rather unleash that pent up anger on the enemy in the most powerful way. He turned around and began to descend from the Suvela mountain toward the waiting army. Only a section of the army had ascended the mountain along with the leaders to inspect the route they were to take to enter Lanka.

Having seen enough, Rama walked back to the army. Time had come to make their move. If they were to attack the next morning, the divisions of the army would need to be moved to various strategic locations to steer a multipronged incursion into the demon city. Now that their leader had been seriously hurt—not so much his body as much as his ego—by Sugriva, this would be the best time to strategize.89

Half way down the mountain, Rama stopped and looked intently at the army. He raised his hand in a show of power and readiness. All the vanaras standing in the ocean and on the shore for kilometers ahead both on the beach as well as on the bridge raised their hand in unison roared and shouted at the top of their voices.

The effect was so powerful that it traveled across the waters to shatter the hearts of the Lankans who had never heard anything this loud and powerful before. Even the walls of their houses reverberated.90

Without wasting much time, the vanara leaders under the supervision of Lakshmana, Sugriva, Hanuman and Jambavan began to divide the army and assign them to various strategic locations beyond the Suvela mountain. As soon as a divisional leader was assigned his position, he would rally his own battalion and plan the coordinated march in that direction. The movement was in such synchrony that it seemed really sharp and precise for a band of restless vanaras function.

The massive rearrangement was carried out under the cover of darkness—the movements quiet and brisk, each vanara fully aware of and alert to requirements. Not a word was breathed….

devious trick

His ego was colossally dented—a dent no amount of flattery could repair, a dent that had permanently perforated his self-belief. Even when he walked around his palace, Ravana was extremely conscious that his pummeled ego was evident to his people. When the palace women stared at him, he could sense disgust. Or is it pity? He could tell it was not positive. How could I let a monkey humiliate me to such an extent? This was the second time in a matter of weeks that a monkey that invaded his privacy and shredded his pride. He had to find a way to stifle the constant chatter of noises in his head that were heaping insults on him. He had to convince himself that he was still in control.91

He suddenly knew how—summoning the magician and sorcerer Vidyujjihva par excellence. Ravana whispered his plan in the wizard’s ears. The trickster was on board. He was confident of his wizardry. With his plan set in motion, Ravana upped and walked out of the palace on to the path he had taken a million times. Pain and pleasure at the sight of the gates of the newly reconstructed Ashok vatika was excruciating and simultaneous—pain because he knew things were no longer the same and pleasure because Vishwakarma had recreated his favorite garden despite the massive mess the monkey left behind. But, he knew that no amount of artificial effort would bring back the freshness and glory of a natural garden. Besides, every time Ravana entered Ashok vatika, he could see the fixed subtle smile on Sita’s face, almost as if She was controlling her laugh. She had been witness to it all. And Ravana wasn’t happy about it. But what he was about to do now would wipe that arrogant smirk out of Her face permanently.92

Dismissing the formal acknowledgments and greetings of all rakshasi women along the way, Ravana zoned in on Sita, who was seated under the simshupa tree on the raised platform. Placing one foot on the raised platform and his right hand on his knee, Ravana spoke to Her. “I have bad news for you, my dear. Although the vanara army managed to reach the shores of Lanka by building a large stone bridge across the ocean, Your Rama has been killed even before the battle has begun. While the whole vanara army settled down for the night and slept, my spies tracked them carefully. Once they had confirmation that everyone had retired for the day, they alerted the whole rakshasa army. Prahasta, the commander in chief, raided the sleeping army and eliminated them. My men butchered each and every one of the monkeys without making a single noise. Amid that carnage, Prahasta managed to chop off your sleeping husband’s head. No one resisted, and everyone died in their sleep. Most monkeys are lying dead and mangled on the shore. Sugriva is dead from a snapped neck, and Hanuman is lying lifeless with a broken jaw. The few that did manage to escape the slaughter are being chased now by my men as we speak and will be terminated soon. The ocean is red with the blood of the slain, and the corpses will now nourish the millions of sea creatures for weeks to come. With the death of your husband and his monkey troupe, you have no hope.”93

Sita was appalled by the cruel, insensitive graphic descriptions of the massacre of Her husband and His friends. Her head was reeling, unable to come to terms with what She had just heard. It can’t be true. How could He die after coming so close to rescuing Me? She was hysterical. That was exactly the reaction Ravana was expecting. In this state of extreme shock She would have no clarity on Her decision. To drive Her deeper into the grief hole, Ravana had planned a final trick—a trick to drive Her insane. He clapped in his trusted magician.94

Vidyujjihva walked in, cold and expressionless. Although Sita saw the eccentric magician walk in, She could never fathom what he would bring along. She was teary-eyed and was very confused with what Ravana had just said, She was trying to ascertain what the demon was up to now. By now, She was used Ravana’s persistent efforts to please Her for the last ten months. He had done everything in his power to coax Her to marry him. But this time it was different. He didn’t walk in with the intent to please Her or win Her. He walked in with a coldness to destroy Her.95

By this time, Vidyujjihva had walked in and stood next to Ravana. He had in one hand a formidable bow. In the other, he had a chopped head dripping blood. The moment Ravana signaled with a nod and an eyebrow lift, Vidyujjihva threw both of it on the platform on which Sita was sitting. Blood and gore splashed all over when that head hit the platform. The head began to roll toward Sita and settled down right next to Her. Magically, it stood upright when it reached Her, the wide-open stony eyes staring at Her. That face was her beloved Rama’s! But rather than His lovingly gaze was the cold stare of death. Sita panicked, wrenched back and hit Her head on the tree. She couldn’t believe that She was staring at the severed head of Her husband whom She hadn’t seen in eleven months. She never expected to ever see this. She began to wail, grabbing Her knees and crying in agony like a krouncha bird that had lost its mate. The hunter Ravana had mercilessly slaughtered Her husband and now life had lost all meaning.96

Ravana took sadistic pleasure out of Her heartbreaking cries. He walked up to Her, Ravana lifted his feet and thumped it right next to Rama’s and spoke down to Her. “This game is over. You will do better now to prepare yourself mentally to accept me as your husband as you are bereft of any source of protection and hope now.” Sita, numb from this horrifying experience, was still asking questions. How could all those astrologers of Ayodhya who had predicted a lengthy life span for My husband be so wrong? Even if they were wrong, how could Rama, who was so intelligent and prudent in the science of warfare and politics, be so careless as to be butchered so ordinarily and so easily? How would anyone in Ayodhya believe that their valorous prince had crossed the massive ocean only to die in a small puddle?

As Sita sobbed relentlessly, a sudden movement alerted everyone. An envoy rushed hastily toward Ravana and whispered, “Minister Prahastha wants an audience with you very urgently.” Casting yet another lustful and devious glance at the suffering Sita, Ravana walked away, Vidyujjihva following him.

As soon as the two exited the premises of Ashok vatika, the bleeding head and Rama’s bow both magically disappeared, leaving Sita stunned and totally bewildered.97

empathy and ego

She entered the courtroom stealthily. She wasn’t allowed entry without an invitation or a special appointment. Lanka had a strict policy of entry into various entry points. But that day, she broke all rules. She was perhaps inspired by her husband’s actions to break rules that day or perhaps the inspiration had made her compassionate that day. From the time she was born, she had never come across any act of kindness. She was always surrounded by selfishness of the highest order. If someone did something for anyone else, it was only because of fear. From the time she was married, she kept hearing the word “compassion” so many times that she had begun to wonder what it really meant. She learned the real meaning of compassion the day she saw him argue fearlessly in the court of Lanka with his own brother. Her husband was fighting with his brother because he loved his brother dearly and did not want to see him finished. Obviously, no one in Lanka understood his compassion all these years, much less his wife.

But today Sarama, for the first time, Vibhishan’s wife experienced compassion. She was witness to Ravana’s verbal harassment of Sita. She could empathize with Sita and understand the pain She was going through, separated from Her husband for so long and tormented in so many ways. She had been observing Sita for months now. She had noticed something in Her that she had never seen. After Ravana left Sita devastated and torn, Sarama approached Her gently. Placing her hands over Her heaving shoulder, Sarama tried to pacify Sita with words of comfort. Sita was unable to come to terms with Her husband’s freak death despite having come all the way. Sarama wiped the tears off Sita’s face and gave her the wisdom She so badly needed. Sarama said, “If the lone vanara that had entered Lanka was so alert, agile and capable of wreaking havoc on an entire division of the rakshasa army, how could a whole host of vanaras be so inattentive as to allow themselves to be slaughtered in sleep? How could a mere mortal build a bridge across the impassable ocean?” As she spoke, her voice seemed drowned by the sound of loud drums, conches, bugles and other war instruments. Referring to those sounds Sarama pointed out, “If preparations of war are still on, it simply means the enemy is still around Lanka. Why prepare for a war if the enemy has perished?” Sarama’s words made sense to Sita and considerably pacified Her, but She wanted assurance that Rama was really safe. She begged Sarama. “Please ascertain the truth, not by hearsay but from a direct confirmation.” This was why Sarama had dared to sneak into Ravana’s courtroom uninvited.98

As she entered, she found Ravana in a deep and animated conversation with Prahastha. Both the men appeared worried. She edged a little closer to eavesdrop on their conversation, knowing that she was risking being caught. Suddenly, a loud, thunderous noise disrupted everyone’s thoughts in the room and filled everyone’s hearts with fear. The helmets fell off the heads of the startled rakshasa men. Even the chandeliers rattled. A sentry rushed in to inform the clueless courtiers, “That sound was made by the monkeys on the shore. It seems every monkey in that assembly there has raised an unmistakable war cry.” Just the magnitude of the noise sent shivers down Ravana’s spine. Everyone was bewildered by the confidence in that unified call and the sheer force with which it reverberated through every street, home and palace in Lanka. It was a warning that the force was coming and that the opposition wasn’t small.

Faces in the courtroom became ashen with fear, reflecting the fading color on Ravana’s face. Malyavan, Ravana’s maternal grandfather rose to speak. The rakshasas revered his intelligence. He barely spoke, but when he did, it oozed wisdom and maturity. “A weak enemy should immediately be subjugated, but an equal or stronger one should be first dealt in ways other than war. A king should never underrate such an enemy. Alliance with Rama is the best course of action in the current circumstance. You have made a grave mistake by instigating someone righteous. The unrighteous can never thrive in a tiff with the righteous. The entire Rakshasa race is paying the price for your lust. If you open your eyes and look all around, you will only find signs of death and predictions of an upcoming annihilation. Even the animals in our kingdom understand this, and their behavior is changing out of great fear. I sense that Rama is not an ordinary human, but is Lord Vishnu incarnate. Make peace with Him and save yourself and your kingdom from certain disaster.”99

Although burning with rage, Ravana acted cool and spoke calmly. “Old age has gotten to you. You do not speak the language of the valiant Rakshasa race anymore. Taking your old age into consideration, I have decided not to take your insulting words seriously. How can a man who has been kicked out of his own kingdom cause any harm to me? Hosts of gods tremble in front of me. And you expect me to fear a mere mortal? No one wins a war just by building a bridge. You will soon watch a dreadful display of my prowess as I eliminate the entire monkey army and finally sever Rama’s head. Remember that Ravana never bends. I don’t mind being cut in half, but I will never bend. I promise you that Rama and his monkeys will not return alive.”100

Malyavan was so fed up of his grandson’s vanity that he walked off without saying a word. Sarama could see Ravana’s fury at his grandfather’s insulting exit right in the middle of a conversation. He looked around, embarrassed. But everyone was glancing at the floor or elsewhere to avoid the gaze of their discomfited king. Ravana looked at Prahastha to pick up the discussion from where they had it left off before the pandemonium. He quickly barked orders to divide the army and cover the gates. They deliberated quickly and assigned each of the city gates to powerful rakshasa men and sent along huge divisions to safeguard all the entry points. The safety of the city and its citizens was the most important. The war was not Ravana’s biggest concern. An even bigger concern was the monkeys’ indiscipline and propensity to enter from anywhere anytime. He wanted to first ensure that the city was covered from all directions, leaving no scope for surprises within the city while the army was engaged in warfare outside.

A powerful thud shook everyone. This time it wasn’t just a sound coming from somewhere outside the palace. This time it was from inside the courtroom. Everyone turned around to the section of the courtroom where the sound originated—a rugged vanara was crashing down with an entire portion of the ceiling. The courtiers were aghast by the daring these monkeys. This lone vanara had not gate-crashed, but ceiling-crashed into Lanka’s courtroom. Without waiting for people to recover from their shocks, the vanara began to walk toward the throne where Ravana was standing. Confidence exuded as he walked in and no one dared step in and stop him. His expressions were intense. While everyone wondered who he was, Ravana wondered how it could be. While for everyone, he was just another monkey, for Ravana, he reminded him of a friend, oh well, let’s just say an acquaintance from the past. That uncanny resemblance to Vali simply threw Ravana off guard. He knew Vali was dead, but here he was walking up to him. The only confusing thing was he seemed way younger than Vali.

The vanara positioned himself right in front of the throne, facing Ravana eye to eye. Hands on his hips, the vanara was here to make a statement. This time Ravana decided to wait to ascertain before saying anything. His experience with Vali was nothing less than the greatest embarrassment of his life. That memory was enough to silence him for the time being. Sensing the cause of Ravana’s paralysis, the vanara spoke up. “I am Angad, the son of late Vali. I am here on behalf of Lord Rama to issue a final warning. Let’s put it this way, you are being given the last chance. Return Sita, else you will join my father. Rama’s arrows are the most powerful and never miss their mark. If you have any desire to live, return Mother Sita.”

Why Angad for this mission?

Rama wanted to send an official warning to Ravana and offer one last chance for compromise before the war began. He had taken the advice of His council regarding whom to send. Sugriva was low on confidence as his opinion was disregarded and Vibhishan was crowned king of Lanka. So he kept quiet to avoiding giving inappropriate advice. Vibhishan had suggested praying to the ocean god, which had failed miserably and wasted three precious days of Rama’s time. So he chose to keep quiet now. Hanuman usually preferred not to share his opinion in the presence of superiors. So he kept quiet, too.

Sensing the need of the hour, Jambavan, the intelligent and clearthinking gifted son of Lord Brahma made a well-rounded suggestion in his trademark soft and humble way. He always offered his advice, first appreciating Rama’s omniscience. Jambavan believed Angad should be sent to convey the last message to Ravana. He had been observing Angad’s behavior from the time Vali departed. Even though Angad was the prince of Kishkinda, he had been overshadowed by the presence of powerful personalities such as Sugriva and Hanuman. Angad was the leader of the search party officially, but Hanuman had walked away with all the glory. Angad was happy with Hanuman’s victory and heroism, but somewhere it had left him lonely and he seemed to have lost his confidence. He barely even spoke.

Jambavan could understand his quietness and felt it would be great if Angad could also receive some share of the glory Hanuman had received in the past. Besides, he was convinced the emissary had to know how to speak aptly and not only glorify Lord Rama with his fine speech, but also be stern enough with the deliverance of the warning that it would resonate in Ravana’s mind.”

Jambavan felt that if Hanuman were sent again, Ravana would feel that theirs was a one-hero army. That would again send a wrong signal. Introducing Angad would totally shake them up.

Even politically, Angad would be the best choice as he was the son of Vali, a friend of Ravana. Ravana would not feel threatened initially on seeing Angad and would be open to a discussion with a friend’s son. Additionally, a subtle point they would make was that if Angad had chosen to glorify and even take refuge in the same Rama who had killed his father, then Ravana could also consider the possibility of connecting the most noble Rama.

Most importantly, Jambavan felt the omniscient Rama knew all this but still wanted to test their decision-making process. Thus, he made his suggestion and left the decision to Rama.

“Seize him! Kill him!” The king instructions incited four powerful rakshasa bodyguards to pounce on Angad and hold his arms on both sides. Angad simply rocketed up, heaved them all along. As he rose higher toward the ceiling, the four of them hit their head on the ceiling and their skulls cracked on impact. This heinous elimination of the four most powerful of Ravana’s bodyguards rattled the courtiers. Before Ravana could even react, Angad spoke again. “O Ravana, I want to show you a little of the power of Lord Rama and his army. If you really have any of the strengths you claim, I challenge you to come and lift my left foot off the ground. You will not even be able to shift a toe.”

Angad had chosen his words carefully. He knew a true warrior would never deny a challenge. Ravana was in a fix. He could neither refuse nor accept. He turned toward his son Indrajit and gestured with his eyes. Indrajit stood up. “O mighty king! You need not rise up for this puny monkey.” Angad grinned, and Indrajit caught that. He knew these monkeys always had a trick or two up their sleeve. He approached Angad who was standing erect with his super confidence with his left leg pushed forward. Indrajit, feigning calm and disdain, bent a little and tried to lift the leg grabbing the knee with his left hand. He felt that lifting a monkey’s leg was child’s play for a powerful rakshasa such as him, the defeater of Indra. What? How? Why? Indrajit was shocked it was heavier than expected. He turned a bit and used his stronger right hand. Same result. He bent a little more and held the calf muscles. No, nothing at all. He started to become a little concerned as everyone was watching, all their hopes pinned on him. This was after all a royal challenge. He bent lower and lowered his hand further and his head to align with the monkey’s calf muscles. All he could see was a pair of powerfully shaped rippling muscles. He put all his effort on yanking the leg off the ground. He firmly gripped the monkey’s ankles with both hands. He hunched posture was good enough to ensure complete energy transfer to his hands. Once absolutely sure that he had complete grip and total control over the haul, Indrajit heaved with all his strength. Still nothing. The foot seemed embedded into the ground. He gave up after several attempts and returned to his seat, his head hung low.101

Angad looked at Ravana. Not even a single word had to be spoken now to make the king of Lanka stand up and descend from his throne. Bending low probably for the first time in his life, Ravana put both his hands in an effort to pick up the monkey’s foot off the floor. But in a moment, he realized that this was something else. Suddenly more arms began to emerge from the sides of his torso. The arms gripped at various places all around the leg and together they tried to lift the foot off. But in spite of twenty arms trying their mighty best, the foot just didn’t budge. Angad spoke kindly to Ravana looking down at him. “O king, I am going to make it much easier for you now. I am lifting my foot off the floor, but will only be placing a single toe on the floor. I now challenge you to lift only that toe and prove your might.”

This was getting a bit too much. Look at the confidence and audacity of that lone monkey. He is playing around with the most powerful man in the universe currently. Was he really?

Ravana realized that his reputation was at stake with all his ministers keenly looking at him and wondering if their king would really lose what seemed to be the most reasonable challenge posed to a mighty warrior. Angad had by then placed a single toe of his foot on the floor and was challenging Ravana. Ravana mustered all his strength, and this time did not even try the two-hand approach. He straightaway used his 20 arms to pluck out the single toe so sternly embedded into the ground. His herculean effort led nowhere. He had plucked even the mighty Mount Kailash. Here he was unable to move a single toe of an insignificant monkey. With a look of disbelief more than defeat, Ravana stood up.

Useless Reverence

When Ravana went to lift Angad’s leg, Angad remarked that he was a strange person because he was ready to touch the feet of a monkey but not ready to touch Lord Rama’s lotus feet.

He, in fact, called Ravana a donkey ready to carry heavy burdens instead of understanding the import of what he was carrying. He carried Lord Shiva on his head like a load, but could not understand the greatness of Lord Shiva and his worship-worthy Lord Rama. When one meets a saint, one places one’s head at their feet, but Ravana foolishly picked up Lord Shiva and carried him around. When one places one’s head at the feet of great people, it means that one lays down one’s burdens at their feet and one becomes light. But Ravana instead of placing his burdens at the feet of Lord Shiva, he made Shiva another burden for him to carry.

He had only one question to ask Angad. “What is the source of your strength?”

That was exactly the question Angad had been waiting for. The purpose of the whole experiment was simply to get Ravana to ask this most important and possibly life-changing question. Angad closed his eyes and with folded hands, he replied. “I simply inhaled while chanting the name of Lord Rama and held my breath. When the name of Lord Rama is within me, it assumes the weight of the whole universe.”

Obviously Ravana thought this was all rubbish. When Angad realized this demon wasn’t going to change and understand the deeper things in life, he simply turned around and propelled himself up breaking open another section of the ceiling of the courtroom and disappeared. Just above where Angad stood was big a gaping hole—not a hole in the ceiling, but a hole in Ravana’s confidence in his strength.102

Steadfast Faith

There is another version of this story with a unique perspective.

When Angad left as a messenger, Rama offered him a parting instruction. This was His expectation from the mission Angad was taking up. He asked Angad to keep two things in mind—complete the work of passing on the message to Ravana and do the best for Ravana. What was best for Ravana, Rama did not clearly say. It was up to Angad to guess.

As Angad was moving toward Ravana’s palace, he began to feel like a doctor about to visit a sick patient. When a patient meets a doctor, he has to set aside his ego and intelligence to be able to admit his weakness and accept the doctor’s diagnosis. If he is too egoistic, he will never be able to admit that he is sick and needs help. If he uses his intelligence, he will think that he is smarter than the doctor and can cure himself. The patient has to trust the doctor’s intelligence.

After some interaction with Ravana, Angad realized that this was an incurable patient. His mind, intelligence and ego were totally out of control. Thus, it was impossible for him to accept the treatment of any doctor as he thought he was too smart.

When Angad realized that if he could not do good for Ravana, he had to at least complete the work of Rama. To make the message of Rama loud and clear, he decided to punch Ravana. The punch was so intense that Ravana trembled and fell off the throne. Ravana’s ministers were shocked to see their king roll off the throne. It all happened so fast that before anyone had the time to even stand up, Angad pounced on Ravana and delivered the punch. With his fall, all his crowns rolled off his ten heads. Ravana got up and began scrambling to recover his fallen crowns. While Ravana was doing that, Angad managed to seize four of the ten crowns and throw them out of the courtroom with great force. The crowns landed at the feet of Lord Rama. The vanaras sitting close were frightened, assuming the enemies had shot a missile at them. But Rama simply laughed off their unnecessary fear. But he didn’t consider it cowardice, considering fear was a natural emotion. In fact, in the beginning of creation, the first emotion Lord Brahma ever experienced was fear. Thus fear and courage can reside in the same person. So their fear did not disturb Rama so much making him doubt their courage.

Angad punched Ravana to show him the power of his arms. The same arms that were held by Lord Rama when Vali handed them over to Rama before dying. Angad wanted Ravana to know that the arms held by the Lord have the strength of the Lord in them. He wanted to tell Ravana that it was not the arm of an ordinary monkey, but it was the arm of the monkey touched by Rama.

In addition to punching Ravana with his arms, Angad also challenged Ravana with his legs by asking him to lift his leg if he actually had any strength. By doing that Angad established that his legs were empowered by the Lord and his energies. The name of Rama in his being made him heavier than the mountains. Additionally, mother earth, who was troubled by the presence of demons like Ravana, held on to the feet of Angad intensely, making it impossible for anyone to lift that which was embedded into the earth.

Angad had full faith that his foot would not move when he challenged Ravana. Many people asked him if he felt scared that his foot would be shaken. Such a question is reflective of a weak and terrorized mind. As far as Angad was concerned, his doubtlessness reflected his intense faith in Lord Rama. Lord Rama had sent him as His envoy and by doing that He had reposed full faith in him. Angad proved that faith worthwhile. While leaving Lord Rama, Angad had told Lord Rama, “You don’t really need anyone to do Your work, but it is Your greatness and humility that You are making me to do this.”

Sarama slipped out of the courtroom. She had seen and heard enough. She had enough information to allay Sita’s fears. It wasn’t She who needed to be afraid, it was Ravana’s turn now. She had suffered enough. Now Her heroic husband was coming.

past-life regression

The whole thing was stressing him out. His son was too small to discuss such issues. His ministers were too immature to give him good suggestions. His wife was too concerned to talk straight. He was too confused to figure it all out. He needed someone he could trust, someone with experience, someone who was his own and someone who had the guts to speak up in front of him without fear. None fit that bill in Lanka. Yet he yearned to have one good talk before hurling himself into the inevitable battle that awaited his next move. After much deliberation, Ravana realized that there was only one person in the entire universe that fitted his requirement. It was someone trustworthy, someone with immense experience of winning against even the gods, someone whom even Vishnu served as a gatekeeper, someone who was a rakshasa like him and someone with the courage to refuse his own guru if he thought he was wrong. It was none other than the daitya king, Bali, who ruled the sutalaloka in the subterranean regions.103

Ravana found his way to sutala that night. It was a very different world. The rules were completely different from that on earth. After passing through heavy security protocols, especially designed to monitor entry into Bali’s palace, Ravana entered the king’s inner chambers. The splendor of sutalaloka was such that Lanka felt like an amusement park in comparison. As he walked into the palace looking at all the luxurious wonders, he wondered how much he could have enhanced the security system of Lanka had he amassed this kind of wealth. After the entry of the savage monkey army, Ravana had really become apprehensive of Lanka’s manpower as well as the security systems. He felt he needed some urgent upgradation. After observing the security systems of sutala, he was even more convinced Bali could really help him the most.

At first glance, Bali’s grandeur made Ravana swoon. He had never seen such charisma in any king anywhere in the universe. He offered his respects and proceeded with his problem. Bali was very kind and cordial. He offered Ravana an ornate seat and sat next to him, all ready to hear his woes. Ravana began speaking about how Lanka had been surrounded on all sides by two men who had come with an army of monkeys. He obviously had to share the fact that he had abducted the wife of one of the men. Bali’s brow rose when he heard that. He was used to rakshasa men behaving like this. But every time he heard any such atrocious act committed by someone of his own clan, he cringed. For a little pleasure and thrill rakshasa men went to any extent and took any amount of risk. History only repeated itself with different names.

Bali had a simple question to ask. “Why would you be concerned about two men and a group of monkeys? Weren’t rakshasas fond of eating monkey flesh? And since when did humans become a threat to the rakshasa clan?” Ravana knew this was coming. He cleared his throat and spoke about his most embarrassing episodes. “These monkeys are different.” He explained how three monkeys had come into his life and messed up things for him. As he spoke, painful memories flooded back into his mind.

The first monkey had burned down his golden city. The second one had spat on his head and harassed him before running away. The third monkey had embarrassed him the most silently. When Ravana spoke about these embarrassments, one after another, Bali tried to suppress a smile. But he had a burning question. “If three monkeys managed to do this to you, then I dread to think about the consequence of an attack by the whole army together.” Ravana promptly answered, “That is precisely why I am here for your help. You are a rakshasa yourself, isn’t it apt that you help another rakshasa?” 104

Bali asked him, “Have you ever considered returning Sita and saving yourself and your whole kingdom from damage and disrepute?” Now that was a point Ravana did not want broached at all. He abruptly stood up, annoyed. His lips trembled with rage, “I have come here for help, not advice.”

Bali sensed the sensitive condition of Ravana’s mind. He decided to deal with the matter more tactfully. He stood up and patted Ravana on his shoulder and pulled him along. “ I have something interesting to show you.” This piqued Ravana’s curiosity. He was upset by Bali’s suggestion, but he complied by following Bali to a huge garden. When they reached the middle of the garden, Ravana saw something that brought a sparkle to his eyes. His ran ahead like a gleeful child who had discovered a new toy. Right in front of him was the biggest mountain of gold he had ever seen. It was so huge that it occupied a considerable portion of the garden. He couldn’t even see its summit. The top of the golden mountain was studded with brilliant stones.

The structure astounded Ravana. Slipping his hand under the mountain base, Ravana made an attempt to move it. Back in the day, he had successfully lifted the Kailash mountain. This surely had to be lighter. No matter how much he tried, it did not move. Giving up, he asked Bali, “Is there a reason I can’t lift this mountain when I have already dislodged the Kailash years ago?”105

Bali had brought him there only to prompt this curiosity in him. Instead of answering, he simply said, “Follow me to understand the secret of this mystical golden mountain.” Ravana was still wondering. What could it be? Bali took Ravana up a flight of stairs. They reached a terrace and stopped. Turning around, Bali pointed. Ravana followed Bali’s finger to something totally unexpected. The golden mountain in the garden wasn’t really a mountain, it was instead a giant earring. Ravana’s mind exploded. How was it even possible? Whose earring is it? If this is the size of the earring, how big is the wearer? As these thoughts were befuddling Ravana, Bali gave him some insightful and life-changing information.

Bali eased Ravana’s doubts. “This earring is of the rakshasa Hiranyaksha who lived in the previous yuga. At some point in his life, when his tyranny reached a crescendo, Lord Vishnu incarnated as Varaha, the boar, and eliminated him after a gruesome fight. In that fight that Varaha slapped Hiranyaksha so hard that one of his earrings fell off and hurtled into the sutalaloka. It has been lying here since. What came next shocked Ravana even more.

“Ravana, you were Hiranyaksha in your previous birth. What is even more shocking is that Varaha who had slaughtered you in your last life has reincarnated as Rama. In spite of changing the yuga, Varaha’s strength is exactly the same in Rama, but Hiranyaksha’s strength has diminished in you, Ravana.” After revealing this most confidential secret, Bali adviced Ravana to return Sita to Rama and not let history repeat itself.

When Ravana heard this, he was extremely disturbed for a moment. He could clearly understand now the reason for his hatred toward Rama. This was more than a single lifetime of hatred. The karmic cycle had to be completed, and the universe had conspired to bring them back together for the final face-off. Bali’s advice didn’t even enter Ravana’s ears. His mind was too pre-occupied with thoughts of revenge. Rather than seeing the complication and the near impossibility of victory, Ravana chose to focus on revenge.106

Ravana walked away from Bali a changed person. His goal now was not just to enjoy Sita but to eliminate Rama and avenge his past life.

## Chapter 6

Veneration and Humiliation

From as far into the deep water horizon they could see, incessant waves of bodies were surging closer. Their heartbeats grew louder and their jaws dropped deeper as the trembling rakshasas watched the massiveness of the monkey army headed toward them. As the distance between the armies became shorter, the warriors’ roar became louder. It seemed like two vast oceans with turbulent surges were going to crash into each other. Passion had peaked. Now the only goal was to spill as much of the enemy’s blood as possible.

The two armies rammed into each other—the rakshasa army with its advanced vehicles, weapons, armors and strategy, and the vanara army with its raw power, purity, purpose, determination, trees and stones. Only Rama and Lakshmana carried bows. Some vanara leaders had maces. The rest were totally reliant on their fists, teeth, nails and whatever trees and stones they could find. The vanaras followed no structure. They simply pounced on the demons, pulled them down from their vehicles and pummeled them. The demons used sharp, potent weapons and managed to massacre thousands of vanaras in one stroke. Leaders clashed with leaders, and soldiers clashed with soldiers. The mace-wielding Vibhishan and his four demon friends formed a protection circle around Rama and Lakshmana. As he moved further into the enemy’s block, he kept destroying the demoniac forces with his mace. Blood was everywhere—on soldiers’ bodies, on the battleground, on smashed vehicles and broken weapons.

Confidence Is Key

The vanaras roared like lions even though they were monkeys. This change in sound showed their confidence level. Your voice and tone reflects how confident you are. The lion is the king of the jungle and when he roars, there is great confidence in that roar because he is aware of his power. In the rakshasa army, Indrajit was known for his powerful battle cries and sounded like thunderclaps, hence the other name Meghanath. Just as Meghanath’s confidence came from his own prowess, the vanaras’ confidence came from remembering the power of Rama.

Compared to the rakshasas of Lanka who were experts in the science of war and the use of sophisticated weapons, the vanaras were insufficiently equipped both in skill and equipment. Although they were limited in several ways, it seemed strange that they were roaring confidently like lions. The fact is wars are never fought with weapons but with will power.

At one point in the war, Angad found Hanuman alone on the peak of a mountain fighting against thousands of rakshasas attacking him from all directions. He ran up to help his source of inspiration. While they were fighting, it was not just a display of their courage, but an attempt at trying to spread the glories of Lord Rama. As they continued attacking, they kept announcing to the demons that the reason they were all dying was because they had never glorified god. Even after they killed the demons, they continue to spread the glories of Lord Rama. They would take the head of the dead demons and throw it at Ravana as if to say that this what is going to happen to his head as well. And for other demons, they would pull them with their legs and throw them toward Lord Rama so that at least after death, they would fall at His lotus feet and be blessed.

The battle raged through the day with neither team gaining ground. The sun began to sink slowly, vultures and other carnivorous birds began to hover over the battlefield waiting to feast on the aftermath. But they were disappointed, as the battle just did not end at sunset. It continued into the night, even though it was dark and no one could see or identify anyone. They kept at it by just hearing and feeling their enemies.

Night and Day

When the sun sets, the demons become more and more enthusiastic. After all, this war was between Suryavansh, which is the sun dynasty and the dynasty of the nightly demons. Brahma had created both day and night. Both complement the other, and both need to be used in the right way. The right way is to use the day for action, that is karma, and the night for rest. But when the night—the mode of ignorance—is used for actions in the mode of passion, it is detrimental to the society. The nightly demons show this aspect of their nature. Because they are in the mode of ignorance, their energies multiply at night.

Torches were soon lit and flame arrows began to fly from Rama and Lakshmana’s bows causing massive disruption in the rakshasa camp. The brothers could shoot six arrows at a time, with each finding its mark and perfectly eliminating six demons at one go. The fall of the rakshasa men in heaps annoyed Indrajit. His terrible rage made him break the battle code. The rakshasas hardly followed the codes of kshatriya warfare. For them, the end justified the means. Donning an invisible form, Indrajit began to fly over the battlefield in his multidirectional chariot. Soon arrows began to shower from the skies on the heads of unsuspecting vanaras and instantly killing them. The sharp arrows rained so fast that self-defense was impossible. The rakshasas stepped away, leaving the vanaras totally exposed to Indrajit’s slaughter-fest.

Rama and Lakshmana stepped in to the rescue of the fleeing and panicking vanaras. They began to counter every raining arrow with their arrows. But Indrajit was so quick that he would already move far away from his shooting position even before the arrows reached their destination. Indrajit’s chariot wheels were like propellers, with no ground friction slowing them down. When Indrajit saw the brothers actively trying to overpower him, he knocked the nagastra weapons (snake weapons) on his bow and shot at the brothers. The serpent-shaped infallible arrows zipped out of Indrajit’s bow and rushed toward the brothers. The arrows pierced into the bodies of Rama and Lakshmana, leaving them bleeding. The vanaras winced when they saw their heroes wounded. Infused with a burst of vengeful energy, they launched a vigorous attack on the flying invisible demon prince.

Through the dark clouds of illusion, they could hear his rattling chariot and bow twangs. Sometimes they could even see a flash of his golden armor and a streak of his golden spear, but of him they could see nothing. One by one, all the great vanara heroes except for Hanuman and Jambavan fell to Indrajit’s deadly arrows. A flaming axe flew through the sky and wounded Sugriva very badly. Thirty-three crescent-shaped arrows pierced Angad and an iron hook clawed into Nala’s chest. Ten diamond-shaped arrows pierced the king of bears and a barbed spear tore Vibhishan’s shoulder. Hanuman flew into the clouds. He couldn’t see his opponent, but he saw a flaming sword flying toward him. He grabbed the sword, but it changed into a young woman crying for mercy. So he let it go and it changed back into a sword and cut him down to the ground. The whole field was covered with the dead and dying.107

Indrajit shredded all the trees and rocks that the vanaras were hurling at him. The pulverized pieces rained back on the vanaras, unsettling them. Once the vanaras were temporarily deactivated, Indrajit turned his attention back on the brothers to complete what he had begun. Lacerating their flesh with a glut of arrows, Indrajit wounded them mortally. Blood gushed from wounds of both their arrow-embedded bodies. The wounded brothers staggered and collapsed on the ground. Indrajit laughed heartily. He had faith in his prowess. I have defeated the greatest of gods, how could two humans have lasted beyond a few minutes? As the brothers collapsed to the ground, the vanaras dropped their weapons and rushed to the spot. Indrajit guffawed. “I have won the war even before it has truly begun.” Gathering the rakshasa soldiers, he exited the war field, leaving behind an army of distraught monkeys surrounding their fallen heroes. As the vanaras gathered around the unconscious Rama and Lakshmana, their hearts broke to see the brothers impaled with so many arrows that there was not even a single finger’s gap between arrows. Blood was oozing out from their wounds, slowly creating a puddle around them.108

Meanwhile, a celebration broke out in the Lankan palace as soon as Indrajit delivered the good news to his father. Ravana’s heart swelled with pride on hearing the details about his son’s heroism. This was definitely a proud moment for the father. Ravana embraced his son with great joy and smelled his head affectionately. It was a rare moment of exhibition of emotion by the king. Ravana quickly made a declaration across Lanka. “The war is over. The prime enemies are dead.”

This was an important move to allay the fears of the citizens so who dreaded being surrounded by a vast army of tyrant monkeys. He even declared that the streets of Lanka be decorated with festoons and flowers to give it a festive look. Then he did what he was dying to do. He summoned Trijata and ordered her to take Sita to the battle site and allow Her to see with Her own eyes the dead bodies of the two slain brothers. Trijata was soon air bound with Sita, soaring toward the battleground. On their way, the watched celebrations in Lanka reach a maddening pitch. By the time they reached the grounds, along the way, extreme celebration had given way to extreme lamentation.

The morose vanaras were mum. They had all gathered around in large numbers to deal with the catastrophe. When Sita saw what had caused the distress, She fainted. The brothers lay mortally wounded in a pool of their own blood. Seeing Sita distraught and losing her mind, Trijata spoke logically. “Look, the brothers can’t be dead for several reasons. First and foremost being, the pushpak vimana was designed in such a way that it doesn’t fly with a widow in it. So if the chariot is still in flight, you couldn’t be a widow. Else this machine wouldn’t fly at all. Besides, look closer at the bodies of Rama and Lakshmana. They may seem lifeless, but they haven’t started to lose color. And most importantly, the vanaras are sad, but they aren’t crying. In fact, they appear to have gathered around their heroes to protect them. Why would anyone do that to protect dead bodies?” Trijata’s arguments were very reasonable and Sita stopped lamenting the loss of Her husband. Trijata reversed the pushpak vimana and dropped Sita back in Ashok vatika. Once in the privacy of Her tree home, Sita began to offer prayers for the safety of Rama and Lakshmana.109

The vanara leaders were very confused. Their medical chief Sushena was doing whatever he could, but the problem seemed more complicated than his expertise. Sugriva started to weep. So much effort had gone into reaching this stage. This was not how he had foreseen the end. How could Rama and Lakshmana have fallen on the very first day of the war? Why didn’t we protect the brothers enough? Seeing Sugriva’s pitiable condition, Vibhishan walked up to him and wiped away his tears assuring him this wasn’t the end. Surely, some solution would come up. As he spoke, he saw movement from the corner of his eye. Rama had opened his eyes with great struggle. Many arrows were entrenched in his body, but He still managed to sit up. The movement of His arms and legs were restricted because of the invisible Nagapasha that had bound Him. When He saw a bloodied Lakshmana lie lifeless next to Him, Rama’s heart broke. Uncaring for His own delicate state, He began to cry at His brother’s apparent death. He was disheartened to know that His brother had sacrificed his life for His cause. Rama felt responsible for the loss, and He urged all the vanaras to return to their abodes and let Him deal with His destiny alone. It didn’t make any sense sacrificing innocent lives for His sake. It wasn’t fair.110

Sushena spoke. “There is a solution to this problem. If someone manages to procure some herbs very rarely found on earth, but definitely in the Chandra and Drona mountains way up north, then I could do something to save the slowly ebbing life of Lakshmana.” The moment he spoke about the impossible, the leaders immediately looked out for Hanuman. That’s when they realized that he was missing. No one knew his whereabouts. Where could he have disappeared in an hour of such dire need?

Suddenly a fierce storm rose, speedy gusts blew dust from the battleground. The vanaras covered the wounded brothers to save them from the sudden inexplicable dust storm. When the dust settled, they found in their midst the most gigantic eagle. Although huge, a very positive aura emanated from it. To their greatest surprise, Hanuman was standing right next to the giant bird. Immediately, they could connect the reason for his disappearance. While they had been staring at the problem, Hanuman had ventured out to fetch the solution to the problem. Jambavan immediately recognized Garuda, the bird carrier of Lord Vishnu. Seeing the old soul offer his respects to the mighty bird, all the vanaras followed suit. Offering him respect right in front of his eternal master, Lord Rama embarrassed Garuda to the core. As soon as Garuda walked toward Rama and Lakshmana, the arrows embedded into their torso dislodged and flew off. The invisible Nagapasha unwound, and Rama could move freely. As soon as Garuda ran his hands over the bodies of Rama and Lakshmana, the gashes and wounds healed mysteriously, and their skin regained color and texture within seconds. Lakshmana opened his eyes. With tears of joy, Rama embraced His brother. After a minute, He pulled in Garuda into that embrace, who felt thrilled to be in the loving embrace of his eternal master.111

Garuda went on to explain to Rama and the curious vanaras the nature of the Nagapasha weapon that had numbed the brothers. He said, “The Nagas are my step-brothers, but also my arch enemies. While I focus my energies on contributing positively to the cause of this universe, the Nagas cause great harm and damage to the universal equilibrium. When the Nagas join greater negative forces, their strength to harm becomes even greater. The only sure antidote to the Nagapasha is my presence. The moment I appear close, the Nagas immediately flee as I have the permission and the power to gobble them up.” In a nutshell, Garuda warned Rama and the vanara leaders to not take the demons’ powers lightly and always remain alert as they would keep using the many secret powers in their possession. After sufficiently cautioning them, Garuda offered his final respects and disappeared at unimaginable speed.112

Once he was gone, the vanara leaders declared to the whole distressed army that Rama and Lakshmana were completely alright and ready to resume battle. Amid loud celebratory cheers and hooting, Hanuman and Angad carried Rama and Lakshmana through the vanara ranks to boost their morale. Night had set in, and the vanaras were exhausted after a mentally draining Day One. There wasn’t much time before daybreak so they lay down wherever they could find some space to stretch their tired limbs. As Vibhishan started to settle in, two birds flew and perched on a rock next to him. He immediately got up. They were no birds. They were his spies who had fetched information from Lanka. This was serious— probably a toss-up between victory and defeat. It was as if the god of death himself had issued a warning. As the spies were sharing their observation with Vibhishan, Rama walked in. Vibhishan updated Him, and a look of worry traveled from Vibhishan’s face to Rama’s. Ravana’s latest strategy cooking in his camp and in the cauldron of his cruel brain made everyone anxious.113

Chandi stotra!

Though incomprehensible, the name shook everyone. What could the consequence be? It couldn’t mean anything less than the total annihilation of their entire army. A detailed discussion with Vibhishan clarified matters for Rama and He could now understand the gravity of the matter. He assigned this outlandish project to Hanuman with a very unconventional instruction. “Do whatever is needed to stop this master plan of Ravana!” Hanuman loved such instructions. The freedom that came with such instructions just fueled his enthusiasm. He could now use all his unconventional talents and ideas to serve Lord Rama. In a flash, Hanuman was a fly on the wall of Ravana’s palace. His roving eyes constantly scanned and noted points and strategic locations that he could use to exit at the appropriate time. A sudden strange sound caught his attention. It was almost like a drone. On closer observation, he noticed that it came from a slightly ajar door that was about to be shut. He had to decide soon. He leaped, and in a blink just before the door shut, he entered the room—the venue of the chandi stotra.

Hanuman was shocked to find that of all people in the universe, Brihaspati, the guru of the demigods was leading the recitation of the chandi stotra. Why would he do that? Why would the guru of the demigods favor Ravana, and that too, secretly? One look around, and it was obvious from the stringent security around Brihaspati that he was reciting it under duress. Hanuman swooped down and perched on the manuscript off which Brihaspati was reciting the shlokas. No one could detect the real identity of a fly. With his feet, he craftily rubbed off two words from the manuscript. The trick failed miserably when he realized that Brihaspati was not referring to the script. He knew the entire stotra by rote, except for a few words here and there. Brihaspati’s photographic memory could gloss over the missing words.

Hanuman looked around carefully for some idea to impede this recitation. He noticed that though sealed from outside, this section of the palace was quite massive inside. It was designed like a very complicated maze—exactly as he needed it to be. Hanuman quickly surveyed the guards stationed around Brihaspati. Noticing that their attention wasn’t on Brihaspati and that they were not expecting any sabotage, Hanuman knew he had to act. In an instant, he changed from a fly to a gigantic monster. Brihaspati was flabbergasted. He shrieked loudly and collapsed. In that moment of panic, Hanuman returned to being a fly again. Panic struck everyone. Brihaspati scampered hither and thither across the room in utter fear. No matter which direction he ran to, Hanuman reappeared in his ghastly form to scare him further. The guards couldn’t figure out what was happening. The guru of the demigods had turned pale and was, in some manic frenzy, screaming aloud and covering his eyes. Brihaspati ran into the maze and couldn’t find his way back. Amid this commotion, Hanuman managed to erase three full verses from the script. The recitation turned chaotic and Brihaspati was in no position to continue. And when Ravana forced him to complete the recitation, he unwittingly omitted reciting those three verses. His memory had failed him. When Ravana became aware of the mayhem, he came to redeem the recital. With his expertise in chanting, he tried to mollify the goddess, but in vain.114

Ravana realized that the recital was a failure when nothing happened at the end of it. Instead of abandoning the entire effort, Ravana planned something even more drastic. Luckily, Hanuman had decided to hang around to understand Ravana’s plans. This time, Ravana performed a fire sacrifice to appease Goddess Chandi—something more effective than mere recital. In a short while, a group of very trustworthy and scholarly brahmanas were selected to perform this ritual.

As the brahmanas were making their way to the ritual arena, a very enthusiastic young brahmana begged them to allow him to participate in this supremely auspicious and important yagna for the benefit of the kingdom and their king. They allowed him in because of his willingness to help, coupled with their need for assistance in such a massive sacrifice. They did not repent their decision of inviting him. He was all over the place assisting them all, as if he had ten hands. As soon as an officiating brahmana asked him for an item, he was there, pronto. His contribution was so significant that soon every brahmana was pleased with the young boy. They blessed him and asked him to ask for anything he wanted.115

“The opportunity to serve great souls is the greatest gift ever,” he said. This reply impressed them even more. They were now more than eager to grant him some tangible blessings or favors. They insisted that he ask for something immediately.

Very reluctantly, he spoke up. “If you insist, let me ask you something that will benefit our king. I request you to make a small change in the final chant. Although the change seems small, it will benefit our master greatly. Substitute the letter “ha” by the letter “ka.” That’s all I want.” The young brahmana’s genuine sincerity to serve their master and his attitude in serving them had pleased them so much that they didn’t really think much about the implication of what he was saying.
