They did it.

*Jaya tvam devi chamunde*

*Jaya bhutartha kaarini*

Instead of saying “haarini” they ended up saying “kaarini.”

One small change, and the result flipped. The goddess was enraged with the sacrifice, and her fiery eyes emitted rays that destroyed the entire sacrifice—everything turned to ash.

The young brahmana walked out unscathed with a smirk. Hanuman now returned to his form and flew out of Ravana’s camp before anyone could track him.116

insult after insult

This was the second time in the history of Lanka that it had been attacked from all four gates. The first time, Ravana himself had attacked Lanka back when it was ruled by Kubera, the head of yakshas. Ravana was young and full of enthusiasm then, and he had easily overwhelmed the yaksha army and conquered Lanka. After conquering Lanka and tasting success, Ravana gradually became complacent. He began to think that Lanka was unconquerable under his rule and that he was invincible. His sister Surpanakha had once warned him of his smugness. She had warned him never to take his enemies, fire and sins lightly. But Ravana couldn’t care less.

Actually, Ravana was quite convinced that Rama would not attack him as He did not have the support of any army. He was living in the forest, and He wouldn’t take the help of Ayodhaya’s army. About that, Ravana was right. Rama didn’t take the shelter of Ayodhya. But it never occurred to Ravana that Rama could seek help from the vanaras and reach Lanka. Not only had Rama attacked Lanka with the vanara army, but He had attacked Lanka from all four sides exactly the way Ravana had attacked it when capturing Lanka from Kubera.117

Ravana now sent his most powerful man into the war zone. Dhumraksha speeded into the battleground on his massive chariot drawn by donkeys. A headless body crashed right in front of his chariot, bringing it to a screeching halt. Even before he recovered from the shock, a massive tree smashed into his chariot, completely destroying it. Dhumraksha jumped off just in time, but fell straight into the clutches of Hanuman. The demon smashed his mace on Hanuman’s head, but it barely mattered to him. But when Hanuman retaliated by smashing a rock on the demon’s head, the rakshasa hero died an instant death.

Then came Vajradamstra from the southern gate where Angad was eagerly waiting for action. As soon as he entered the battle arena, a huge boulder fell from somewhere up above and smashed his chariot. Luckily, he saw it coming and escaped in time. His chariot driver was not that lucky though. Even before he recovered, he saw Angad rushing toward him with a massive rock held over his head with both his hands. Not giving time for the demon to react, Angad hit the rock on his head and left him bleeding. Recovering from the attack, Vajradamstra struck Angad on the chest with his club. Angad reeled in pain, but he recovered quickly and resumed his attack with a tree that he plucked out of the ground to counter Vajradamtra’s sword assault. After some rounds of combat, the opponents fell on their knees exhausted. After heaving a few heavy breaths, Angad suddenly lunged forward and grabbed Vajradamstra’s sword and in one swift motion chopped off his head.

Next to fall was Akampana when Hanuman hammered a rock on his head. He hardly put up a fight against the incomparably strong Hanuman.

The only rakshasa general to probably pose some threat to the vanara army was Prahastha, the Lankan commander in chief. Incensed by the freaky deaths of his most powerful men, Prahastha entered the game in a manner most dynamic and destructive. His golden chariot wheels shone like twin suns. Sixty-four horned serpents drew the chariot which bristled with swords and harpoons. His blood red silk flag had an embroidered snake made of emeralds and a lion made of topaz. Prahasta flexed his muscles and rolled his bloodshot eyes. The north gate opened as he led out the veterans, the grand army of the rakshasas. The soldiers followed, with bells tied on their arms and legs.

He wreaked havoc in the vanara army, slaying thousands of vanaras. A torrential shower of arrows fell from all directions with each twang of his bow.

Seeing the damage he was causing, Nila stepped in. He uprooted a huge tree and faced Prahasta alone. He dodged the thousands of arrows aimed at him. He swung the tree forcefully and in a smack killed all of Prahastha’s chariot horses and destroyed his deadly bow. Prahastha leaped off his dismantled chariot and struck Nila’s chest with his club. Nila staggered a bit, but fortunately landed next to a huge rock. As Prahastha inched ahead to hit him more, Nila turned around just in time to batter his head with the huge rock. He died instantly. By midnight, the rakshasas were tearing their hair in shame. They returned leaderless to Lanka.

When Ravana heard of the demise of Prahastha, his most trusted man, he was crushed. It was painful to imagine a Lanka without his most able commander. Ravana donned his armor and decided to enter the battle arena. A massive army walked out of Lanka. This was the most equipped and well-trained army of all. Ravana stood on a golden chariot amid the huge army and rode his way into the battle ground. The rakshasa king indeed appeared impressive. For most of the vanaras, this was their first glimpse of the demon king. Rama saw him from close quarters for the first time, even though He had seen Ravana from a distance when Sugriva had jumped into have a little quibble with him. The moment Sugriva caught sight of Ravana, he rushed in like a moth rushes into fire. Seeing Sugriva race toward him with a huge boulder, Ravana shot an arrow that pulverized the rock. Another arrow hit Sugriva so hard that he collapsed to the ground, bleeding profusely. Six vanaras covered their king and picked up some boulders and tree trunks to attack Ravana. Ravana shot six arrows simultaneously, and each went through their chests, ripping open their hearts. The six vanaras fell instantly, inducing great fear among the vanara ranks. They began to run to seek refuge in someone who could save their lives.

A very furious Hanuman stepped in and challenged Ravana with the most hateful words. He reminded him of some painful memories that Ravana was trying to forget. The most important of them was to remind him of the killing of Akshay Kumar, the very dear son of Ravana. Ravana’s refreshed memories evoked more anger in him. He jumped out of his chariot, lunged forward and hit Hanuman hard on his chest. The powerful blow forced Hanuman to stagger backward. It just took the hero a moment to recover, and he retaliated by slapping Ravana powerfully. The strike was so hard that the sound reverberated across the battleground, attracting everyone’s attention. Ravana took a while to regain his balance and catch his breath. He was highly impressed by this monkey’s prowess. He clenched his fist and punched with all his might into Hanuman’s chest.118

As Hanuman took his time to recover, Nila jumped on Ravana. Flinging off the monkey, Ravana picked up his bow and began to shoot a steady stream of arrows in all directions. Whatever Nila picked up to use as an assault weapon, Ravana shattered it to pieces. Finally, unable to attack properly, Nila reduced his size drastically and with one jump reached on the flagpole of Ravana’s chariot. From that vantage position he began to strike the demon king in so many different ways. Ravana wasn’t able to catch him as he was so small, which was so irritating. So Ravana finally picked up a fire weapon and managed to corner Nila. Then catching hold of him, flung him to the ground. With Nila out of the way, Ravana focused on the more important things at hand.

Bad Leader, Good Leader

The rakshasas learned a harsh reality during the war. They always thought that their master Ravana loved them and cared for them. But as the war progressed, they realized that he only loved himself. As the vanara attack became unbearable, the rakshasas ran back to their leaders for protection. But Ravana stopped them from returning and forced them to restart the fight. He always reminded them that he had fed them for so many years and given them so many facilities, it was now time to return the favors by fighting until the end. He told them clearly that if they turned their backs in this war, he would chop of their heads himself. That’s when the rakshasas saw his true colors. He gave them so much comfort not because he loved them but so that he could use them when the time came.

On the other hand, the vanaras had a totally opposite experience in the war. There would be times when they would run away for fear of their enemies and hide behind Rama. He would not threaten them with dire consequences. Instead, He would call the vanara chiefs and request them to allow the retreating vanaras to recoup after fighting for long hours. He would take up the mantle and lead from the front and protect the returning vanaras from the enemies.

Ravana would compel everyone to fight for him, but he wouldn’t share the credit of the victories. He always felt that he was giving them an opportunity to show their prowess in war and earn a good reputation as warriors. He made it clear that he didn’t need them, instead they needed him.

On the other hand, Rama always gave the credit for all His victories to the vanara army. Even for the killing of legendary rakshasas such as Kumbhakarna or Ravana, He gave all credit to the vanaras. When the vanaras exclaimed that it was Rama who did everything and the only thing they had done was run away like cowards, Rama denied that and said that their initial fight with these demons tired them out, making it easy for Him to step in and kill them. Rama offered credit to His army even when He was responsible for it.

In fact, Rama was not only concerned about the soldiers of His army, but also those of the enemy. In the war between Rama and Ravana, Rama told Ravana to allow their armies to rest as they fought on. The armies on both sides got an opportunity to rest as the heads of both the camps fought. When that happened, the rakshasas realized that Ravana was too selfish to care for their welfare, but Rama was so magnanimous that He even cared for the well-being of the enemy’s army.

Even before he could think of where to begin, Lakshmana stood in front of him with his weapon raised. He began to shoot arrows at Ravana at astonishing speeds. Nonetheless, Ravana was dexterous enough to neutralize all those high-speed arrows. In retaliation, Ravana shot one single missile that hit Lakshmana on his forehead, causing him to swoon for a moment. The Raghu dynasty prince recovered soon, picked up his bow and shot an arrow that halved Ravana’s bow. Lakshmana’s bow was churning out arrows at the speed of the wind. Three of those arrows sliced through Ravana’s body, rendering him unconscious for a few seconds. When he regained composure, Ravana stood up on his chariot, picked up a sharp javelin and threw it at the younger prince with such force that the weapon pierced through his body. Lakshmana fell backward. The spear continued drilling through Lakshmana’s body and embedded itself into the ground. Ravana found this to be an opportune moment to end the war. He leaped off his chariot and reached the place where his opponent lay unconscious, impaled by the spear. His idea was to take the wounded and disabled brother captive. With two of Rama’s loved ones in his control, the war would be as good as over.

Sitting on his hunches, Ravana put his hand under Lakshmana in an attempt to lift him. Interestingly, Lakshmana was heavier than he had expected. It didn’t make sense. Judging by his dimensions, it was impossible that he would be so heavy that it was getting difficult for Ravana to haul him. Yet here was Ravana struggling to pick him up. Suddenly, he was rolling on the battlefield. A powerful kick dislodged him from what he was doing. The kick was so violent and damaging that he spewed blood from his mouth. He was so stunned for a few minutes that he was unable to get up. All he could hear were shouts and jeers of monkeys mocking him. While Ravana was still dealing with the pain of the kick, Hanuman picked up Lakshmana effortlessly and took him to a safe location for Sushena’s team to take care of his wounds. Interestingly, as soon as Hanuman picked up Lakshmana, the lance that had gone through his body, sprung out of his torso and zoomed back to Ravana’s chariot and positioned itself for reuse by its master.119

Ravana staggered up and walked to his chariot. The constant jeering by the monkeys annoyed him. He picked up his bow and sent a series of arrows to silence the particularly infuriating mockers. On seeing the vanaras in trouble, Rama stepped in. He was already very furious with Ravana for the state Lakshmana was in. Ravana clambered up his chariot as Rama mounted on Hanuman’s shoulders. This fight was unparalleled—thousands of arrows of all shapes and sizes zipping and zooming through the air from both directions. Finally, Rama shot an arrow that penetrated through Ravana’s armor and pierced his chest. His bow fell off his hand and he collapsed to the chariot floor. Rama cut off the flag of Ravana’s chariot and declared, “Go, Ravana. Take rest and return to this fight after you recover.” There was no anger in Rama’s voice, instead there was calmness no warrior could gather amid a fight. The words crushed Ravana’s pride. A shamed Ravana returned to his city. Every curse heaped on him in the past now began to echo in his ears as he re-entered his city. A certain fear that Ravana had never known in the past began to grip him. But amid all this, Ravana’s only joy was the fact he had brutally injured Lakshmana. The injury wasn’t a regular one as the weapon wasn’t regular.120

There was a crisis on this side, but a celebration on the other, a wail here and victory there. Ravana was happy and Lord Rama was sad. Lakshmana had been brutally attacked by Ravana’s fatal weapon— known as Shela—fashioned by Mayadanava. Though a cure was available, it would take a good few hours, even if Hanuman were to travel at the speed of lightning. Jambavan had warned Hanuman to return by sunrise at any cost. With sunrise, Lakshmana’s life would wane. The weapon had been programmed to create havoc in accordance with the movements of the sun, and sunrise would mark the beginning of its strongest effect and that would be impossible to endure. This knowledge had caused the rakshsas to celebrate and Ravana to smile despite the insult he had suffered. But it had caused great pain in the vanara army even though there was every reason to celebrate the victory of Rama over Ravana and the several insults he had to undergo in his very first fight sequence.121

Understanding the paucity of time, Hanuman made a mental note of the herbs that he was supposed to bring and leaped without further delay. As soon as he was airborne, Hanuman realized the catastrophe that awaited him. Impossible! How could this happen? The sun was rising at midnight!

From the altitude he was at, Hanuman gazed toward the Lankan palaces and could see Ravana laughing. Instantly, Hanuman could comprehend what had transpired behind this supernatural phenomenon. Ravana had dared to meddle with the systems of nature. This was his last trick to prevent Lakshmana from getting a chance to recover. Hanuman took a detour and instead of proceeding toward the mountains to find the life-saving herb, he soared toward the sun. As he was zooming ahead, a flashback of his childhood days hit his mind. He had taken this same route many years ago. Back then, he was eagerly rushing toward the sun to enjoy a fruit, and so many years later, history was repeating itself. But now, he was rushing to save a life. Back then, he thought about satisfying his own needs and now he lived to satisfy others’ needs.

As soon as Hanuman reached his destination, with one kick he dislodged Aruna, the charioteer of Surya, the sun god. Aruna was blown a few yojanas away. Surya was flabbergasted. What’s going on here? One look at Hanuman and he realized that this was the same person who had attacked him as a child. His anger transformed into fear now. With folded hands, he spoke to Hanuman.

“What do you want from me? Please don’t trouble me again. I am only doing my duties.” Hearing Surya plead this way, Hanuman spoke up.

“You are lying! How is it your duty to rise at midnight? How can you alter the laws of nature?” Surya was now in a fix. Perplexed, he peeped below and saw Ravana signaling him with a wave of his hands to rise fast. He begged Hanuman, “Please understand my predicament. I want to help Lord Rama in His mission. But I am so fearful of Ravana. No one can survive the wrath of that demon. All the gods shiver at his sight.”

Hanuman became thoughtful for a while. Suddenly, he smiled, “I got it! I have a great idea that can help both. Your name is Bhanu and my name is Hanu, so, both of us are alike. If you follow what I say, you can satisfy both sides aptly. Ravana wouldn’t be angry with you, and Lord Rama will be pleased with you.”

Surya became curious, “Tell me, O clever Hanuman! What should I do now?” With a naughty grin, Hanuman said, “Come closer, I will whisper my idea to you.”

Surya unwittingly came closer to Hanuman and bent low, bringing his ears close for Hanuman to whisper. Hanuman put his arms around Surya as if holding him to whisper into his ears. As soon as his arms were firmly fixed around the Surya’s neck, he tightened his grip and locked him in his armpit.

Surya struggled to escape his grip, but in vain. With the sun under his armpit, Hanuman began to fly toward the mountains. Everyone, including Ravana, who witnessed this was left dumbfounded. As Hanuman was flying, his armpit glowed with the sun’s effulgence. He managed to get the herbs and revive Lakshmana; now that time was his captive, it was needless to say that he was punctual. Surya, even with his pain and embarrassment, was happy. Fearing disgrace from Ravana, he had chosen to side with the wrong, but then he was forced to be on the side of the righteous by the virtue of another indignity.122

## Chapter 7

Annihilation and Rejuvenation

At times Ravana felt like a snake troubled by an eagle, at others like an elephant harassed by a lion. The angry face of Rama often flashed in front of his eyes. The wounds caused by Rama’s arrows hurt more than all the other wounds on his body. The numerous curses that had been heaped on him suddenly flooded back into his memory. He had made the one mistake of underestimating humans and monkeys while asking for a boon from Lord Brahma. While he had inoculated himself from every other type of being, he felt humans and monkeys were of no consequence and did not need to be even mentioned in the request. He had considered it below his dignity to ask Brahma for immunity from humans and monkeys, that too in front of his siblings.

Repentance made no sense now. This was the time for action. He had to muster all the courage, strength and strategy he had, to save his everything from the calamity that weighed heavily on him. He ordered the sealing of all ramparts and gates across the city. With Prahastha gone, Ravana took charge of the security himself. He needed his best men together now, and no one could be spared any comfort. Comfort reminded him of his brother Kumbhakarna who was snoring away peacefully in his relaxation zone. What was the point in having a powerful giant of a brother if all he did was sleep when his kingdom was falling apart?123

He ordered the wake-up team to rouse Kumbhakarna again. Just a few days back, he had woken him up for a quick consultation. Obviously after that session, there was no action at all for over a week. So sleepy Kumbhakarna went for a small nap. His catnaps lasted anywhere from a few days to a few weeks, while his regular quota lasted for six months. Waking him after his six months’ sleep was tough enough, but waking him in between was almost an impossible struggle. After the wake-up team managed to do their task, they often needed a few weeks’ rest, as their bodies would be sore from working so hard.

The wake-up team comprising hundreds of well-built rakshasa men walked into the huge cave within which Kumbhakarna snored away. Inlaid with diamonds and gold, the cave was cozy and opulent to the taste of Kumbhakarna but more than anything else, it was secluded and peaceful, away from the hustle-bustle of the city of Lanka. As he wheezed and snorted, his flared-up nostrils gushed hot air. His mouth was a gaping hell from which emanated the unbearable stench of rotten flesh. He wore the most opulent attire, although it was not exactly what anyone would wear to sleep. He was in all probability in a hurry to sleep and just didn’t have the energy to change before dropping off.124

The wake-up team piled up a mountain of cooked deer, buffalo and pig meat close to the face of Kumbhakarna such that the aroma would enter his nostrils and tempt him to relinquish his sleep. Next to the meat mountain was a huge barrel of fresh blood. Then a couple of them climbed atop Kumbhakarna’s torso and massaged it with sandalwood paste. They managed to drag up a massive garland to adorn his neck. They then sprayed perfume all over his body. Some stood on either side of his ears and began to eulogize his heroic acts. They burned incense all around the cave. They blew conches, clapped, beat drums, struck gongs and screamed in unison. They poked him with spears, clanged maces and even pounded him with hammers and fists. Even with all these simultaneous disturbances, Kumbhakarna hardly stirred.

They called the special forces of the rakshasa army. Ten thousand men were reeled into the wake-up mission. They surrounded him from all directions and begin to create a ruckus. They then involved animals—elephants, horses and camels—to climb over him and trample him. Then a few men began to clobber his knees and elbows with massive pillars and logs of wood, even as some hit these delicate joints with heavy clubs. When nothing woke him up, they became really furious. They tried a series of completely unorthodox ideas to awaken the hulk—tearing out his hair, biting his ears, pouring pitchers of cold water into his ears, placing boulders on his forehead, striking with huge hammers, tying his hands and legs with ropes and pulling it with force that could rip off his limbs. Kumbhakarna finally moved. The moment his sleep broke, his nostrils whiffed aromatic food, which inspired him to open his eyes a bit. The first thing he saw was a huge roasted buffalo. Immediately, his eyes opened more and he began to salivate. He sat up, dusting everything off his body, including the animals. He yawned incessantly, forcing fluctuations in the air pressure within the cave of his mouth. He sat up and grabbed as much meat his hands could hold and shoved all of it into his mouth. He then gulped down two thousand pitchers of blood and wine.125

When they felt that he had sufficiently gratified himself, the demons approached him carefully. Offering their respects, they began to converse with him. Kumbhakarna yet again wanted to know the reason for this untimely provocation. He warned them that it had better be for good reason. When they spoke about the crisis in Lanka and the humiliation his brother had to recently face in war, his eyes rolled. Anger consumed him, and he wanted to run and destroy the whole host of restless monkeys right away. But the demons stopped him from venturing out directly to fight without reporting to Ravana. Kumbhakarna slowed down and instead decided to go talk to his brother.

As he entered the courtroom from the backdoor created only for his entry, an army of much smaller demons that had just managed to successfully wake him up followed him. Ravana gave him a brief rundown of everything that had happened from the time he had returned to sleep that fateful day when Vibhishan made an exit from Lanka. Too many men had been lost, including some of the most trusted and efficient. This was the time to cause some serious damage to the enemy. None could cause as much damage as Kumbhakarna, both physically and psychologically.

Pride Walks Alone

Ravana awakened Kumbhakarna when more than half his army was dead and gone. Every general from Lanka who went to war took an army along with him, but Kumbhakarna refused to take any army. He felt he was enough. Kumbhakarna represents pride. Pride is one such vice that prefers to stand alone. Kumbhakarna felt that All other anarthas or vices need the company of other vices, but if he took an army along, he would have to share the credit of victory with them. He preferred to take all the credit. Pride is enough to wipe out all the credits of the good you have done in your life.

As soon as the giant demon entered the battlefield, panic gripped the vanaras. At each step, he caused irreparable damage. Without even his knowing, he kept crushing hundreds of vanaras under his feet. Besides, his repulsive body odor permeated the entire area.

Pride Goes Before a Fall

When Kumbhakarna approached the vanara army, the vanaras in their excitement did not wait to hear from Vibhishan about the attack strategy. They attacked him prematurely. Many spiritual practitioners are like the vanaras. They become easily excited and try to fight against pride on the strength of their own spiritual practices. Just as the unguided vanaras had very little impact on Kumbhakarna, these spiritual practitioners have very little impact on the mountain of pride. Just as Kumbhakarna began to eat up the vanaras, pride when awakened tends to eat up the spiritual practitioners.

The vanaras began to run hither-tither to save themselves from this giant. Watching the giant trample the vanaras like ants, Rama wanted to find out who he was. He walked up to Vibhishan and inquired. Vibhishan said, “The giant with a spear in his hand, creating havoc is my brother Kumbhakarna. He has been hungry since his birth. Right from the moment he was born, he began to eat anything and anyone in his vicinity. During the fight with the demigods, Kumbhakarna had pulled out the tusk of Indra’s elephant Airavata with his bare hands and struck it into Indra’s chest. When Indra was hurt badly, he rushed to Lord Brahma to seek refuge. When Brahma heard that Kumbhakarna was on an eating spree, gulping down as many living beings as possible, he realized that if this giant wasn’t checked, he would consume the whole world. Thus Brahma cursed him to eternal slumber. When he pleaded, Brahma agreed to dilute the power of that curse. He could now sleep for six months and then wake up for a single day. In that one day, he was free to eat as much as he could eat. No matter how much he ate, his hunger was never satiated. Once that day ended, he would return to sleep and use the next six months to digest everything.”126

This graphic description helped Rama makes sense of the unbelievable obliteration He was witnessing. Kumbhakarna was gobbling up the vanaras by the dozens. Six hundred bow lengths in height and with large ears, the wreck-causing demon was indeed a sight. The vanara army was in so much terror that it had become impossible to formulate any attack strategy. His thunder-like roars shattered the tiny hearts of the vanara soldiers. Angad had to put in great effort to stop them from running away in all directions. Only when he told them to consider the giant as a big toy that moved did they halt. When they stopped running, he said, “Collective effort can pull down any big toy.” With that said, Angad decided to lead a multipronged attack on Kumbhakarna with a plethora of vanaras stationed around the demon with huge trees and boulders. The idea was to attack simultaneously from every single direction. Accordingly, they smashed so many things so forcefully into the demon from all sides, but he was unfazed and the trees and rocks smashed into smithereens instead. With one single sweep of his spike, he managed to kill a thousand vanaras.

Seeing the magnitude of the destruction, Hanuman stepped in and picked up a massive rock. Flying up above the demon’s head, he threw the stone with great intensity. The stone smashed against the skull of the demon and burst into powder. That power-packed smash caused the demon to stagger a little backward and caused some bleeding on his forehead. Other than that, nothing really happened to the hulk. Kumbhakarna recovered quickly and struck Hanuman with his lance. The unexpected blow took Hanuman by surprise, and he vomited out blood. The army was surprised to see their most superior member, their hero beaten so badly. With Hanuman temporarily out of his way, the giant was all out to destroy. Although he was dropping the monkeys into his mouth constantly, they were very alive even when inside. He had so many to eat up that he didn’t have to time to chew on them. Some managed to escape through his nostrils, yet others managed to find a way to wriggle out of his ears.

As the vanaras clamored to safety, Angad stepped in with a huge mountain and hurled it at the giant and rushed away. The mountain hit Kumbhakarna on his head and was in pieces. Almost as a reflex, Kumbhakarna threw his spear toward Angad. Angad quickly side stepped, missing the spear by a whisker. The furious vanara prince jumped up and pounded the giant on his chest. The demon staggered momentarily. Shaking his head, he slapped Angad so hard with the back of his hand that the vanara prince flew far away and flopped to the ground unconscious. He then turned his attention to the vanara king Sugriva. Yanking off the spear that had missed Angad and embedded into the ground, Kumbhakarna sped toward Sugriva. Noticing the oncoming demon, the alert Sugriva ran at him with equal speed carrying a boulder. Timing his jump to perfection, Sugriva leaped and threw the rock at his chest. On collision, it shattered to powder, much to the dismay of Sugriva and the vanaras. When the dust of that powder settled, Kumbhakarna’s rage was visible in his bloodshot eyes.

Roaring with his mouth wide open, Kumbhakarna hurled his spear at Sugriva. As the spear was rushing toward the stunned Sugriva, it broke midair. Everyone was shocked at the speed of that intervention. Only when they saw Hanuman fall on the ground did they realize his involvement in the act. He had jumped and broken off the spear midair with his knees. It was not an ordinary spear. It weighed twenty thousand tulas and no one except Kumbhakarna could even lift it. Yet Hanuman broke it in midair with one kneestrike. This was probably the highest insult for the demon. His fury now had reached the pinnacle. Pulling out a massive tree from its roots, he swung around and whacked Sugriva with it. The vanara king was thrown with great force toward a rocky mountain and his back cracked with the collision. He totally knocked unconscious at the base of that mountain, leaving the vanara army in utter shock. Even before anyone could recover and do anything, the giant bent over and picked up Sugriva’s unconscious body. Turning around, he began to walk away from the battlefield.

Panic struck the monkeys when they saw their leader being carried away by the enemies. Although they wanted to assist their master, none had the courage to challenge the gigantic demon. When Hanuman saw the state of affairs, he was very tempted to intervene and set things right. But then he realized that it was also the question of Sugriva’s reputation. If he rescued Sugriva in front of all these vanaras, it would almost be an insult to Sugriva’s valor. Everyone would then doubt his capability. Hanuman decided to wait and see if Sugriva was able to find a way out himself.

Pride Surrenders to God

Just as Kumbhakarna represents pride, Sugriva represents knowledge. If Kumbhakarna can defeat Sugriva, then pride has the ability to defeat knowledge. In fact, Kumbhakarna kept Sugriva under his armpit and walked away, indicating that knowledge when subjugated by pride becomes useless. When Sugriva realized that he was tucked under Kumbhakarna’s armpit, he thought that he might not be able to kill pride, but he could at least maximize the damage. He struck Kumbhakarna at his weak points—his ears and nose. The weak points of pride are ears and nose. The proud Kumbhakarna did not want to return to Lanka with his ears and nose damaged as people would laugh at him. The nature of a proud person is that he is concerned with what others will think of him. When Kumbhakarna returned to kill the vanaras, Rama stepped ahead and kept the vanaras under His protection. Pride can be defeated only when god steps in front of the spiritual practitioner and slays it.

Meanwhile, Sugriva regained some of his consciousness and could feel himself tightly held in the giants’ grip. Suddenly a shower of cool water rained on him and helped him get out of the exhaustion. As Kumbhakarna reentered the city carrying the monkey king, the citizens of Lanka welcomed their hero by showering scented water mixed with flower petals. Kumbhakarna had made the mistake of tossing Sugriva on his shoulder, assuming he was still knocked out. As he lost his focus to the praise and adulation, Sugriva took advantage and climbed up the shoulder of the giant and reached up to his ears. With all his strength, he bit off the giant’s ears. Blood gushed out and caused the monster much pain. Then Sugriva jumped off the shoulder and landed on the nose and bit it off, too. The agony that ensued was unbearable. Kumbhakarna brushed off the vandalizing monkey. Holding on to his bleeding ear and nose, he began to howl profanities at the terrible pest. That was enough time for Sugriva to rush back to the safety of his army.

Kumbhakarna was maddened with rage. He couldn’t bear the insult and the pain caused by the haughty monkey. He marched back toward the battleground and began to massacre the monkeys in great numbers. He wasn’t even bothered about eating them up now, he just wanted to get rid of a whole bunch of them, possibly, annihilate the entire monkey race. The whole race had to pay for the folly of their king. As he was exterminating the monkey forces by the thousands and hundreds of thousands, seven arrows pierced into his body, stopping him in his tracks. Standing in front was Lakshmana with seven more arrows mounted on his bow, taking aim once again. Lakshmana’s arrows soon pierced Kumbhakarna’s chest. Kumbhakarna was now only interested in putting an end to this battle once and for all. Brushing aside the younger brother, he moved toward Rama. Obviously, if he put an end to Rama, there would be no fight nor reason to fight.

He picked up a massive iron hammer to finish off Rama. When Rama noticed the demon moving toward him, dragging that heavy hammer, He began to shoot arrows rapidly at the giant. Those very arrows that could destroy the likes of Vali and Viradha in one moment didn’t even slow down this monster. The demon edged closer with his hammer, every step causing great casualty. As he walked toward Rama, he stepped over many monkeys and crushed them to death. When he was close enough, he suddenly swung his hammer with great force. As the hammer edged toward Rama, He knocked on an arrow called vayavyaastra. He timed his shot to perfection. As the hand that held the hammer came in perfect position, the arrow hit it at the right spot, lopping it off the body. Once Kumbhakarna was dismembered, the hammer lost momentum and came crashing down. It was about one inch away from Rama’s feet now. It took a moment for pain to shoot up through Kumbhakarna’s body and for the blood to gush out. That’s when he realized that he had actually lost his hand. The loss of his limb angered him so much that he rushed toward Rama to smack him with his other hand. On his way, he uprooted a palm tree and was about to hit Rama when suddenly the second arm also fell off to the ground still grabbing the tree. The crashing arm flattened hundreds of monkeys and demon soldiers alike.

In a flash the ambidextrous Rama ran toward the howling demon drawing out two swords from His waist belt. With a single swish of both the swords, He sliced off both the feet of the demon. Without any feet, the colossal body of the rakshasa collapsed belly down to the ground. His painful screams reverberated across all directions far and wide. Although totally disabled, Kumbhakarna’s anger did not subside. He wanted to use whatever strength he had to somehow finish off Rama. Using his thighs, he wriggled his body forward. His mouth was a powerful weapon in itself. If somehow, he managed to get close enough to Rama, he could lunge at him and gobble him up.

Seeing the determined movement of the wriggling giant, Rama shot a volley of arrows and covered the giant’s mouth by sealing it completely. With his mouth cupped with so many arrows, not even a single sound spilled out.

Pride Dies a Lonely Death

The ears and nose are the centers of pride. When Sugriva cut off Kumbhakarna’s ears and nose, his ego was hurt. Before killing Kumbhakarna, Rama covered his mouth with a volley of arrows. Why did Rama do that? A proud person loves to hear his own praise, and if he does not find anyone that wants to praise him, he begins to praise himself. Thus Sugriva cut off his ears to prevent him from hearing praise, and Rama blocked his mouth to prevent him from speaking self-praise. Then Rama cut off his arms, which represent the comparative mindset that compares “me” with “you.” Now with the eager-to-hear-praise ears cut off, the enthusiasticto-speak-self-praise mouth blocked and the comparative-mindset arms cut off, pride dies slowly.

The loss of so much blood rendered him unconscious. While the vanaras celebrated the death of the deadly demon, Rama knocked another very powerful missile presided by Indra on to his bow. Shooting the arrow with great force, Rama severed the head of the rakshasa. The arrow carried the severed head with a mouth full of arrows all the way to the entrance gate of Lanka. The head crashed into the gate with such force that it broke into splinters along with the formidable rampart. The loud sound brought all the residents out on the street. The retreating rakshasa army also reached the gates to see the gathered crowd and the colossal head of their leader smashed into the ground like a cracked coconut.

Meanwhile, the headless body of the demon had rolled off over the cliff and fallen into the ocean. As the heavy gigantic body sank into the depths of the ocean, the sharks and fishes began feasting. The vanaras were spellbound by Rama’s display of supreme heroism in the battle against the giant. For a while, it had seemed as if the gorging giant would finish them all. While the vanaras celebrated, Lanka was mourning. No one even spoke. When news about the ill-fated incident reached Ravana, he couldn’t believe someone could actually kill his brother. He had witnessed his strength. Even the celestials shivered at the mere sight of his brother. Yet his head lay at the gates of Lanka, that too with an ear and a nose bitten off, and the mouth stuffed with thousands of arrows. What a horribly painful death!127

Enough was enough. This had to be dealt now with sternness. He would get a wildcard entry now before it was too late. The stupid monkeys would have no counter to what he had planned. Rama’s arrows would be able to nothing either. This was surely going to gain a high score for the rakshasas.

burned powder

A band of vanaras stood akimbo, rejoicing their success and discussing Rama’s heroism. The next moment, the whole troop screamed and were soon reduced to a heap of ashes. The vanaras watching from afar were shocked. How could flesh, blood and bones be condensed to ashes in less than a moment? This was the most insane thing they had ever seen. Suddenly, their answer walked out of the gate of Lanka stamping on the ashes as if to reassert himself. No one knew how to deal with this calamity. No weapons, no army involved. Just one creature!

He was trampling all over the battlefield, causing havoc. Every direction he looked, there were ashes. They had all heard of him, but no one knew him. Not even Vibhishan. He had just come from patalaloka, the netherworld. Not too long ago, Ravana had been there on the pretext of visiting Bali, the saintly king of the demons. But Ravana always accomplished more than one task on any visit. He had invited Bhasmalochana, the demon with a fiery vision.128

With the death of his brother Kumbhakarna, Ravana had gone mad with rage. He even started killing his own people to overcome his frustration. That was until this brilliant idea of calling Bhasmalochana to lead the army and launch an attack struck him. The next day, when the vanara army became aware of someone nondescript becoming the commander of the Lankan army, they exulted. They became overconfident that Ravana was now forced to appoint a non-important demon as chief of the army, because he had lost all his important generals.

They were at their wits’ end when they saw only one person walk out from the Lankan doorway that day. Something was wrong here— something terribly wrong. This monster walked out confidently toward the waiting vanara army. What was most surprising was that he was not only alone, but also blindfolded! Initially, the vanaras giggled, but as he came closer, the leather band he was wearing over his eyes became more and more visible. There was clearly a glow underneath the band. His eyes were glowing. That’s when realization dawned on the vanaras. Bhasmalochana—the one who could burn with his vision.129

Panic struck the whole vanara army. The vanaras began to run helter-skelter. No one wanted to be around when he removed his blindfold. Literally trampling each other, the vanara army began to retreat. The leaders were confused and did not know what to expect. Yes, they had understood the obvious, but what they didn’t understand was the not-so-obvious. Only one question was on everyone’s mind—What to do now? Just then, the inevitable happened. Bhasmalochana removed his blindfold. His eyes were shining like the noon sun. Whichever direction he glanced, there was a flash of light, a short scream, the smell of burning flesh and a heap of ashes. Soon, the battlefield was full of live burning bodies reducing to ashes in seconds. The ashes were not even hot. It seemed that, somehow, he had the ability to expedite the entire hours-long burning process to a few milliseconds.

As everyone witnessed the mass destruction, Hanuman began to run in a totally different direction. When everyone was turning to him with hope, why was he abandoning them and fleeing? When news of Hanuman running away from the battlefield spread, the vanaras lost all hope. The only one who smiled amid this chaos was Lakshmana. Because only he had seen Lord Rama whisper something into Hanuman’s ears.130

Suddenly, one vanara soldier shouted, “Look! Look where Hanuman has reached.”

All the other vanaras turned around to where this vanara was pointing. They were fascinated and curious despite the impending danger. What was Hanuman up to? Far away, they could see him climb up the fortress of Ravana. He had just jumped into the city. If he was running away, why was he running into the enemy camp? It was all so puzzling.

Suddenly, the vanaras saw their hero fly out of the fort holding on his back a huge plank of iron. As he was flying, he balanced the plank with one hand and tail. His other hand was navigating the flight. They couldn’t figure out what Hanuman was trying to do. Lord Rama and Lakshmana exchanged a knowing glance. Hanuman silently flew and landed right behind Bhasmalochana. By now, the vanaras were hiding behind rocks, trees, inside waterbodies—any possible place—to save themselves from the vicious glance of this demon. But more than their own safety, they were curious to know what Hanuman had in mind.

Hanuman’s tail shot up carrying that iron plank high into the airway above Bhasmalochana’s head. The tail expertly twisted and flipped the iron plank. Wow! What brilliance! Every vanara soldier that was attentive enough during that flip knew what was coming.

The tail began to lower the vertical iron plank in such a way that it came right in front of Bhasmalochana and blocked his vision. As soon as the plank landed on the ground, there was a classic blast.

KADABOOOM!

Bhasmalochana was now a heap of ashes. The vanara army cheered and came running toward their savior. Those who still didn’t understand what exactly happened turned behind to see what that iron plank was.

It was a mirror!

Hanuman had made Bhasmalochana see himself!131

father’s sons

Seeing their uncle pulverized into pulp and their father spiraling downward into a pit of shame, Ravana’s four sons decided to stand up to the occasion and avenge their uncle’s gruesome murder. Trisira lead the gang that consisted of Devantaka, Narantaka and Atikaya. Together the quadruple rushed out of Lanka with their respective armies to annihilate the monkeys. Each was extremely powerful and was an expert at conjuring various tricks in an ongoing fight. Tagging along with them were the formidable rakshasa heroes Mahaparsva and Mahodara. The six of them left along a trail of dead bodies as they stormed ahead cutting through the vanara phalanxes.132

Narantaka opened the fight with the vanara leaders by throwing a spear at Angad’s chest. To his shock and disbelief, the spear broke when it collided against his chest. Angad then ran at great speed and smashed Narantaka’s chest with his fist and broke all his ribs, killing him instantly. Seeing his nephew die a miserable death, Mahodara rushed at Angad along with Trisira and Devantaka. When Angad was being rounded up by three rakshasa leaders, he began to throw trees and boulders at them, but the three managed to neutralize all his attacks. Mahodara mounted on an elephant and attacked Angad first with a powerful club. The vanara prince struck the elephant with such a force that the beast dropped dead. He then broke one of its tusks and hurled it at Devantaka. The tusk pierced through his body and injured him badly. By then, Trisira began to hurt Angad with his piercing arrows. Seeing Angad cornered by three powerful warriors, Nila and Hanuman stepped in to protect their prince. Hanuman leaped toward Devantaka with his clenched fist and knocked him hard on his skull and cracked it open. At the same time, Nila shoved a huge rock into Mahodara’s head. Mahodara had just mounted another elephant carrier when the vanara sprang up to kill him. His body fell off the elephant and was crushed under the panicking beast’s feet.

Seeing the death of all his colleagues, Trisira became very upset and furious. He took on Hanuman with great anger. Drawing his sword, Trisira swung it so swiftly that it caused multiple cuts across Hanuman’s torso. This angered him so much that he punched Ravana’s chest so hard that the demon toppled off the chariot on to the ground. Hanuman jumped off the chariot after him and picked up Trisira’s sword, which had slipped off his hand. Catching him by his neck, Hanuman made the three-headed demon stand up. With one swift move, he severed all his three heads, ending his life immediately. At this point, Mahaparsva attacked a vanara leader named Rishabha. After a brief conflict, Rishabha brought down a spiked club on Mahaparsva’s head, crushing his teeth and eyes and killing him right then.

After all of them were finished, the last to step out was Atikaya. As soon as he entered, the vanaras began to run madly for they thought Kumbhakarna has somehow resurrected. Vibhishan explained to Rama and Lakshmana, “This second giant running amok is Atikaya, another son of Ravana and his wife Dhanyamalini. Like his father, Atikaya too has sufficient blessings of Lord Brahma that make him invincible and deadly. He probably is one of the only ones who is able to counter Indra’s thunderbolt missile with ease. If he is not taken down at the earliest, he has the potential to wipe out the vanara army.” Hearing this, Lakshmana flung into action. What followed was a volley of various types of missiles and arrows flying in both directions. To a large extent, both could not find ways to damage each other beyond just superficial wounds.

Finally, Lakshmana could hear a mystical whisper that revealed a secret that could put an end to the enemy. It was the voice of Vayu whispering the secret—Atikaya’s armor was impenetrable and no matter how many arrows were shot at him, they would bounce off that shield. “Use the invincible brahmastra that has literally no counter weapon,” the voice said. When Lakshmana invoked the brahmastra, Atikaya began to panic. As the ultimate Brahma weapon raced toward him, he tried to counter it with numerous arrows, spears, divine missiles, hammers and clubs. But nothing prevented the brahmastra from severing his head off his body. The severed head rolled on the dusty battleground, forcing the demon soldiers to retreat to Lanka.133

The news of the death of four of his sons and two of his ministers broke Ravana. He began to wonder if Rama was indeed Lord Narayana as many claimed. He was no longer sure of victory. Everything seemed bleak. As he sat brooding over the loss and the impending end, Indrajit reassured him. “Remember when we had conquered the heavens so effortlessly? Remember how in the first war, you had almost killed the two enemy brothers?” After a pep-talk with his father, Indrajit left to complete what he had begun on the first day of the war. Before venturing, he went into his secret, sacred altar and threw a goat into the sacrificial fire to please the goddess. Immediately, he became invisible as did his chariot.

No one saw where the arrows were coming from. No one saw who was shooting them. Indrajit was in the battlefield. He was not only powerful and skilled but also deceptive. Fighting with Indrajit wasn’t as easy as fighting with all the other demons in Ravana’s army. The ease with which he shot arrows, the monkeys stood no chance. It wasn’t just his ease, it was also his speed that made things difficult. Most often the monkeys didn’t even realize where the arrows were coming from until they were too close to them. Indrajit could become invisible at will. The morale of the vanara army was already low when they saw Indrajit enter the battlefield, but dropped to dangerously low levels once he invoked the supreme brahmastra.

The result of the invocation was a shower of millions of arrows in directions Indrajit pointed toward, with each arrow finding its mark and killing thousands of vanaras. Some of the most powerful vanara heroes succumbed these onslaughts. Soon Hanuman, Sugriva, Nila and Jambavan were rendered unconscious. Finally, Indrajit directed all the fervor of the celestial missile to Rama and Lakshmana. Rama quickly told Lakshmana that the only way to end this was to succumb to it. If Indrajit stopped, they could find a solution later, but he wouldn’t stop until he saw Rama and Lakshmana dead. The brothers decided to stay defenseless against the arrows. The arrows found their mark the chests of Rama and Lakshmana, leaving them unconscious. The brothers’ fall left the vanara army horrified.134

The now-rudderless monkey army lost all direction and were thrown into disarray—some ran to save their lives, some lost hope, some ran toward the fallen bodies of Rama and Lakshmana and some went searching for a savior. Taking advantage of the dismay, Indrajit struck again, slaughtering thousands of monkeys. Meanwhile, Jambavan had recovered and arrived at the scene of disaster. Someone had informed him about the brothers. As soon as he spotted the heroes, his first question was, “Is Hanuman alive?”135

Jambavan knew that Hanuman was the only one dependable during crises. Jambavan believed that as long as Hanuman was alive, hope was afloat. Hanuman could not hold back his tears upon seeing the comatose Rama and Lakshmana. But Jambavan’s confidence in him reassured him of a way out of this mess. Jambavan took Hanuman aside to share an action plan. He gave him details of four herbs found only in the Himalayas—the mrta-sanjivani, which could bring the dead back to life; the sailya-karani, which could extract embedded weapons and quickly heal wounds; the suvarna-karani, which could restore the natural skin tone, and samdhani, which could join fractured bones and severed limbs. Post instructions, Hanuman leaped toward the northern realms. Bringing the herbs on time was key. Hanuman knew that such impossible time-bound activities were more complex than impossible activities. He had to bring the herbs before sunrise the next day for Rama and Lakshmana survive.

As Hanuman flew rapidly toward the Himalayas, something caught his attention—a charming hermitage in the middle of a forest on a hill. He spotted some sages performing a fire sacrifice in the hermitage. Something compelled Hanuman to descend and meet the sages. The reasons for his descent were both personal and professional. He was thirsty from going without water for days during the battle. The glistening river flowing next to the hermitage seemed just the right place to quench his thirst. Moreover, he had to reconfirm his route. Jambavan had mentioned Dronachal, the specific region in the Himalayan range he would find the herbs.

Surely, these sages would be able to tell if he was flying in the right direction. Even before Hanuman could utter a word, a sage began to speak. “I am a seer of the past, present and future. I can see that you have come in search of the sanjivani herbs from the Himalayas to revive Lakshmana. Do not to worry. Rama will surely win the war against Ravana. Take a quick dip in the river and come back so that I can bless you with divine vision to help you differentiate the herbs from the plethora of plants there.”

Something did not feel right to Hanuman, though—this sage appeared to glorify Rama, yet indulged in self-praise. With this niggling doubt, Hanuman walked toward the river to quench his thirst. As he was bent low and cupped water in his right palm to drink it, a crocodile attacked him. Not wanting to waste any time, Hanuman continued drinking water with his right hand and fighting the crocodile with his left hand. In a few minutes, he was done with both. The crocodile was dead and his thirst quenched. In place of the crocodile now stood a beautiful apsara. She thanked him for liberating her from a curse and warned him about the fake sage he had just met. “He is the demon named Kalanemi sent by Ravana to delay you from getting the life-saving herbs,” she said.136

Armed with this new knowledge, Hanuman returned to the hermitage. The sage seemed shocked. How did he manage to outlive the crocodile? It did not matter. He had yet another idea. He called Hanuman closer to teach him the mantra that would impart the divine vision to find the herbs. Hanuman came closer but instead of hearing him, he punched him in his face, killing the fraud sage.

Thirst for Love and Devotion

Hanuman was thirsty. Kalanemi gave him a kamandalu (a water pot) filled with water. Hanuman replied that the water was not enough to quench his thirst. So Kalanemi sent him to the river.

The learning is that had Hanuman been satisfied with a little water, he would have been trapped by Kalanemi. Similarly, people with small thirst (desires) become trapped by fake saints who promise miracles. Some are thirsty for money, some for children, some for name and some for legal victories. Such small thirst can be quenched by a kamandalu of water. But Hanuman had big thirst and a big appetite. People with small thirst and small appetite are content with material things, but those with larger thirst and a larger appetite need bhakti and prem.

With that accomplished, Hanuman leaped toward his destination. When he arrived on the Dronachal mountains, a bigger, more complex challenge awaited him. The mountain was filled with millions of herbs. To make matters worse, they all looked similar. How on earth am I going to identify the right herbs Jambavan wanted me to procure? He thought hard but could not figure out how and realized that he could never tell the right herb from the wrong. He was convinced it wasn’t a good idea to select herbs. That’s when he realized the process would take him forever, and if he did carry it out, he couldn’t risk carrying back the wrong herbs.137

So he arrived at a very dynamic and unusual conclusion. Surely, the herbs were on this mountain itself. What if he carried the entire mountain, then there would be no risk of wrong herbs. He then did the impossible—he picked up the entire Dronachal mountain and holding it on his right palm, he took a leap toward the southern regions. A huge mountain flying in the air, held at its base by a huge Hanuman.

Hanuman was determined to reach on time and save Lakshmana’s life. After much calculation, he realized he had sufficient time to make it back much before the scheduled time. Just as he was becoming proud of his timing, something hit his chest. Suddenly, he found himself being dragged downward at great pace. He had lost control of his flight and some mystical force was now dragging him down. He landed on the ground with a thud and the mountain slid away from his grip, parking itself on an open ground close by.

Hanuman caught his balance and shook his head to stabilize himself after the fall. He looked around and noticed that he was in the outskirts of the city. Although this place was not too far from the city, it had a rural atmosphere. He noticed someone walk toward him. He was shocked to see the person he was gazing at had an uncanny resemblance to Lord Rama. Carrying a formidable bow, the mysterious person walked up to him. With a serious look on his face, he asked, ‘Who are you and why are you passing over Ayodhya carrying such a huge mountain.” The mere mention of the word Ayodhya brought clarity to Hanuman. Instantly he knew the person was none other than Bharata, Lord Rama’s brother. Hanuman immediately bowed down with great respect and introduced himself to be the servant of Lord Rama. Bharata was very pleased to meet an associate of the brother he hadn’t met for more than thirteen years now. He began asking Hanuman innumerable questions about his brothers and their whereabouts. Hanuman explained to him that he didn’t have enough time to answer his questions now as Lakshmana’s life was now in danger and he had to return immediately with the mountain before it was too late. He purposely chose not to mention that Rama also was in the same state as it would become unbearable for Bharata.138

Bharata understood Hanuman’s predicament. Even then there was something that Bharata wanted Hanuman to do before he left—convey the news of Lakshmana’s fall to Sumitra, the mother of Lakshmana. The soft-hearted Bharata could never muster enough courage to convey bad news to anyone. He felt he had already hurt everyone enough in Ayodhya simply by being born as the son of Keikeyi. He cajoled Hanuman to Sumitra’s palace in Ayodhya. When Hanuman conveyed the news to Sumitra, her reaction stunned him. Instead of crying at the loss and the possible death of her son, she had a totally different approach toward it. She told Hanuman that no matter what happened to her son, service to Lord Rama must never stop. If one son was dead, Sumitra told Hanuman, he could take her second son Shatrughna along with him to serve Lord Rama in place of Lakshmana. Hanuman was amazed. Lakshmana and his mother both lived in a mood of sacrifice. Both were ready to sacrifice anything for the service of Lord Rama. He had never seen such dedication in his life.

Mothers’ Love

Another version of the story says that when mother Kaushalya heard about this news, she reacted very differently. She told Hanuman, “I am older than Sumitra. Here is my instruction to you. Do not give Sumitra’s message to Rama. Instead tell Him that He has to return with Lakshmana and Sita exactly as He had left with them. If He is unable to bring them back, He need not return to Ayodhya.”

Promising Sumitra that her son would be safe and that the service to Lord Rama would continue unobstructed, Hanuman walked out of Ayodhya. With the Dronachal mountain in his hand, he shot up to the sky and flew southward with renewed enthusiasm and determination.

When the silhouette of Hanuman carrying the massive mountain on the palm of his hands appeared in the sky, the entire monkey army applauded and cried. Their hero had come back. His superheroic actions continued to amaze them. He had gone to bring some herbs and here he was returning with a mountain. As soon as Hanuman neared the battlefield, the fragrances of the various herbs wafted heavily in the air. As soon as the aroma reached Rama and Lakshmana’s nostrils, they regained consciousness.

Not only Rama and Lakshmana, but also every slain vanara woke up as if from a deep slumber. In fact, the vanaras had a policy of gathering their dead soldiers and keeping the bodies in one area for an honorable cremation once the war was over. As soon as the mountain arrived, even these heaps of dead vanaras were revived.

The cause of celebration in the vanara army became the cause of frustration in the rakshasa army! Ravana had disposed of the bodies of dead demons into the ocean just so that the enemies would be unable to estimate the losses and would always be clueless. What seemed to be a master strategy until now seemed foolish upon the revival of the dead vanaras.139

Honoring the Dead

Vedic culture teaches us to respect everyone, including the dead. The vanara army in that spirit preserved the bodies of their soldiers to give them a respectful cremation. That respectful act proved to be wise when Hanuman brought in the Dronachal mountains with the healing life-restoring herbs. Whereas the policy of the rakshasas to dispose the dead bodies disrespectfully into the sea proved a disaster.

Rama and Lakshmana woke up, and seeing each other back to life was such great joy. With great love they embraced each other.140 Lakshmana was Rama’s hand, an integral part of Rama, not different from him. Rama was so happy to regain his association. How could he sufficiently reciprocate everything Hanuman had done and was doing for him? This personality was indeed very special. Right from the moment Rama saw him first in Kishkinda, He knew that Hanuman was special and that he would play a special role in His life. Rama was so grateful to Hanuman. This time it was not about just saving His life or that of His brother. Every single vanara that had ventured into this project was revived from doom. Whenever He saw the demons kill them mercilessly, Rama felt bad seeing the innocent die. Seeing everyone return to life, brought tears to Rama’s eyes and a gratitude toward Hanuman in His heart.141

Indeed, Hanuman had done what none could. The direction of the battle was about to change, and now it was time to pull out all stops for the end….

## Chapter 8

Deception and Destruction

The war had reached a crescendo. Ravana had lost his most important men, and all but one of his sons had died. This was now a do-or-die situation.142

His heroic brother was butchered in the most gruesome way. To top off his misery, every single vanara that had died over the past few days of the war was resurrected by the power of the herbs that Hanuman had procured from the northern mountains. With the revival of the entire vanara army came great confidence and power. They rushed to the gates of Lanka together, venturing further into the battleground. Their mind was free of barriers. They had no desire to follow the regular rules of warfare either. If Indrajit could use deception, why care for rules? This was the last leg of the war, and there was no holding back. The vanaras were determined to put in their all and eliminate every evil force in Lanka.143

The raid of Lanka began. When the restless monkeys stormed the city, chaos reigned in all directions. Hundreds of these rampaging raiders swooped down on every rooftop, street and palace corner. Hundreds more were even hanging from the trees and lampposts across the city. They slid down domes of palaces and terraces swinging from one place to another. The citizens were mortified. Although the monkeys left the citizens unharmed, their erratic movements scared them. When Ravana realized where the whole army was headed, he began to lose control. The reckless monkeys were rushing to the center of Lanka—his palace.144

Ravana unleashed the last seven powerhouses at his disposal—the most powerful and trusted generals left in his army—sending them in seven different directions to deal with the monkeys. He had many more soldiers, but no leaders left. Ravana was hoping these seven leaders would rally the army and make a strategy to protect the royal palace at the very least, if not the whole city. But the casualties began right at the door. As soon as Kampana stepped out the door, Angad who was already waiting there, pounced on him, twisting his head and killed him instantly. Even before he realized who had attacked him, Kampana was dead. Prajanha, who was just behind, rushed ahead to attack Angad with a raised axe, but the vanara prince lopped off his head. Even before they achieved any goals, two of Ravana’s seven leaders were dead and gone.145

In another direction, two other rakshasa leaders walked out to meet two of the most formidable vanaras. Sontaksha wrestled with Dvivida, and Yupaksha hurled himself at Mainda. Within a short spell, Dvivida tore open Sontaksha’s face with his nails. When the agonized demon was screaming in pain, he threw him on the floor and crushed his skull with his knees. Meanwhile, Mainda had managed to lock Yupaksha in a tight deadlock and squeezed the life out of him. Once he confirmed that the demon had indeed died, Mainda flung away the carcass.

As these two demons were about to meet horrible deaths, Kumbha was informed, but by the time he arrived to their rescue, he saw only their discarded corpses. He attacked both Mainda and Dvividha, who were no match for the strength of the angry son of Kumbhakarna. Just then Angad came to their rescue. Kumbha wasn’t fighting like the regular rakshasas. He wasn’t only doing his duty as a warrior, he was also seeking revenge. He had seen his father’s mangled head. When Angad jumped on Kumbha, all his pent-up anger exploded. He began hitting Angad ferociously and incessantly and beating him to pulp. The battered Angad fell unconscious. He would have died had Kumbha hit him a few more times. Fortunately for the vanara prince, Sugriva stepped in at the right time. He hurled a powerful mace that cracked the skull of Kumbha, distracting him. The blow was so intense that Kumbha struggled to stand up. Having seen Angad in such a state, Sugriva felt it necessary to attend to him than to continue this fight. So, he told Kumbha to return home and tend to his wounds before returning to fight.

Kumbha was in no mood to return home. There was no going back. He came knowing that he may not survive and had decided to fight until he had killed every monkey in the army. Leaving Angad comatose, Kumbha jumped at Sugriva. Gathering all his power, Kumbha squeezed Sugriva. Sugriva used all his strength to break free from the tight clasp. Before Kumbha could gather his senses, Sugriva rammed and pushed the heavy demon into the water moat. Even before Sugriva could move away from there, Kumbha bounced back. The raging Kumbha pounced on Sugriva. Both the powerful heroes fell to the ground with Kumbha on top. He began to pound on Sugriva’s chest with his fists. Sugriva’s armor broke, and blood gushed out of his wounds profusely. Using his legs, Sugriva managed to dislodge the demon mounted on him. Rolling off, Sugriva gathered all his strength and jumped right on the chest of Kumbha and pounded it open. That finished the struggle. Kumbha lay lifeless in a pool of his own blood.146

At yet another gate was engaged the second son of Kumbhakarna, brandishing a club and beating to pulp hundreds and thousands of vanara soldiers. One smash of his club, and the vanaras were no more. When he heard about his brother’s elimination, he rushed there immediately. When he saw the body of his dear brother, rage consumed him and he began to hit everyone around furiously, causing immense bloodshed. Hanuman stepped forward as Nikumbha swung his club which led to it landing square on Hanuman’s chest. When that happened, instead of Hanuman’s chest shattering, his club shattered to smithereens. Nikumbha was shocked. Hanuman clenched his fist and smashed his chest. The demon staggered in pain. Recovering quickly, he mounted another attack on Hanuman. But Hanuman was too quick for him. He swung just in time to avoid the punch. Before Nikumbha could recover his balance, Hanuman locked his arms, hauled him up and flung him to the ground. The very next moment, he landed on his chest with great force, simultaneously crushing his ribs and twisting his head off his torso to finish him.

The last to enter was Maharaksha, the son of Khara. He did not want to waste time dealing with all other vanara leaders. He pulled up his chariot straight to where Rama was. Of course, by the time he reached there he managed to leave behind a trail of corpses. Although he put up a decent fight, he wasn’t any good in comparison to Rama. Killing the son was much easier than it had been to kill his father Khara in Janasthan. Once Maharaksha collapsed, the rakshasa side was in deep trouble. They had lost every single leader in their camp. The only two that were left alive were the invincible father-son duo. The palace had been surrounded from all directions now. The two heroes had only two options. Fight and die or surrender and live. Of course, those two would have been the most obvious options. But considering it was the evil Ravana and the trickster Indrajit, it was evident they weren’t about to give up so soon. It was a question of their reputation. Ravana knew that Indrajit performed best in high-pressure situations. And this was the highest pressure possible. There had been times in the past where they had been this close to losing battles and their very lives too, but Indrajit’s shrewdness had saved them then. Ravana hoped it would save them now, too.147

trickster’s fraud

The vanaras were shocked. There was absolute disbelief everywhere. Indrajit had come out of the palace on his grand chariot. But that was not what had caused the disbelief. What had been the surprise trigger was that he wasn’t alone. Someone else on that chariot had caused the confusion. Standing with Indrajit on that chariot was Sita. Yes! He had brought Sita with him. No one could understand what he was up to. Was he returning Sita to Rama? Was that the purpose? Was this Ravana’s way of declaring truce?148

As they were speculating, Indrajit did the unthinkable. Right in front of their eyes, he drew out a dagger and in one quick swipe, slit Sita’s throat. Blood gushed out, Her eyes rolled up and She collapsed on the chariot itself. One single word escaped from Her lips before She went out of sight—Rama! A dirty grin stayed plastered on Indrajit’s face throughout the ordeal. It was as if he was teasing them with pain. The goal of every war was to agonize the enemy. Everyone who was a witness to that gruesome act was shocked beyond belief. They saw Sita die right in front of their eyes. Even before they could do anything at all, he had killed Her. Every single one of them was traumatized.149

Having accomplished what he wanted to, Indrajit returned to the security of his palace and shut the doors behind him. He knew that for some time there would be no action from the enemies. It would take them some time to assess the reality. Meanwhile, he had something very important to do. The drama that he had just enacted was simply to buy some time for what he was about to do.150

When Rama was informed of Indrajit’s horrendous act, He collapsed. He couldn’t handle the news. Lakshmana fell on his knees and held his brother’s unconscious body over his lap. Someone handed gave him some water, which he sprinkled over the serene face of Rama. When He woke up, He was in tears. Vibhishan rushed to Rama upon hearing about the public execution of Sita by Indrajit. Dismissing it as an impossibility, Vibhishan warned that it was a ploy to distract. Surely, Indrajit was planning something big for which he had possibly bought time with this act of killing of Sita. Even the thought of that possibility gave everyone hope.151

Confirming Truth

Another version of the story is that Hanuman could not bear to see this. He lifted an enormous boulder and rushed at Indrajit, with all the monkeys following him. A sharp and furious encounter followed in which Indrajit disappeared from the field. Hanuman immediately realized that this whole thing was Indrajit’s trick to subdue Rama. He immediately took the form of a bee and went to Ashok vatika where he saw Sita with Her head in Her hands and became convinced that She was alive. He instantly returned to Rama and gave Him the happy news.

Rama stood up with renewed optimism. What could Indrajit need so much time for? Vibhishan didn’t have an answer, but he knew where to get that answer. Rama got a team of His best men together— Lakshmana, Hanuman and Vibhishan. “You have full freedom to do the needful and put an end to the one who has caused such emotional and physical pain.”

Vibhishan chalked out a plan. Whatever the secret was, it was hidden in Nikhumbila, Indrajit’s sacred place of worship. Indrajit spent a considerable amount of time there every single day. That place was indeed the secret of all his powers. Whenever he needed to become even more powerful, he would perform a sacrificial ritual to please goddess Nikhumbila. His rituals were so elaborate and perfect that the goddess was pleased and granted him any boon he requested. To stop him, they had to stop the ritual from culmination. When Indrajit began the ritual, he seldom left it in between. The only way to force him to leave the ritual was to orchestrate chaos— and vanaras were chaos experts.152

Armed with a team of the most impish and dangerous combatants of the vanara clan, Vibhishan led the way with Hanuman, Lakshmana and ten of the toughest vanara warriors following him closely. They had discussed the plan thoroughly. The rest of the army was instructed to carry on the attacks and engage the enemy in a combat with renewed intensity. Accordingly, the vanaras focused on executing the cover operation to perfection. In all different directions in the city, they caused raids and destroyed the security measures through surprise attacks typical of forest raiders. While the rakshasa army was distracted, the special mission team was edging toward their destination taking an underground trail that very few in Lanka even knew about.

This was supposed to be the escape route for Indrajit in case of any such surprise attack. Vibhishan used it as the entry route to enter Nikhumbila with his troops. The tunnel began right in the middle of the city of Lanka, very close to the exit gate behind the palaces. This was to the extreme south of Lanka. Since it was supposed to be an escape route, it was strategically designed to open closest to the gates to enable a smooth escape from the Lankan premises into the wilderness. The tunnel was many miles long and it traversed underground pretty much through the city of Lanka until it took a sharp turn that led toward the valley that was at the far corner of Lanka. Although while trudging inside they had no idea where they were headed. Only Vibhishan knew the map of the trail.153

Finally, they found the exit door that would lead them to their destination. They braced themselves for the outbreak. Hanuman kicked the door with full force, breaking it into splinters. Two guards stationed just outside the door were flung by the sheer force of the kick. When Hanuman stepped out of the splintered doorway into Nikhumbila, the guards were shocked at that sudden entry. They didn’t even know that a door existed there. A layer of rock that blended into the terrain covered it very astutely. When the door shattered, the rocky layer fragmented, exposing a gapping tunnel. Stepping next to Hanuman was Lakshmana, with his potent bow, and on the other side stood Vibhishan, with his inseperable golden mace. The ten other vanaras aligned themselves behind the deadly trio. The rakshasa soldiers braced for an arduous battle.

Although they were just thirteen, they moved at the speed of a thousand. With just one punch, they could kill. Hanuman was killing a hundred at one go, while Lakshmana was shooting arrows at the speed of the mind. Each arrow penetrated through at least four rakshasas, killing all of them instantly. And he shot several arrows at a time. As they moved through Nikhumbila, they left a heap of dead bodies at every turn. Nikhumbila was by no means a small area. It extended to miles in each direction. Speed was of utmost important in this mission. That was precisely why Vibhishan chose this team. While Hanuman and the rest continued the massacre, Vibhishan pulled Lakshmana away. They had more important matters to focus on. The vanaras were more than enough to handle the onslaught of rakshasa men.

The sanctum sanctorum of Nikhumbila was quite a distance away. Vibhishan knew that Indrajit would soon learn of the infiltration into his sacred haven. He also knew that Indrajit would rush out and deal with the calamity himself. That would give them a chance to sneak into the inner sanctum and do the needful. As soon as they reached the sanctum sanctorum through a secret passage, they found Indrajit storming his way out to deal with the messy situation at the entrance. Taking advantage of Indrajit’s absence, Vibhishan and Lakshmana stealthily entered the sacred worship house. Before the guards stationed there could raise an alarm, they were quickly silenced. When they were sure that there was no more scope of intrusion and of their entry being relayed to Indrajit, the two busied themselves with their plan. The plan was simple—make the entire ritual impossible to complete. Considerable effort had gone toward procuring all the ingredients for that final sacrifice that would make Indrajit immortal and undefeatable. Creation was difficult, but destruction was easy. In a matter of minutes, the two of them had rendered the possibility of a sacrifice impossible. After messing up the whole place, they coolly sat there waiting for the prince to come back.154

By this time, the vanaras should have made their exit. The idea was to continue fighting till Indrajit arrived. As soon as they spotted Indrajit, they had to back away and make a quick exit before he came too close. So, if everything worked well that much time would be enough for Vibhishan and Lakshmana to reach there and do the needful.

Indrajit was flabbergasted to see the vanaras retreat quickly as soon as he entered the scene. It wasn’t the normal trend of these monkeys. No matter what these monkeys did, they never retreated like that. And if they did then there had to be strategy behind it. Confused and bewildered, Indrajit returned to complete his most important sacrifice. He had already broken one rule of the ritual by walking away in between. He hoped that goddess Nikhumbila would forgive this lapse and grant him the boon. When he stepped into the sanctum sanctorum, a surprise awaited him. The whole place was in total disarray.
