Every single yajna ingredient was scattered. Every guard in the arena was dead, and the corpses were heaped in one corner. The brahmanas, who were assisting the yajna were all huddled up together near the deity of goddess Nikhumbila, were shivering in fear. They had witnessed the gruesome death of all the guards.

Seated right in the middle of the arena on a wooden seat was Lakshmana with his powerful bow clenched in his hand. A little distance away was his uncle Vibhishan leaning on a tree casually. His mace was placed on the ground leaning on the same tree, an arm’s distance away. An angry prince gritted his teeth glowering at his traitor uncle. He had always felt that his father had been too lenient with him and his treason simply proved his point. What he did till now was the height of betrayal. Bringing the enemy right into the nephew’s secret hideout and that too by creating a distraction was the worst way a person could let down his family and that too at the most crucial juncture of the war, when all else was lost. Indrajit began to hurl abuses at his uncle. “What kind of a man are you, Uncle? Don’t you have any shame? You are bringing the enemy to harm your elder brother’s favorite son. Don’t you have any responsibility toward the family? How can you be such a wicked fool? How do you not have any pride of your rakshasa birth? The very religious principles you speak so highly about will despise you for having let down your own family. By abandoning your family, you have become like a dog licking the feet of strangers. Even the worst of relatives is better than an outsider. You are a disgusting blot on the rakshasa race.”

Vibhishan didn’t even become angry with his barking nephew. He simply smiled knowing well that these lofty words were simply a ploy to buy time to think about a strategy to escape the sticky situation he found himself in. Of course he had valid answers to every allegation heaped upon him. Before the end, Vibhishan thought that it would be prudent to give his nephew some words of wisdom, something that his father had clearly failed to offer all his life. “Dear Nephew, the words “religious principle” coming from your mouth lose all gravity. If you are so concerned about religious principles, is this how a nephew should speak to his uncle? I agree that I am born into the rakshasa dynasty, but the reality is that I have no traits of a rakshasa. In fact, I hate impudence and violence, which are character traits of a first-class rakshasa. My understanding of the essence of all religious principles is that one should abandon the association of sinful people even if they are your closest relatives. There definitely cannot be a more sinful person than one who has forcefully abducted another’s wife. Three faults surely lead to destruction—mistrusting one’s friends, sexual relations with another’s wife and stealing another’s property. Having done all of these, Ravana is surely destined to be doomed and anyone connected with him will also be finished. You may speak a lot, but you are, simply put, an impudent little boy with no sense of right and wrong. Very soon you will be lying dead in front of the very goddess you worship. Today, even she won’t stand up for you as you have angered Lakshmana and Rama. The last offering to goddess Nikhumbila will be that of her devotee.”155

An arrow whizzed past Vibhishan and hit Indrajit on his shoulders very powerfully, embedding itself deep into his flesh. Enough words were spoken. Now was the time for action. Lakshmana did not believe in discussions with people with bad attitudes. Indrajit pulled the arrow out and broke it with one hand staring at Lakshmana in rage. He grinned remembering the time he had nailed him down with the Nagapasha. Even before Lakshmana could react, he had been pierced with seven arrows from Indrajit’s bow. Vibhishan had been covered with a hundred arrows from top to bottom. While he was disentangling himself, Indrajit shot so many arrows at Lakshmana that he seemed drenched in a shower of arrows. By the time he dealt with that scourge, Indrajit was gone. Vibhishan and Lakshmana hurried up to catch him with before he left Nikhumbila.156

Luckily, the vanara troop had returned and surrounded the chariotmounted Indrajit from all directions. But that little gap that he had carved out had given him enough time to send a message to the palace of Lanka and order for fresh reinforcements to be dispatched to Nikhumbila. Soon the rakshsas began to arrive in thousands. Vibhishan did not want to engage in combat with his nephew and thus he focused on battling the inflowing rakshasa army. As the sun set, the clashing forces closed in on each other and fought furiously. While Hanuman and Vibhishan were brandishing their maces and eliminating rakshasas in large numbers, Lakshmana cut off the head of Indrajit’s chariot driver. Indrajit now had to maneuver the chariot himself while still engaging in the fight. Seeing his predicament, four vanaras jumped on to his chariot from four different directions and smashed the chariot to pieces. Indrajit, because of having been forced to jump off the badly damaged chariot, was now in a compromised position. He directed all his anger at the monkeys who had just wrecked his favorite vehicle. Lakshmana jumped in, breaking Indrajit’s bow in half. Quickly picking up a spear that was fallen to the ground, Indrajit hurled it at his uncle with great strength. Lakshmana cut the spear into three parts with his neatly aimed arrows. When his spear was disabled, Indrajit became furious. He invoked a very mighty bow that suddenly appeared in his hand and shot arrow after arrow at Lakshmana. Every third arrow was a powerful missile. No matter what came his way, Lakshmana had a counter.

Finally Lakshmana knocked a powerful arrow awarded by Indra and offered a silent prayer to his lord and master Rama. That effulgent and potent arrow whizzed through the air like a meteor in the sky. Cutting off Indrajit’s head, the arrow carried it away, leaving the lifeless body on the floor of Nikhumbila. This was perhaps the greatest victory in the war thus far. Indrajit had proven to be the greatest obstacle and caused the greatest damage to their army. When the vanaras saw Indrajit fall, their joy knew no bounds.

The rakshasas froze seeing their leader dead. They never thought that it was even possible to kill the invincible Indrajit.

The vanaras massacred the shocked and paralyzed rakshasas in multitudes. A few rakshasas managed to escape, but when they went and shared this bad news with Ravana, he slaughtered them instantly.157

Meanwhile, Lakshmana had fainted due to exhaustion and Sushena had to administer herbs to restore him to full vitality. Rama was thrilled at the astounding success of the mission. This was a great step toward their ultimate goal. Now only one big hurdle on their path remained and that was the rakshasa king himself. The sun had set and an intensely adventurous day was over. No one on either side had any energy to engage in warfare anymore. Six long days had passed from the day the war had begun. Much had been gained and lost. The next day could prove to be the penultimate day of the battle. Lord Rama wanted the army to have as much rest as they could before the end game.

final battle

The night wasn’t kind to Ravana. It was probably one of his most painful nights. The death of his most dear son had left a void no amount of wealth could fill. He wasn’t as much affected by the loss of his other sons or his own brother. As much grief was building up the more anger surged. His son had been slain and his brother was responsible for it. In a burst of anger, he rushed toward Ashok vatika to kill Sita who was the root of all problems in his life. Not once did it occur to him that he was equally responsible for his current predicament. Ravana spent the night oscillating between anger and grief.158

Way before dawn, the rakshasa army rushed out of their fortress to attack. Initially, they managed to slaughter thousands of monkeys who were caught unawares, but once Rama stepped in, the game changed drastically. He used a spectacular gandharva weapon that caused showers of arrows from all directions. The rakshasa army began falling like a pack of cards. Then something surprising happened. By the power of that weapon an illusory effect occurred. Everyone began to look like Rama. All the rakshasas and all vanaras looked exactly like Rama. The rakshasas then began to kill each other assuming they were eliminating Rama. Everywhere they looked, they could only see Rama. Everyone was so absorbed in Rama that all they could see was Rama. Within an hour, two hundred thousand foot soldiers, eighteen thousand soldiers on elephants, fourteen thousand soldiers on horsebacks and a large number of chariot fighters were slain. Those that survived the illusion were so confused and disorientated that they darted off not wanting to be part of this mad war anymore.

The vanaras were in awe of Rama. They had never seen anything like that. They never even knew such weapons existed. They couldn’t comprehend that such illusions were even possible. In fact, all of them had seen it. Not only did all the rakshasas look like Rama, all the vanaras looked like Rama, too. For a few moments, all of them had become Rama-ized. In that moment, they all realized that Rama was no mortal being. That realization increased their love for Him and their dedication to serve Him till their last breath.159

Suddenly conches started to blow. From within Lanka emerged an army that was colossal. This was probably the biggest army of Lanka. The vanaras couldn’t fathom that even at the far end of the war, so many rakshasas could exist. Ravana was a master strategist. He had never exposed this army during the whole war. Thus, they were fresh and energetic. This was his personal army. He reserved them for the worst-case scenarios. The other armies of Lanka were good enough for most wars, but this division was brought in only during extreme emergencies. The advantage was the element of surprise, as no one would ever expect the best men to be kept for the last leg of a war.

Hundred thousand chariots, three hundred thousand elephants, sixty million horses each carrying warriors entered into the battlefield with countless foot soldiers. Right in the middle of the battle formation spread over a huge tract, stood the majestic golden chariot of Ravana. It was drawn by eight horses and was equipped with innumerable weapons, each of which was sourced from various gods. The presence of Ravana with such a huge army evoked fear in the hearts of the vanaras. They were all exhausted by now. Fighting with an army that was fresh and energetic was going to be an uphill task. Ravana himself was quite fresh as he hadn’t really strained himself this far. As the vanaras were staring at the rakshasa king in awe of his majestic presence, a huge vulture landed with a thud on the umbrella of his chariot. Ravana jolted in shock. He tried to shoo away the scavenger with his bow, but no matter what he did, the vulture continued staring at him hungrily. It was an ill omen heralding a disaster.160

Realizing all eyes were on him, Ravana turned around and focused on the impeding war. His chivalry was the need of the hour. From the moment Ravana turned around, thousands of vanaras kept falling down every minute. Not wasting too much time with the regular vanaras, Ravana turned his attention to Rama directly. Invoking the most destructive Rahu missile, Ravana attacked Rama. Struck on the forehead with great force, Rama remained unaffected. Dislodging the arrow, He retaliated with a Rudra weapon. As an effect of that missile, countless arrows whizzed toward Ravana. When they collided against his steel-framed body, they all collapsed on the floor rendered useless. Not a scratch on his body.

Ravana then pulled out the asura weapon that was one of his most powerful assets. When that arrow zoomed out of his bow, it brought forth innumerable arrows with various types of heads. Some had heads of lions, tigers, vultures, serpents, and crocodiles. With their mouths wide open, these arrows rushed toward the vanara army in general and Lord Rama in specific. Rama quickly countered the attack with an agniastra. Technically, the fire weapon could burn anything. Although most of Ravana’s arrows were rendered ineffective, there were many shafts that managed to cross the fire ordeal and pierce through the vanara force, killing so many. Then Ravana invoked the rudra missile once again, which Rama foiled using the gandharva weapon. They fired missile after missile at each other, but none seemed to gain any advantage. The missiles they were releasing were unique and destructive. When invoked and released, each produced a different sound. The fight between the two supremos became so interesting that either army stopped fighting and watched them battle.

Seeing no end to this duel, Lakshmana intervened by shooting a sequence of seven arrows that knocked down Ravana’s flag, severed the head of his driver and shattered his bow. Taking advantage of the momentary pause in the exchange of arrows, Vibhishan jumped in and with a single swing of his mace killed all the horses yoked to his brother’s chariot. Ravana was furious with his brother for his audacity. Picking up a lance, he threw it with great force aiming at the chest of Vibhishan. Even as the lance soared through the sky with great speed, three of Lakshmana’s arrows checked its progress by cutting it off neatly into three pieces. Now directing his entire fury at Lakshmana who dared break his spear, Ravana picked up another very heavy lance and hurled it at Lakshmana.

Even before either Rama or anyone else could retaliate, the spear pierced through Lakshmana’s body seriously wounding the son of Sumitra. Seeing Lakshmana collapse on the battleground, Rama got wildly angry at the oppressor. He fired so many arrows at Ravana that the king of Lanka could just not counter. He was filled with fear seeing Rama’s rage. He fled back to the safety of his fortress. Ravana’s exit gave the vanara leaders time to reband and decide how to deal with Lakshmana’s fall. While the battle continued unabated, Lakshmana was carried away to a distance. There, Sushena inspected the wounds and his vital parameters. He assured Rama that there was nothing to worry and that they were now better equipped to deal with such crises. He gave Hanuman a list of herbs to procure from near the Mahodaya mountain. Hanuman rushed while the rest waited. In a very short duration, Hanuman covered considerable ground and managed to procure the required herbs.

Pestle and Mortar

Here is an interesting folktale that gives another angle to this story.

The physician, Sushena said, “Now all I need is a divine pestle and mortar that in the present context is only available with Ravana. I heard that he keeps them in his inner chambers.”

Hanuman immediately made his way into Ravana’s apartments. However, Ravana had foreseen Sushena’s need for a pestle and hidden it next to his bedside table, determined not to let it out of his sight. When Hanuman noticed that Mandodari was sleeping soundly next to the king, he thought of a plan to distract Ravana. He slipped under his bed in a tiny form and tied Ravana’s hair to the bedpost. He then grabbed the pestle and mortar and ran toward the door. Ravana woke up in an instant and tried to run after Hanuman, but was yanked back to his bed by his hair. He tried to untie the knot but failed because Hanuman had cast a spell. The knot would not be undone until Mandodari had kicked Ravana on the head with her foot!

Hanuman chuckled as he watched the mighty king shaking his wife awake. He bowed his crowned head before her and begged her to kick him. Hanuman had a hearty laugh and then sped back to Sushena and gave him the pestle and mortar.

As Lakshmana got up, they could see Ravana go down the slopes of the Trikuta mountains on a brand-new chariot.

Chariot of Dharma

There is a very interesting discussion at this juncture between Vibhishan and Rama. Seeing Ravana return with a new chariot, Vibhishan asked Rama a practical question. “How can you fight Ravana who has access to a chariot, powerful weapons, armor and has a chariot driver? You are bereft of all of this. You don’t even have proper footwear since Bharata kept them.

Rama smiled and replied. “My dear Vibhishan, I do have a chariot, I do have the best of weapons, I do have the armor and I do have a chariot driver too.”

Vibhishan was surprised. Rama continued, “One does not necessarily need a physical chariot. To fight against durjanas or vices, one has to be stationed on the dharma ratha. I am standing firmly on the chariot of dharma, adorning the armor of good qualities. I am holding the weapon of knowledge in my hand and determined to destroy the evil qualities. The dharma is driven by the most capable driver named sadhana or disciplined devotion. With such a powerful combination, no anartha can last too long in a combat.”

Vibhishan was impressed with Rama’s perspective on the fight.

Out of thin air, a dazzling golden chariot studded with the most shimmering precious diamonds appeared. Drawn by a thousand horses of greenish hue, it was fully loaded with weapons and protective armor. Driven by an expert charioteer, the chariot was indeed splendid. The soft-spoken charioteer introduced himself as Matali and requested Rama to accept the chariot as the gift from Indra to aid him in the upcoming fight against Ravana. All the gods were watching with rapt attention to witness the outcome of the battle. Indra had personally sent his own chariot along with the most expert driver to help Rama in this most crucial war that concerned the whole universe’s wellbeing.

Indra’s Reasons For Sending His Chariot to Assist Rama toward the End of the War

Several interesting reasons exist regarding Indra’s reason to send his chariot to Rama, that too toward the end of the war when there was no real need.

During the battle, Indra had a strong premonition that only Ravana would win. This was because Indra had always lost against Ravana. Hence it was difficult for him to imagine someone actually defeating Ravana. If doubt had not haunted him, he would have sent his chariot to Rama at the beginning itself. He was doubtful that if he sent the carriage to Rama and Ravana won, then he would be in a bad state. Hence, he delayed sending the carriage to Lord Rama. However, when he saw Lord Rama winning, he sent his carriage, thinking that not sending it now would be foolishness. Now Lord Rama’s name would go down in history, and Indra’s name would not be taken, hence sending the carriage was only right.

God never asks us for anything. He is apta kaam–never in any need. But when we give willingly, He kindly accepts. By accepting the chariot of Indra, Rama is telling the world that the moment positive ideas arise in our heart, good behavior and patience rise along, that is, the day we offer everything we possess to God it is blessed.

Indra’s is the carriage of reward of good deeds (punya), and the carriage of the dharma (the cosmic law), because it is received as a result of good deeds and following the dharma. Indra had fought many battles with Ravana seated on the same chariot, but had lost every time.

From this, it was indicative that based on the rewards of the good deeds and following dharma, one could receive great rewards, benefits, and perhaps also heaven. But were these enough to defeat Ravana? Then one may think that great deeds and dharma are useless. That is not the case. It is indicated here that when the great deeds that is, punya and dharma and the chariot are utilized in defeating Ravana, they lose. However, when the same are offered to the god and Lord Rama sits on the carriage instead of Indra, Ravana is easily killed. What this means is that, when we utilize our good deeds, rewards of good deeds, and dharma in serving god, god will accept it and make us blessed.

With gratitude and respect, Rama went around the chariot three times and then boarded it. By this time, Ravana had arrived. Mounted on a fresh chariot, he reentered the war arena with great enthusiasm, which turned into anger the moment he saw that Lakshmana had been revived. Seeing Rama on a celestial chariot, Ravana fumed. The puny gods who were so long trembling in fear seemed to have become bold enough to assist Rama in His fight against Lanka.

This time, the fight between Rama and Ravana was even more intense and spectacular. When the two fought, no one else fought. Rama was more equipped than He had ever been until now. He fought with a ferocity Ravana had never seen in the three worlds that he had conquered. Very soon, both sides experienced great pain from wounds and bruises. The number of weapons hurled was so many and of such variety that it showed expertise on both sides. Every weapon that Ravana hurled, Rama was able to counter it with another weapon. The selection of the weapon was so quick and the usage so precise and fast that most times no one even saw when the weapon was selected, mounted and fired.161

As He saw Ravana, he became more angry. Here was the person who had kept Sita hostage for ten months now. How could he still be alive? Rama became furious at this thought and began to attack Ravana with a fury much worse than before. Ravana was wounded with so many arrows, and so much blood gushed out that he collapsed on the chariot. When his master collapsed on the vehicle, Ravana’s chariot driver had to take a quick call. Keeping the safety of his master forefront, the charioteer whisked the chariot away to safety. When he was sure that they were far enough and Rama’s arrows could not torment his master further, the chariot driver stopped and began to gently tend to his master’s wounds. He had made sure that they were close enough to a river to access water to wash away the wounds and then apply healing herbs.

After a considerable amount of time when Ravana opened his eyes, he found himself lying on a grassy patch with the sun shining above him. He found his driver close by bringing water for him to drink. The moment he realized what had taken place, he burst out in anger. Furiously smashing the water container, Ravana chastised the driver with expletives. He was so upset for having been brought out of the battlefield in that state. It was the highest insult to a warrior to be taken out of the battlefield in this manner. Ravana heaped insult upon insult on the driver whose face had become swollen with shame. He kept saying that his name and reputation was tarnished because of the driver’s foolishness. He felt that by now people must be calling him impotent and cowardly. Although very upset at being insulted, the driver was patient enough to explain the reality of the situation after he fell unconscious. When Ravana heard the graphic details of the way the monkeys were trying to jump onto the chariot to drag away his comatose body, Ravana realized that the driver had acted wisely. But refusing to accept it, his ego hurt more than his body.162

a mystical event

While Ravana was away, a very mystical event took place. Agastya rishi appeared out of thin air. Immediately, everyone bowed to the venerable sage. Lord Rama was extremely pleased with his appearance at such a crucial juncture. He fondly recalled the amazing stories narrated to him by sage Suteekshna about his master Agastya rishi. He also recounted the special meeting they had when Agastya had offered him such valuable gifts of weapons that were proving useful in this war. He was also the one who recommended them to stay at Pachavati. Sensing Rama’s needs, Agastya chose to appear to give Rama another gift. Closing his eyes, he invoked the most powerful mantra known as the Aditya Hridayam—an incantation to please the sun god. While granting the mantra to Rama, Agastya explained the very powerful mantra, it immediately rid the reciter of all pains and negativity. The very next moment, Agastya disappeared. Lord Rama closed his eyes and intensely chanted the Aditya Hridayam mantra while facing the sun. As soon as He finished the chant, He experienced great transcendental bliss and energy.163

Rama was now ready to face Ravana. Mounting the chariot once again, Rama directed Matali. “O Matali, drive immediately to where my greatest enemy Ravana is. Even while approaching him, exercise great caution.”

Suddenly Rama stopped speaking. He immediately changed his tone and apologized to Matali for having spoken in a commanding tone. He said, “I am sorry for speaking to you in that instructive tone, as if I was your master. Please forgive Me for that misstep. I spoke out of the eagerness to battle Ravana.”

Matali blushed with embarrassment. For the first time in his life, he had come across someone so humble; He was asking for his forgiveness. He had always come across haughty masters. This small exchange brought tears to his eyes. He was now ready to even die for such a great person and was determined to serve him in the best of his capacity.

Motivating and Demotivating

Rama genuinely loved people, and Ravana expertly used people. Love is founded on respect and use on exploitation. Love cares for feeling, while use cares only for output.

Rama dealt with Matali with great care and humility. Ravana dealt with his driver with ingratitude and harshness. Rama chose his words carefully, making sure he did not hurt his driver. Ravana heaped insults on his driver without bothering to ascertain facts and consider his feelings.

While Matali was highly inspired to serve Rama even more enthusiastically, Ravana’s driver became disappointed and felt demotivated to give his best.

Words impact the human heart the way rain impacts crops. When mild, rains nourish the crop. But torrential rains can destroy the same crop.

By this time, Ravana was back. His wounds had healed, but his ego was still hurt. He was determined to finish off his opponent this time once and for all. The fight had reached feverish levels within seconds of beginning. At one point in the fight, Rama cut off Ravana’s head. An uproar arose from both the sides on seeing the king of Lanka headless. It seemed for a moment that the war was over. But the very next moment, everyone gasped as they saw a new head emerge in place the one just cut off. The weird part was that the severed head was still on the battleground when a new head replaced it. Immediately, Rama shot another arrow and chopped off the new one, too, and the second head rolled off and joined the one on the ground. Instantly another one popped up.

This miracle astounded everyone. Rama began to shoot arrows at a feverish pace and these arrows kept cutting off Ravana’s limbs. But no matter what, each limb was replaced by a fresh one.

What’s happening? How in the world could this be possible? The vanara army was flabbergasted. For the last hour or so, Lord Rama had been severing Ravana’s every limb and every head, but these kept reappearing. He even tried throwing the detached limb at a great distance and in opposite directions and even into the ocean. No matter what He did, a new limb reappeared instantly.

Ravana seemed to enjoy the perplexing his enemies.

“It’s impossible to kill him like this. Nothing conventional seems to work on him,” Ravana’s brother Vibhishan advised Lord Rama, who kept failing repeatedly in His attempts to destroy Ravana. Putting His assault to a brief halt, Lord Rama turned toward Vibhishan and enquired with concern. “What else can I do? Do you have a better idea?”

“If I may, I would like to share a secret about Ravana’s powers that only we three siblings know.” The eagerness of Lord Rama and the vanara leaders that had gathered around him right in the middle of the war, prodded Vibhishan to continue. They had made sure that Lakshmana heavily engaged Ravana in another direction, while this conversation went on as discreetly as possible. “Lord Brahma had offered Ravana a boon-bane when he performed severe austerities. What you see is Lord Brahma’s boon in action! Even if he is cut into pieces, and the pieces are scattered in a million different directions, his body will reassemble instantaneously. Every limb of his body will regrow immediately. No one can undo this boon. But we can focus on the bane part of the boon.”

Everyone was all ears, ready to comprehend the magnitude of the impossible task that lay ahead. “At that time, Lord Brahma fashioned an arrow called the brahmabana. He directed that unless that arrow was shot into the belly of Ravana, his death was impossible. No other weapon would be fruitful. And even this weapon, if aimed at any place other than his belly, would prove futile. With this kind of coded protection, Ravana knew that he was practically immortal. His only task was to make sure that he hid the arrow where even the wind couldn’t enter. And that he has done astutely.”

Brother’s Betrayal

Vibhishan gave up his loyalty to his brother and revealed the secret of his death. He abandoned bhratru dharma or duty toward a brother. For many this seems to be a sin. Just because one has a disease for many years doesn’t mean he should get attached to it and become loyal to it. Disease must be eliminated at any cost. Loyalty to a disease and to the wrong person even if it’s your own brother is detrimental to your own well-being and society.

When Vibhishan concluded, only one question remained. Who will do it?

Realizing Lord Rama’s predicament, Hanuman quipped, “My Lord, please allow me to serve you in this venture. I will locate the arrow and deposit it in your custody soon.”

“Here is a clue. Ravana’s wife Mandodari is the only person who knows where that arrow is. Needless to say, her quarters are very secure.” Vibhishan smiled and Hanuman got his hint from that sly smile.

“Jai Shri Rama!” With a loud cry, Hanuman took off on his adventure landing straight into a desolate section of Lanka. In a second, he transformed into an old astrologer. His mace became the astrological treatise he carried under his arms. He wobbled around the city undetected. He held a walking stick in his right hand and wore a clean but simple white garment. The red tilak on his forehead shone brilliantly. His cheeks drooped, revealing his frail age. The wrinkles around his eyes were signs of wisdom and experience.164

“Jai Shrimaan Ravana!” With every step, he recited this sacred mantra. Seeing his stature, the already tense people of Lanka began to come to him to get their charts read. He would sit on street corners and read several horoscopes. Every finding of his was on the dot. His words became a salve for their anxious burning hearts. Slowly, he began edging closer to the main palace.

Soon, the queen heard about this wonder astrologer who was an ardent devotee of her husband. She was eager to have an audience with him. Rejecting the gorgeously ornate lion throne that was always offered to elevated guests, the astrologer chose to sit on the floor on a kusa grass mat. This one action convinced Mandodari that this was the perfect person to give her an honest and clear prediction about Lanka’s future. This war was already becoming unbearable. She had almost lost everyone dear to her. Yet, there was hope until her husband was alive. But what would be the future of Lankeshwar?

“The future is either black or white. Either Ravana will rule forever or he will die today.” Intensely absorbed in his astrological books, he spoke with concern in his eyes. “How can we make sure that victory is ours? Tell me. Tell me. Is there anything we can do? Tell me…tell me…” Mandodari panicked hearing the clarity with which he spoke those words.165

“The secret that you hold in this house will save you as long as it’s a secret.” The mysterious astrologer spoke looking at her, his eyes lifting his gaze from the astrological books.

“Secret! What secret are you talking about?” She acted totally surprised. She was actually confused. How could anyone know such deep secrets? That too, a complete stranger whom she had just met. She hoped against hope that he was talking about something else.

“Don’t fool me. I know everything: the past, the present and the future. I just want to warn you because I am concerned about the welfare of our able king. Don’t rely on anyone. Even if Lord Brahma comes himself, don’t reveal the treasure to him.” Saying this, the old man got up and began to stagger toward the gate. He walked a few steps and turned slowly to face her again.

There were worry lines visible on her face, “Are you sure it’s safe enough? Vibhishan is aware of every nook and cranny of this palace. What if he actually knows the whereabouts of this treasure which has become your life now?” Mandodari seemed confident yet concerned at the same time. She said, “No, no. He can never know unless I tell him. But since this is a question of my husband’s life, no precaution is enough. I totally trust you to help me find the best place to house it secretly. Right now it’s inside this glass pillar. Where should I…”

CRAAAAAACK!

“Jai Shri Rama!” Even before Mandodari could complete her sentence, Hanuman kicked at the glass pillar and quickly grabbed the arrow and dashed out of the palace.

Everything happened so quickly that none of the soldiers had a chance to react, let alone stop Hanuman.

WHOOOOSH!

With one arrow, Ravana was history! The arrow was shot so expertly by Rama that it hit right at the center of the navel of Ravana where it dried up the nectar that was stored there. That nectar ensured the immortality of Ravana. Once that nectar dried up, Ravana was nothing but an ordinary mortal like anyone else. When the nectar dried in his navel, Ravana’s lifeless body toppled over from the chariot and fell on the battleground dead.

Rolling Heads

Some insights into the head cutting phenomenon :

There are so many different versions and interesting insights into the head cutting story of the Rama-Ravana war. Here are some that can help you delve deeper into that story.

One understanding is that the ten heads of Ravana possessed ten kinds of intelligences. This was a boon offered by Brahma. According to this boon, the heads excelled at politics, Vedic texts, administration, all types of enjoyments, etc. When Ravana was planning to abduct Sita, he consulted all his intelligences and his ten heads for their suggestion. Nine of the ten heads rejected the idea and voted against it. Only the last head that was his original head refused to listen. His own head was too stubborn to listen to any advice.

Rama kept cutting Ravana’s heads, but they kept growing back. What is the significance of the head cutting episode? The head is the center of buddhi or intelligence. But it is also the seat of doubt and ignorance. To eradicate the vices in individuals and society, ignorance has to be cut off. Thus Rama was cutting the head of Ravana. But every time he cut the head, it grew back. It simply means that we may make some attempt to destroy our ignorance by attending satsang or hearing divine knowledge, but the moment we step out of the talk, we slip into ignorance again. Just as the head is the center of thoughts, the arms are the center of action. Bad thoughts need to be cut. So does bad action. Therefore, Rama cuts Ravana’s heads and arms too. Society’s need for change in thinking is achieved by cutting the head and its need for change in behavior is achieved by cutting the arms. Rama tires of cutting so many heads and arms of Ravana. This shows that winning the war against evil is not easy. It is a continuous war and not a momentary victory. Just doing a little good won’t solve all problems forever. They will keep popping up one after another, and one has to be vigilant and keep the fight on.

By getting innumerable heads and arms, Ravana thought that he could become immortal, but his calculations went awry. Not just that, it was quite an insulting experience, too. Because when he died, so many of his heads and arms were strewn all over the place. Ideally, when a person dies, his body is cremated, but in the case of Ravana, vultures and foxes gnawed at his heads. It was extremely devastating for Mandodari to watch this.

Most people associate greatness with the ability to perform great miracles. By this standard, Ravana performed so many great miracles, whereas Rama had to struggle hard to achieve anything. Ravana could fly across the ocean, while Rama had to construct a bridge. Rama had to fast to please the ocean god, while Ravana forced all the gods to submit. Ravana never seemed to be injured and was so tough to kill, whereas Rama suffered several injuries during the battle. Miracles are not an indicator of the greatness of the soul. Saintly people use their powers for the welfare of the world, but arrogant men use their powers to display greatness. Saintly people hide their greatness within the folds of their humility.

Indrajit represents lust, Kumbhakarna represents ego and Ravana represents illusion or moha. Even after the destruction of lust and ego, illusion survives. To destroy illusion, one needs the grace of the guru. Therefore, Agastya rishi comes to offer his blessings to Rama. Just as Rama was cutting off Ravana’s head and each time a new head would appear, similarly most people feel it is impossible to deal with illusions as each time we cut through one illusion, another appears. People try a couple of times and then they give up the efforts.

Ravana’s heads also represent the numerous attachments we have in life. Just when you feel all your attachments are destroyed, another attachment sprouts out. It is not enough to simply attack at your attachments. Alongside, one has to destroy the source of nectar, which is at the navel or one has to attack sanskar or chitta or consciousness that is at the very root of the habit development.

## Chapter 9

Elimination and Illumination

Ravana’s passing heralded both joy and sorrow—joy to those who hated him and sorrow to those who loved him. The gods were thrilled that their greatest enemy and the biggest threat to the functioning of the universe had been vanquished. His elimination gave hope. Not just Ravana, but even the negativity that he fostered was now removed. The universe could breathe easy.166

The news of Ravana’s demise spread through the city of Lanka. Hundreds of citizens poured in to see their king’s last remains— more out of disbelief than concern. Ravana’s queens, too, stormed in and slumped around their husband’s body, wailing and beating their chests, each trying to outdo the other in agony. The pandemonium of hundreds of these hysterical women was intense and uncontrolled, with each enacting pain deeper than the other. Of all Ravana’s wives, Mandodari’s emotions were the most genuine. Although extremely saddened by her husband’s death, she was also aware of his debauchery. As she stood next to his dead body, for closure, she decided to say everything she hadn’t dared when he was alive. His ego had never allowed her to speak nor could it ever accept advice from her. She said, “O, you arrogant man. You had invited your own end the moment you had deceitfully abducted Sita and displayed your cowardice.” Although her husband’s killer was standing right next to her, she directed all her anger at her husband and all her respect to Rama.

Witnessing the plight of Lanka’s queens as well as that of the citizens, Rama advised Vibhishan to conduct the final rites for his deceased brother immediately. Vibhishan refused. “I deny any connection with a person who had abducted other men’s wives and embraced adharmic values. Ravana may have been my elder brother and deserved respect, but his abominable actions have erased any respect I may have had for him.167

Rama explained to the crestfallen brother calmly. “Vibhishan, with death, all hostilities die. It does not matter what a person had done when alive, the dead body always deserves respect. After all, he was a heroic king of the rakshasa clan. Shed all hatred and cremate your brother’s mortal remains with the highest level of respect and attention, Vibhishan. Rama’s simple yet profound words touched Vibhishan. If anyone deserved to truly hate Ravana, it was Rama. But here he was talking respect instead of spewing hatred. This mood of Rama drove Vibhishan into action. He took full responsibility for the cremation. He went into Lanka and brought with him Malyavan, their grandfather and the eldest of the family, to preside over the ceremony. He conducted the last rites of the deceased king of Lanka in the most respectful and detailed manner. With that done, Lanka settled down a bit.168

Rama set aside His bow, assumed a very sober mood and got down to setting Lanka on a new course. First, he looked toward his chariot driver and said, “Matali, thank you for your valuable contribution during the most crucial time of the war and I request you to return to your duties with Indra.” He then spoke to Lakshmana. “Lakshmana, do the first things first. Arrange for Vibhishan’s coronation as the king of Lanka. It is essential for him to assume full charge and bring back stability to Lanka.” As Lakshmana started to busy himself with the coronation ceremony, Rama summoned Hanuman. “Hanuman, deliver this good news to Mother Sita who is in Ashok vatika.” When Hanuman reached Ashok vatika, Sita was thrilled to see him. They were meeting after a month. Hanuman related every detail of the war and ended his narration with news of Ravana’s death, Rama’s victory and Vibhishan’s coronation. Sita was overjoyed. Eleven torturous months had finally ended. She was so happy with Hanuman that She wanted to reward him. But She had nothing in Her possession. So She gave him Her heartfelt blessings. Nothing in the three worlds could match the news Hanuman had just shared with Her. Sita blessed Hanuman. “This day on which you have given Me this news will become the most special day of the week. You will be glorified and worshipped on this day forever all over the universe. Hanuman, all virtues will reside in your heart, and Lord Rama would always shower His mercy on you.”

Ruler of Hearts

Hanuman became renowned across the three worlds because of Mother Sita’s blessings and the grace of Lord Rama. By using his talent, strength and skills and by exhibiting a conscientious attitude, Hanuman established a kingdom and resided and ruled in the hearts of Sita and Rama forever. Seated on this high throne as a king of their hearts, he rules the hearts of devotees of the Lord. His luster and brilliance are recognized and praised by all who come into contact with him. By the blessings of Mother Sita, the whole world worships the brilliance and power of Hanuman.

Hanuman now turned his attention toward all the rakshasis around Ashok vatika. They had tortured Sita so much during her stay there. Seeing Hanuman sent shivers down their spine. The last time he was here, he had managed to burn down the whole garden. It had taken weeks of rebuilding to bring things to near normal. But nothing ever goes back to normal once broken. When they noticed him stare angrily at them, they trembled. Hanuman asked Sita, “O Mother, if You grant me permission I could torture-kill all these rakshasis who have been harassing You for so many months. In fact, I want to know How you want them tortured.” They had tortured Her, so it was only fair for Her to choose their torment.

Instead of answering him, Sita told him a story. “A man was traveling through a dense forest. Suddenly, he found a tiger darting toward him. After a brief chase, he climbed up a tree to save himself. Just as he was catching his breath with a thought that he had just managed to escape sure death, he was shocked to discover a new reality. Facing him on that very tree was a fierce bear staring at him. The bear, however, offered him protection. Rather than being nasty, it was welcoming and hospitable. The pleasantly surprised man settled in and went to sleep on one of the branches of the tree. After a while, the man woke up and the tiger below began to converse with him in a rather friendly manner. He explained that he was extremely hungry and needed to eat something. The tiger assured him that he didn’t want to harm him but rather just wanted to fill his belly. Finally, he suggested that the man push down the bear. The tiger would thus make a meal out of the bear and walk away. The man could then return home safely. That made sense to the man and he quietly pushed down the bear. Deftly catching hold of a branch, the bear managed to save himself from the tiger. Now the man was really scared. He had unwittingly turned his host hostile now. The tiger now coaxed the bear to shove the ungrateful man down. The man knew his time was up. But what the bear said surprised him. The bear claimed that just because the man had become ungrateful did not mean he should give up his magnanimity. The bear refused to push down his guest. The man felt ashamed of his small-mindedness and expressed gratitude to the bear for his high-mindedness.”

Sita said to Hanuman, “Human beings should learn from that bear. If a bear could show such nobleness, its only right that human beings learn a little more kindness. Yes, the rakshasis in Ashok vatika did trouble Me a lot, but they were simply obeying a bad master. Had they had a good master, they would have probably been kinder. They shouldn’t be punished for the badness of their master. With Ravana’s death, all hostilities end and all anger has abated. Great people prefer to immediately forget the pain given to them and remember forever only the kindness extended to them. Repaying evil with evil is also evil. Forgiveness is the greatest virtue of a human being. I have decided to forgive the troublemakers and let them go.” The rakshasis heard this and threw themselves at the feet of Sita—the one they had treated horribly was now treating them with kindness that was unheard of in Lanka. From this small story, Hanuman got a glimpse of Sita’s wisdom and greatness even in the horizon of such a painful experience.169

Hanuman had completed his immediate task having delivered the message of Ravana’s death to Sita. He sought Her permission to return and asked Her if She had any message for Rama. She said, “All I want is to be reunited with My husband.” Understanding the urgency, Hanuman returned to his master and shared his discussion with Sita. Hanuman said to Rama, “O Lord, my master! I urge You not to delay even for a moment and proceed to Ashok vatika immediately for the much-anticipated reunion.” Instead of being excited and enthusiastic, Rama was serious and cautious with His words. By this time King Vibhishan and Lakshmana joined them. Rama assigned a task to Vibhishan. “Go to Ashok vatika and arrange for Sita to take a bath, be dressed up in royal attire and the best of ornaments. Then arrange to bring Her to Me.”

Rama’s tone and lack of love in His voice surprised the observant Vibhishan. Lakshmana noted it, too. However, Vibhishan proceeded to Ashok vatika and first offered his sincerest respects to Sita. “I beg Your forgiveness for all the atrocities my brother Ravana had heaped upon You. Please also forgive the city of Lanka for its deafening silence through the eleven months of Your struggle.” He then gifted Her the most beautiful silken garments and intricate jewelry. “O Sita! After You bathe, deck up in these beautiful clothes and ornaments.” Vibhishan then stepped aside allowing the women accompanying him to help Sita. All this upset Sita. “I am not interested in all this. All I want is to see My beloved husband right away. Why are you people delaying Me with this pageantry?” she said.**170**

Vibhishan said, “It’s Your husband Rama’s desire.”

She complied immediately. In a short while, Sita ascended an ornate palanquin and taken to the outskirts of Lanka where Rama was seated. He was still following the rules of exile and wasn’t prepared to set foot into the city. When the palanquin arrived, immense excitement spread among the vanaras. All of them had risked everything in their lives for the sake of this one woman. They hadn’t even seen Her yet. Now they wanted to see the woman for whom such a massive war had been waged. Who was it that they had fought so intensely to rescue? Who was Rama so madly in love with that He was ready to change the direction of the whole world for?171

As the excited vanaras jostled for a first glimpse of Sita as She stepped off the palanquin, Vibhishan and his four trusted men began to push them back to create space for Sita to walk in comfort. Rama noticed that they were trying to force the vanaras to follow protocol. He tapped Vibhishan on his shoulders and said, “Do not curb the enthusiasm of the vanaras who have toiled so selflessly to rescue Her. It is not at all appropriate to show insensitivity now when the task has been accomplished and Sita was safe. There was nothing wrong in their eagerness to see a chaste woman for whom they had waged a war and sacrificed so much. Let them see their object of worship and meditation of so many months.” As the vanaras looked on, Vibhishan escorted Mother Sita to Rama.172

Sita approached Rama in the most loving and yet extremely shy manner. He had been the object of Her meditation constantly for the last eleven months. Now She would be reunited with Him once again. Her shy eyes were riveted to the floor as She walked toward Him. Finally, Her eyes caught the two lotus feet of Lord Rama upon which She had fixed Her glance for hours every day as they walked through the forest. Slowly, She lifted Her gaze and absorbed every inch of Him from His toe all the way up until She reached His beautiful moonlike face. Seeing His handsome face was like diving into a pot of nectar. She was so thrilled that She broke into a beautiful smile. Perhaps for the first time in so many months She smiled.

But the stern look on Rama’s face disturbed Her. In fact, He was not even looking at Her directly. Why would He do that? The vanaras were surprised, too. Lakshmana was most shocked. He had seen Rama cry in separation from Mother Sita more than once. He had seen the greatest lamentations of Rama during the four rainy months they were stuck in Kishkinda. When He had so much love for His wife and missed Her so intensely, why would He not even want to see Her when She was finally before Him? The confusion was gnawing at everyone, and it only grew with the silence of Rama.173

Rama eventually broke His silence with the harshest words that anyone had heard him speak. “I have done what I needed to do to avenge the insult to the Ikshvaku dynasty. Now I leave it to You to decide the course of action. You are free to choose any path You may want to take. After being away for eleven months in a strange land, You may have undergone many experiences that may have forced You to reconsider Your future.”

Sita, who was thus far in a very happy and a peaceful mood, lost Her temper when She heard Her husband’s words. Flames of rage spewed from Her mouth. “How dare You speak to Me like that? Never for a moment did I think of nor want to be on any other path but being with You. Do not think that I am an ordinary woman. I am the daughter of King Janak, and My birth is not ordinary. My character is pure, therefore, You cannot judge Me like that. All my paths—mind, body and soul—lead to You. If this was what You wanted to do in the end, then why did You make a show of waging a big war and coming to save Me? Why did You trouble so many millions of innocent vanaras to lay down their lives for Your sake just to save Me? If You had planned to reject Me, then why didn’t You just send that as a message through Hanuman instead of love letters?”174

Still quite enraged, Sita turned to Lakshmana and ordered him to light a pyre for Her to enter. Lakshmana looked at his brother. Rama looked away. No one could even dare to approach Him and discuss it. Lakshmana had no option but to arrange a large pyre. Sita folded Her hands in respect and circumambulated Rama thrice. She prayed to Agni, the fire god, asked him to accept Her only if She was pure in words mind and body, Sita silently walked into the raging fire. The hot flames consumed Her and She did not wince for a moment. Everyone standing was shocked. After a moment, the huge crowd became restless and started to wail and cry on seeing Sita being consumed by the fire.175

Just at that moment, the serpented-headed Lord Shiva and Lord Brahma, the most important of the gods, appeared there. The fire ordeal had caused great pain to even these most exalted of personalities. How could Rama be so cruel and hardhearted to expect His wife to enter a raging fire right in front of His eyes? While everyone was staring at Rama through their tears, Rama was looking at Lord Brahma with folded hands. Lord Brahma understood his role. Rama wanted him to take on the task of explaining the reality to all the confused beings.176

Lord Brahma began by introducing who Rama really was. “Rama and Sita are the eternal energies that govern the three worlds. One cannot exist without the other. O’ Rama, you are Lord Narayana, the Supreme Vishnu tattva who has incarnated into this world to

annihilate the miscreants. Sita is none other than the goddess of fortune, Mahalakshmi. Having accomplished the task for which You have appeared into this world with the elimination of Ravana along with the whole demoniac force, it is time for You to wind up your pastimes and return to the spiritual world.”177

That very instant, a huge ball of flame burst out from the pyre. Everyone was amazed with the dazzle of that fireball suspended in thin air. The ball descended to the ground and it opened up to reveal two beautiful personalities within it. One was mother Sita, who had just been consumed by the fire and the other was Agni, who had escorted Her back safely from his abode. Sita appeared more effulgent and beautiful than when She had gone in. Agni announced that his fire was always the test of impurity in this world. Fire burned anything impure, and his fire could never touch anything pure. Agni said that Sita was purity personified and that by Her touch, fire began feeling purer than before. She purified fire with Her touch. He vouched that not even for a moment did She deviate from the path of Lord Rama in Her body, mind and words. The fire god then requested Rama to give up His harsh speech and cold demeanor and accept Sita gracefully.

By now, Lord Rama was in tears. The love for His beloved Sita that He had suppressed for years finally gushed out. Yet His voice returned to its normal loving sweetness. He said, “I know that Sita can never even for a moment forget Me just as I cannot think of a single moment when I do not remember Her. We are as inseparable as sun and sunshine. Without Her I do not exist and without Me She does not exist. However, this fire ordeal was essential to forever shut the mouths of those vile people who may try to malign Her in the future. I know well that Sita is bhumi suta. The material elements can never harm Her as all material elements originate from Her. I cannot live for a moment without Her presence now.”

Not His Sita

There is a lesser-known, subtler and deeper reason for the fire sacrifice This is mentioned in the Kurma Purana.

In Book 3: Adopt Patience of the Ramayana—The Game of Life series—I had mentioned an incident that was covered in Book 6. It was a secret that I had left incomplete and a suspense that would get unveiled later. This is the time to unveil that intriguing incident.

When Rama was about to chase the golden deer that had attracted Sita’s fascination so much, He did something that He never usually did. Just before He took off on the chase, He asked Sita to step into Their small cottage for a minute. Lakshmana had wondered then what it could mean. Rama never did something like this. He never sought any private time like that with Sita right amid an adventure. Even Sita was taken aback at Rama’s untimely request. Nonetheless, She walked into the cottage closely followed by Rama.

What happened inside that cottage was only known to the two of them. Even Lakshmana had no access to the happenings inside the cottage then. But the great seers like Vyas and Valmiki showered blessings from the divine to see and experience things that no one else could. The Kurma Purana mentions that Rama took Sita inside and invoked the presence of the fire god who appeared through the sacrificial fire that was lit inside the house.

Rama handed Sita over to Agni. Agni respectfully accepted the custody of Sita and promised to take responsibility of Her safety and comforts. As soon as Sita walked into the abode of the fire god, Agni invoked the presence of Maya Sita or a false Sita that was an exact duplicate of the original Sita. She resembled Sita. She was actually Vedavati in her previous life. As Vedavati, she had very intensely desired to have Lord Vishnu as her husband. While performing her austerities to please Lord Vishnu, Ravana had spotted her and had attempted to touch her. She had then self-immolated with a promise that she would be back and be the cause of his destruction.

That same Vedavati was transformed into Maya Sita by Agni and sent to Rama. Vedavati got her desire fulfilled of being called the wife of the Lord for a period of eleven months and at the same time being the cause of destruction of Ravana. It was Maya Sita or Vedavati that Ravana had abducted, and it was her that he had held captive. When Ravana was killed and Vibhishan brought Maya Sita to Rama, He neither looked at her nor expressed His love to her simply because she was not His wife in reality. Rama would never look at any other woman, other than His Sita, even if she appeared like Sita. If you remember the time in the forest when Sati disguised herself as Sita and appeared before Rama. Rama immediately paid his respects to her and recognized her. Similarly, Rama declared to Maya Sita that she could go anywhere she chose to go, now that she was free from the clutches of Ravana. Of course, Vedavati was grateful that at least she could be called Rama’s wife for eleven long months even though she wasn’t. Rama did not tell her to perform the fire sacrifice. Vedavati herself chose to perform it, knowing very well that she had to return to Agni so that the real Sita could be reunited with Rama. With a grateful heart, she circumambulated Rama thrice with a prayer and hope of having Him as her husband in some future life and reentered the abode of Agni from where she had originally come. Once Vedavati entered fire, Agni brought back the original Sita and reunited Her with Rama.

This narration is not in Valmiki Ramayana as it obstructs the flow and the narration of a version that totally focuses on the human side of Rama and avoids the divine side so that the struggle of Rama and Sita is emphasized and learned from.

Rama opened His arms and welcomed Sita into His loving embrace. The union of Sita and Rama brought great joy not just to everyone present there, but also to the whole universe. As He held Sita in His arms, He could feel Her anger toward Him for the harshness He had showed Her, but He knew He had many years of togetherness to help Her dissolve that anger. At the beginning of their journey to exile, Sita, too, had caused great pain to Rama, which had agitated Him. Then She, too, knew that She would dissolve that anger through many years of togetherness. Rama knew that their togetherness had much greater strength than temporary moments of anger.178

To complete that most joyous episode, Lord Shiva pointed out toward the sky where seated on a celestial aerial plane was King Dasaratha, the deceased father of Lord Rama. He had appeared there in his ethereal form to bless his son for having accomplished such an important welfare task. There was yet another reason why Dasaratha wanted to meet his son at this juncture. This day also marked the last day of the fourteen-year exile period. Dasaratha felt that he had let his son down and had been the culprit behind these fourteen years of intense suffering that his sons and daughter-in-law had to go through. When they had left Ayodhya, he wasn’t in an emotional state to even ask for forgiveness for what he had done to them. At that time, his selfishness had made him focus on his own pain. But now he wanted to finally be able to ask forgiveness from his children. He explained to his obedient son that his residence in the heavens gave him no pleasure as the trauma he had caused his children and the citizens of Ayodhya continued to weigh heavily on his conscience. Now that the unfortunate period of exile had ended finally, he would find peace only when Rama returned to Ayodhya and assumed the throne of Ayodhya as its unrivaled monarch.

Energy Mismatch

Some events in life lead to energy being stuck in a relationship. If two people who have gone through some tough phase in their mutual bond and have not spent time together sorting out their differences, energy gets stuck there.

In good relationships, energy flow is free and constant. Two people exchange energies freely and comfortably. But when the relationship goes through a complex phase and they are unable to sort out the problem, then the relationship gets stuck at a particular point and there is a coldness in the connect. This sometimes continues for a lifetime. That energy being stuck between them affects so many areas of life, including health, mind, work, finances, and other relationships

Until these two people come together and sort out their differences, the energy continues to remain stuck. In some cases, people carry such stuck energies over lifetimes.

When Rama left Ayodhya, Dasaratha was in no position to communicate with his son. Rama wasn’t also able to communicate with his father as Keikeyi stood in between them. Thus, their energy remained stuck for all these years. Even after Dasaratha ascended to the heavens, he was not peaceful as he hadn’t put an effort in releasing that energy. Finally, when the fourteen years of exile was over, Lord Shiva helped Dasaratha meet Rama in his ethereal body and release that stuck energy and make their relationship healthier and allow energy to freely flow between them.

Rama stood there with folded hands as His father became emotional. Rama agreed to abide by the instructions of His father to become the king of Ayodhya on one condition: Dasaratha would forgive mother Keikeyi and withdraw all the harsh words he had heaped on her. Dasaratha was in tears. How could anyone be so good? Here was a son who had suffered unlimited pain for fourteen years and yet He was thinking of the feelings of the one who had caused that pain. Dasaratha offered his respects to his extraordinary son. He was born from nectar and was nectar personified in all his dealings. Before departing back to his heavenly residence, the father offered his gratitude to his other son Lakshmana for making this supreme sacrifice of serving his elder brother so sincerely through times of such need. And then he turned to his daughter-in-law and begged Her to forgive Her son’s harshness and cold behavior some time back and focus on His goodness that She always saw and will see going forward. Offering his gratitude to all the vanaras for assisting His son, Dasaratha ascended back to the heavens.

All the gods who had assembled there to congratulate Rama and express their gratitude to Him for clearing up the earth’s burden, began to depart one by one after offering their sincere respects. Finally, Indra chose to leave. Before leaving, he wanted to offer Lord Rama some gift. When asked to choose, Rama simply asked him to revive all the dead vanaras. Instantly, Indra arranged for that and all the long dead ones arose as if from a deep slumber—their wounds healed miraculously and their severed limbs rejoined perfectly. The sun had descended by then, bringing closure to a long and arduous day filled with intense events. Rama decided to spend the last night of His fourteen years of exile in Lanka—of course not in the city in the outskirts. This was probably the first night both He and Sita would be sleeping peacefully in the last eleven months. Togetherness was peace.

trip back home

Vibhishan begged Rama not to leave Lanka. He begged Him to take over the throne of Lanka and rule the world from there. Vibhishan simply wanted to serve Rama for the rest of his life. Rama smiled and embraced Vibhishan. “I still have a very big mission left to accomplish. In fact, that is more critical than even the killing of Ravana.” When he had visited Rama in Chitrakoot, Bharata had given his brother an ultimatum, just like Sita had given Hanuman. He had declared that if there was even a day’s delay in Rama’s return from exile to Ayodhya, Bharata would end his life. So technically at any cost He would have to return to Ayodhya before sunset to save the life of His dear Bharata. Rama had somehow managed to save Sita just on the nick of time on the last day of the thirtieth day of the one-month deadline She had given Hanuman. The killing of Ravana happened on the very last day of that deadline. That day also marked the end of the last day of the fourteen-year exile period. Thus, the next day marked the last day of Bharata’s waiting period. He had said if Rama did not come by the next day, he would end his life. Nothing else was more important to Rama than to reach on time to save His brother.

Thirty Days

Sita had set a thirty-day deadline. Here is a timeline of the events since:

3 days—Hanuman burned Lanka and returned to Kishkinda with the vanaras.

12 days—The entire vanara army moved from Kishkinda to the southern ocean shore.

3 days—Rama offered prayers to please the ocean god

5 days—The vanara sena built the bridge across the ocean.

7 days—End of the war; Ravana is vanquished.

This marked the end of Sita’s thirty-day deadline. It also marked the end of the fourteen-year exile period.

the break-up of the exile period

1 year—In Chitrakoot

9 years—In Dandakaranya forest

3 years—In Panchavati

11 months—Sita in Lanka and Rama in search of Her.

12th month—The war ends, and They return to Ayodhya; coincides with the end of the fourteen-year period.

Understanding the urgency of the matter, Vibhishan offered Rama the services of the pushpak vimana, the mystical plane that could cover large distances in a short duration. Rama ascended the unmanned flying machine that was operated by the mind. He then helped Sita climb in, and then Lakshmana followed. Folding his hands, Rama offered His deepest gratitude to the vanaras assembled together to see off Their hero. Seeing the parting mood of Rama, Sugriva lunged forward and grabbed His feet, begging Him to stay. He begged Rama to be permitted to travel to Ayodhya to witness the coronation ceremony. Vibhishan followed suit. Millions of vanaras folded their hands, begging permission for the same. Rama was most happy to accept this request. It would be such a fulfilling experience to return to his homeland with so many friends and well-wishers that He had gained during His sojourn. As soon as Rama permitted, Sugriva, Vibhishan and Hanuman immediately ascended the vehicle. Understanding the need of space, the pushpak vimana expanded itself into the size of an aerial city. All the vanaras clambered in and the vehicle kept expanding to accommodate more and more of them until every one of them was comfortably seated.179

Once everyone was in, the vehicle automatically took off noiselessly and effortlessly despite the enormous weight loaded onto it. As the vehicle was passing over the terrain of Lanka, Lord Rama excitedly showed Mother Sita all the important places of the war. He showed Her the place where Indrajit was killed by Lakshmana, then the place where Ravana was killed and then the place where Kumbhakarna’s massive body was cremated. As they turned toward the ocean, He showed Her the Nala setu. Sita became emotional when She saw that massive eight hundred-mile-long and eightymile wide stone bridge the vanaras constructed just to save Her. Her love for Her husband increased a million times. She folded Her hands and expressed Her gratitude to the vanaras, who were too embarrassed by Her humility. The Nala setu would remain the monument of love, forever demonstrating to the world what a lover was ready to do for his beloved.180

When they reached the other side of the ocean, the pushpak vimana descended on the very familiar shore where they had all spent so many memorable days while waiting for ocean god to reciprocate as well as to manage the construction of the bridge. Everyone wondered why the sudden descent. That the pushpak vimana descended meant that it was the desire of the One who controlled it at present, and that was Rama. Rama then expressed His desire to worship Lord Shiva before proceeding further toward Ayodhya. Rama was merely following the kshatriya rule of worshipping Lord Shiva, the god of destruction, before and after the war. Before the war Rama did it mentally, but after the war, He wanted to do it elaborately. With this in mind, he requested Hanuman to bring him a Shiva linga to worship.181

Covering the distance of thousands of yojanas in a few minutes, Hanuman was hovering over Mount Kailash. If there was any place in the world, he could find a Shiva linga, it would be here. But despite searching for it in every corner of the mountain range, Hanuman was disappointed. He was aware of Lord Rama’s hurry to return to Ayodhya on time. Bharata had warned of dire consequences, if Lord Rama delayed his return. The countdown had begun, and time was ticking away. Bharata’s life was in danger. But Lord Rama wasn’t going to return until He had performed a thanksgiving ritual, offering His gratitude to Lord Shiva, the Lord of Destruction.

Hanuman could find no alternative than to sit down and invoke the presence of his divine father, Lord Shiva, with his intense meditative calling. A couple of hours went by and lo and behold, Lord Shiva made his appearance and offered Hanuman a Shiva linga with his own hands. Hanuman flew back to the southern shore with the linga. What he saw upon reaching made him sad. How could Lord Rama do that? Someone else has already brought a Shiva linga and Lord Rama, without considering the trouble I have taken, has even finished performing the ritual on that linga. All his efforts had gone waste. What was the point of acquiring the original linga from Lord Shiva himself when Lord Rama was ready to accept a regular one?

He felt humiliated and sad. Lord Rama explained to him that the time for his return was drawing near and the auspicious ritual had to be performed within the stipulated time. Therefore, he was forced to take this tough call. Hanuman was so disappointed for failing to serve Rama efficiently that he wanted to end his life.

Sensing what Hanuman was going through, Rama decided to impart a lesson. He gently coaxed Hanuman with His sweet words. “O Hanuman, please try to understand that whatever happened was a rare circumstance and is definitely not going to undermine your abilities and service to Me. Sita made this Shiva linga from sand with all Her love and devotion, and you have brought this original Shiva linga all the way from Mount Kailash after pleasing Lord Shiva. For me, both these lingas are sacred. However, right now, you look very disturbed. So, I have decided to worship your linga as well. Please place it so we can begin the ritual.”

Hanuman, however, hesitated when placing it down. Lord Rama could understand that Hanuman wasn’t comfortable with the idea of his linga being the second one. He wanted his linga to be the only one. Lord Rama told him, “All right, Hanuman, you remove the Linga made by Sita and replace it with yours.”182

There was an instant smile on Hanuman’s face. He wrapped his tail around the Shiva linga, made by Sita using sand, and tightened his grip. Considering that it was made of sand and could crumble, he did not exert too much force. He wanted to lift it in complete shape and place it very far away. When he pulled, it didn’t budge at all. He put all his might now and, still, the linga wouldn’t even move an inch. Now, Hanuman put his entire force using his hands. Yet, nothing happened. The linga stayed!

Hanuman was frustrated; he decided to use every ounce of his energy to root out the linga. He pulled really hard this time. With the might of Hanuman, the earth below shook, but the linga held fast. With the next heave, Hanuman lost his grip and fell off on the other side, face down. Seeing their hero collapse this way, all the vanaras gathered around the now unconscious Hanuman. Lord Rama entered the circle, sat next to Hanuman and lovingly placed his head on His lap. He gently ran His hands all over the exhausted body of Hanuman. Lord Rama’s touch rejuvenated the disoriented hero.

He opened his eyes to the joy of all the monkeys. When he saw that he was on the lap of Lord Rama, he had tears in his eyes. Lord Rama gently stroked his head and said, “O’ Hanuman, do you remember when we met first near the Pampa Lake, you had introduced Me to Sugriva and had assured Me that you would give Us your constant support? From that day onwards, I had forgotten My father, My mother and even My brothers. You have done more than any relative would have done. I am eternally grateful for all the sacrifices you have made to serve Me.”

Lord Rama’s words made the grateful Hanuman emotional. Lord Rama, then installed the Shiva linga brought by Hanuman close to Sita’s Shiva linga and performed the ritual again. Then He made a declaration that left everyone spellbound. “From this day on, anyone who visits this place will first worship and honor the Shiva linga of Hanuman and only then worship Sita’s Shiva linga.’183

managing delays

The plane next descended at Kishkinda. All the way from the shore of the ocean till they reached Kishkinda, Hanuman showed Sita all the places where they searched for Her and described all the obstacles that came in their path. As the plane descended on the outskirts of the Kishkinda city, thousands of vanara ladies came rushing to greet their loved ones who were on the plane. They hadn’t seen each other in months now. From the time the search mission had begun, there was absolutely no communication between those who remained in the city and those who had ventured out. This was a grand and a much-needed reunion. While everyone was immersed in socializing, Rama was becoming fidgety. Time was running out, and His Bharata had to be saved.184

Suddenly, Sita expressed a desire. She wanted to invite all the vanara women too for the coronation ceremony in Ayodhya. Rama was aghast. They couldn’t afford any more delays. But Sita was adamant. “We couldn’t take without the vanara women, could we? If the men had made sacrifices in the war, the women made many more sacrifices by tolerating the separation of their beloveds and handling the affairs of the household in their absence.” The vanara women were thrilled by Sita’s kindness. They hurried up the plane and huddled around Sita.185

The rest of the journey was a beautiful reminiscence of the thirteen beautiful years They had spent in the Dandakaranya forest. As the plane passed through the exact same route that Sita, Rama and Lakshmana had taken, leading all the way to Chitrakoot. There the plane began its descent again and landed on the banks of the Ganga at the ashram of Bharadwaja muni. The vanara army was excited as they had heard from Rama and Lakshmana about his mystic abilities to generate anything from thin air. They had especially heard of the massive feast he had arranged for Bharata’s entourage that had halted there en route Chitrakoot.

It was already late evening by the time they landed at the sacred confluence of the four holy rivers, Ganga, Yamuna, Saraswati and the river of saintly people that lived there along with Bharadwaja muni. Rama took Hanuman aside and spoke in confidence. Since He would take some time in the ashram, it was important that someone visit Bharat immediately and inform him of their arrival at the ashram, lest he take some drastic step due to not being able to find any signs of Rama’s return even at the end of the fourteen years of exile. Rama wanted him to first make a quick visit to Guha’s village and inform Guha first and the rush to Bharat.

Just as Hanuman was about to leave, Rama touched his shoulders and held him down. He had something more important in His mind—something that could alter the course of His life. There was probably no one better than Hanuman to help Him deal with this sensitive situation maturely. Fourteen years was a long time, and anything could change in those decades—opinions, mindsets, desires, priorities or decisions. Fourteen years ago, Bharata had been insistent that Rama return and take charge of Ayodhya, but He wanted to know if fourteen years hence his desire was still the same. Would attachments not have affected his heart? After all, he was the undisputed king for fourteen long years. Rama wanted Hanuman to observe Bharata’s eyes closely while conveying the news of His arrival. Eyes never lie. If Hanuman were to see in them even a tinge of hesitation or pain on hearing the news, then Rama would decide never to return to Ayodhya. Only if Hanuman saw unconditional love and joy would Rama return. Hanuman was clear about what he needed to do: make a psychological assessment of Bharata.186

embracing life

As he stood in front of the burning pyre, he recalled everything that had transpired in the last fourteen years. Something in his heart told him that his brother might never come back. He was an ascetic by heart. Anyway, whatever it was, he had a vow to execute. So, what if Lord Rama was attached to His vows and lifestyle, even he had the right to be attached to his own vow. He had very clearly told Lord Rama during their last meeting at Chitrakoot that he wouldn’t wait a day more once fourteen years of exile were completed. If Lord Rama delayed even by a day, he would end his life. Today was that day. A few minutes were left for the day to end, and there was no sign of Lord Rama’s return. He was ready to accept the unacceptable. As the clock ticked away, Bharata became more anxious.

Shatrughna, his brother, all the ministers of Ayodhya and the prominent citizens had no idea how to prevent the determined Bharata from jumping into the fire. They could only pray that god would send some messenger to delay this ordeal. Above Bharata, on the branch of a tree sat Hanuman, wondering what to do. Lord Rama had sent him to inform Bharata of his return, but before he could do that, Bharata was already preparing to end his life. He recalled how a few weeks prior, he was sitting on a tree like this, looking at Mother Sita contemplating suicide. And today, he saw Bharata do the same thing.

Whenever he had come across dead-ends in his adventures, he had used the same solution and it had always worked. When the vanaras were unable to decipher what to do at the ocean shore, they had narrated the Rama katha and Sampati had come to their rescue. When Mother Sita tried to end Her life, he had again narrated the Rama katha, giving Her hope to continue living. And today, as he watched calamity unfold, he knew what to do.187

He immediately began narrating the Rama katha—from the beginning of Rama lila until the point where Lord Rama had reached Bharadwaja muni’s ashram, while on his way to meet Bharata.
